# Data schema

Authoritative reference for the shipped data shapes: `Nation`, `NationStats`, `NationTraits`, `NationBands`, `Relationship`, `Person`, `CountryReference`, `Organization`/`Cradle`/`Charter`, `City`, `State`, `SectorMixEntry`, and `WorldDataset`. These are zod schemas at `packages/world/src/schemas/`, not hand-maintained parallel type definitions — the TypeScript types are `z.infer<typeof Schema>`, generated from the same source the validator gates data against.

For system architecture see `ARCHITECTURE.md`. For intent see `NORTH_STAR.md`. The standing rules these shapes follow are in `DECISIONS.md` (§4 for the world dataset, §9 for saves).

## 1. Where the source of truth lives

`packages/world/src/schemas/index.ts` re-exports every schema below. Do not hand-write a parallel `interface` or `type` for any of these shapes — import the zod-inferred type instead, so the type and the runtime validator can never drift.

| File | Schemas |
|---|---|
| `schemas/ids.ts` | Branded id types: `NationId`, `PersonId`, `OrgId`, `CharterId`, `RegionId`, `ChokepointId`, `CradleId`, `TreatyId`, `RoleId`, `FactionId`, `PartyId`, `CityId`, `CurrencyCode` |
| `schemas/nation.ts` | `NationStats`, `Agenda`, `NationTraits`, `NationArchetype`, `NationBands`, `Posture`, `RedLine`, `Nation`, `NationInput` |
| `schemas/relationship.ts` | `Grudge`, `RelationshipEvent`, `Relationship` |
| `schemas/person.ts` | `IdeologyVector`, `PersonTrait`, `Person` |
| `schemas/person-network.ts` | `PersonTieKind`, `TieProvenance`, `PROVENANCE_OF_ARCHETYPE`, `PersonConnection` |
| `schemas/org-cradle-charter.ts` | `Organization`, `Cradle`, `RemovalMechanism`, `Institution`, `PartyKind`, `Party`, `Charter` |
| `schemas/traits.ts` | `TraitRange`, `TraitRanges`, `TRAIT_KEYS`, `NationTraitProfile` |
| `schemas/people-pool.ts` | `NameBank`, `PersonAttributeRanges`, `RoleProfile`, `PersonPoolSpec` |
| `schemas/portrait.ts` | `PortraitHeritage`, `PortraitGenderPresentation`, `PortraitAgeBand`, `PortraitSkinTone`, `PortraitAttire`, `PortraitHairStyle`, `PortraitHairColor`, `PortraitFacialHair`, `PortraitEyewear`, `PortraitFacets`, `portraitRef()`, `parsePortraitRef()` |
| `schemas/geography.ts` | `Chokepoint`, `ChokepointDependence`, `NationGeography` |
| `schemas/zone.ts` | `ZoneId`, `LonLat`, `MaritimeZone`, `InstallationAnchor`, `ANCHOR_KINDS`, `MARITIME_ZONE_PREFIX` |
| `schemas/city.ts` | `City`, `CityKind` |
| `schemas/state.ts` | `State`, `StateId`, `StateRegion` |
| `schemas/sector.ts` | `SectorId` (closed 23), `SectorMixEntry`, `ENERGY_MIX_SECTORS` |
| `schemas/dataset.ts` | `WorldDataset` — the whole-dataset shape the validator gates (`nations`, `cities`, `states`, `relationships`, `people`, `organizations`, `cradles`, `charters`) |
| `schemas/country-reference.ts` | `CountryReference` — static dossier prose for a nation (not part of `WorldDataset`; live stats come from the campaign) |

## 2. Nation

**The roster is every government that holds territory and that a US President deals with, not every UN seat (2026-09-22): 196 of them — the 193 UN members, plus Taiwan, Kosovo and Palestine.** Dependencies (Greenland, Puerto Rico, Hong Kong) are not nations here; they are territory of the government that holds them. The 61 records written before the roster grew are `full`; the other 135 were drafted `thin` from the vendored seed (§9.1), and 59 of those have since been researched and promoted to `standard` (2026-09-22), leaving 76 `thin`.

Every nation — the United States and each of the 195 NPCs — is the same shape, distinguished only by `playable: true` on the US:

```ts
Nation = {
 id: NationId // ISO-3 ("USA", "IDN", ...)
 playable: boolean
 fidelity: "full" | "standard" | "thin" // defaults to "full"; see below
 identity: { name, adjective, capital, government: CharterId, languages, currency, currencyPerUsd?, definiteArticle? }
 geo: { region, lat, lon, neighbors: NationId[], coastline, chokepoints, chokepointDependence?, disasterExposure }
 stats: NationStats // §3 below
 sectors: SectorMixEntry[] // closed enum, mix sums to 1; §5j
 traits: NationTraits // §4 below
 doctrine: { posture, redLines, alignmentPull: NationId[] }
 memberships: OrgId[] // derived at assembly — see §5
 bands: NationBands // derived at assembly — see §5
}
```

Authoring uses `NationInput`, which is `Nation` without `bands` and `memberships`. Both are filled in by dataset assembly, so neither is ever hand-typed.

`identity.government` must reference a valid `CharterId`; `geo.neighbors` and `doctrine.alignmentPull` must reference valid `NationId`s; `memberships` must reference valid `OrgId`s. All four are checked by the dataset validator (§7), not just by the type system.

**`identity.definiteArticle`** (optional boolean) is whether the nation's English short name is said with "the" — set `true` on the six authored records that need it (the United States, the United Kingdom, the United Arab Emirates, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, the Netherlands, the Philippines) and on nine thin ones (the Bahamas, the Central African Republic, the Comoros, the Dominican Republic, the Gambia, the Maldives, the Marshall Islands, the Republic of the Congo, the Solomon Islands). Absent everywhere else, read as `false`. It exists because a rendered sentence that pastes `identity.name` straight in reads wrong for those four ("as long as United States wanted it to hold" shipped in a week-one memo), and a scenario cast across the whole roster has no way to know which ones need it. `displayName(nation, { article: true })` (`packages/world/src/dataset/naming.ts`) is the package's own formatter; the placeholder resolver (`packages/content/src/nation-article.ts`) and the engine's narration helper (`packages/engine/src/naming.ts`) each read the field the same way and fall back to their own name-shape heuristic when it is absent, so the field is additive rather than load-bearing. This field is **not** covered by the `stat-consumers.ts` registry (§3) — that registry's pinned scope is `NationStats`, `Nation.geo`, `NationTraits`, `NationBands`, `Person`, and `City`; `identity` sits outside it, and its consumers are named here instead.

## 3. NationStats — the numbers that must mechanically matter

Grouped as: demography, economy, trade, energy, military, security, politics, softPower. Money fields (`gdp`, `reserves`, `military.budget`, trade flows) are stored in **USD millions**, the canonical unit regardless of display currency (§7.5, `NORTH_STAR.md` §7 checklist item "currency is a display concern only"). `economy.gdpPerCapita` is stored in raw USD (not millions) — the validator checks it stays consistent with `gdp × 1,000,000 / population` within a 15% tolerance, which is how a unit-entry mistake (e.g. entering it in thousands) gets caught even though each field individually passes its own range check. `military.budget` is held to the same kind of check against `gdp`: a defense burden above 35% of national output is a unit error rather than a policy, since the heaviest real burdens — a garrison state, a country at war — run 20-25% and both exist in this dataset. Six nations shipped raw-dollar-shaped budgets against a GDP in millions before the check existed, the worst reading as 13,500% of output.

`trade.topPartners` and `trade.tradeDependence` are **derived, not authored**. Nation records leave both empty; `applyTrade()` (`packages/world/src/dataset/trade.ts`) fills them at assembly time, the same way `applyBands()` fills `bands`, so hand-editing either field on a nation record has no effect — assembly overwrites it. `topPartners` is the ranked nation ids holding at least a 0.03% share of the nation's modeled trade attraction, capped by the nation's fidelity (40 `full`, 25 `standard`, 12 `thin`) and uncapped for the playable nation (schema bound 200, the roster). Whatever its cap, an NPC keeps its lane with the playable nation when that lane clears the share floor: its moves against the President are the game, and an economic move needs the lane (at 196 governments Iran's sanctions-dampened lane to Washington was otherwise crowded out, and with it the channel Iran answers a strike on its nuclear sites through). `tradeDependence` maps partner → 0..1 over a subset of those partners, dropping entries that round below 0.0001 (4 decimal places) rather than recording noise, and its values sum to at most the nation's trade exposure — a country never depends on its partners more than it trades at all.

**These two bounds were widened for a reason worth naming**, because the old ones (5 partners, 3% share floor, 2-decimal rounding) quietly gated a whole gameplay surface off. The economic instrument ladder's rungs 2–5 (`tradeAccess` in `packages/engine/src/instruments.ts`) require `actor.stats.trade.tradeDependence[target] > 0` before they can be played at all — and at the old bounds, the United States carried a live trade edge with exactly four nations (Canada, Mexico, China, Germany) out of 49, because a $27T economy's trade is spread thin across dozens of real partners at a small share each, and "small share" was being read as "noise." Widening `MAX_PARTNERS` to 40 and dropping the floor to 0.03% share / 0.0001 dependence keeps the real second tier of partners a great power actually has — Russia, India, Turkey, Brazil, Saudi Arabia, and about thirty others now carry a genuine, small, live edge with the United States, so the economic ladder is reachable against the rival that fiction and history both make plausible, not just the two the old floor happened to let through.

**Iran, North Korea and Cuba are the deliberate exception.** `trade.ts` applies a `sanctionsDampener` (×0.04) to the gravity pull between the United States and exactly these three, because a standing US trade embargo does not show up in the model's inputs (GDP and distance) — left alone, gravity would rank Iran and Cuba alongside similarly-sized, unsanctioned economies and hand economic coercion a lever against a country there is nothing left to embargo. The dampener is scoped to the US side of those three pairs only: Iran's trade with China or Russia is computed at full strength. Venezuela is deliberately **not** in that set — US sanctions there target the state oil company rather than the trade lane itself, and the model's small-but-real Venezuela edge (oil) is the accurate read.

**`military.arsenal.declared?: boolean`.** Whether the government admits to its arsenal at all; absent reads as declared. An undeclared arsenal is credited with no count on any surface, however well it is estimated abroad: every reader that prints a count reads it through `engine/forces.ts#declaredWarheads`, which returns undefined for `declared: false` and the authored count otherwise. No record sets it at the contract. Its consumer is registered in `stat-consumers.ts` and its copy in `metric-copy.ts`.

### 3z. The vitals: what the country's rates are, and which of them are stored

**`demography` carries three rates and their net.** `birthRate`, `deathRate` and `netMigration` are stored as **fractions per person per year** (0.012 is twelve births per 1,000), for consistency with `economy.growth` and every other rate in this schema; a player surface renders a vital rate **per 1,000**, which is the reading a US audience expects and the form `verify-us-units.mjs` covers. Bounds are `birthRate` 0.004–0.045, `deathRate` 0.003–0.025, `netMigration` −0.05–0.05. The birth bound is wider than the rate law's own baseline clamp because the roster holds three countries the clamp cannot: the DR Congo, Afghanistan and Nigeria all carry a crude birth rate above 30 per 1,000, and a bound that refused the real figure would only push the difference into net migration with the wrong sign. The migration bound is wide and symmetric because three rows (Yemen, Ukraine, Syria) are countries a war is emptying.

`demography.growthRate` is now **derived at assembly**, by `applyVitalRates` (`dataset/vital-rates.ts`), as `birthRate − deathRate + netMigration` rounded to four places. It sits in the pipeline for the reason `applyStateMass` does: the three rates are the facts, the net is their sum, and authoring the sum a second time by hand only makes a copy that can disagree with its own parts. The pass is provably inert on today's rows — every authored figure already satisfies the identity — which is the cheapest moment to install it. Validator check `vital-rates-reproduce-growth` proves it ran and catches a hand-built dataset (a studio overlay, a fixture) that moved one of the four without the others.

Net migration is the **residual** of the demographers' own identity, not an independently sourced column: natural increase plus net migration is total growth, births and deaths are the researched figures, and the growth rate was already authored. Recording it that way is why the derivation changes no number today.

**`economy.costOfLiving` is an index, not a rate.** It is `1.000` on every nation at the dataset snapshot **by definition**, because the snapshot is what the index is measured against; validator check `cost-of-living-opening` pins it at 1.000 ± 0.001. The schema bounds it at 0.1–100,000 (`schemas/nation.ts`), wide enough for two terms at the highest inflation the engine allows, so a save from a country living through a hyperinflation still parses; the authored figure is still pinned at 1.000. Inflation is the rate that moves it once a campaign is running. It is the deflator the fiscal layer needs (receipts and outlays are nominal, `economy.gdp` is real) and the figure a household actually measures. Its unit is `index`, not `share`: there is no denominator anyone could count behind it.

**`economy.debt` replaces `economy.debtToGdp`: a stored stock, not a stored ratio.** The debt is a nominal quantity in USD millions, and the ratio is derived — `debtToGdp(stats) = debt / max(1, gdp × costOfLiving)`, in `packages/world/src/dataset/fiscal-identities.ts`. A stored ratio is two facts collapsed into one number that can disagree with both of them, which is the defect `bands`, `memberships` and `State.population` are all derived to avoid. Storing the stock also makes the inflation-erodes-debt effect fall out of the identity instead of needing a term for it: the stock is nominal, output is real, so a rising price level lowers the ratio on its own arithmetic.

Migration was mechanical — `debt = debtToGdp × gdp` at `costOfLiving = 1.0`, for every nation on the roster (50 at the time of the migration, 61 now). The retired ratios are recorded in `RETIRED_DEBT_TO_GDP` (same file) so the conversion can be **checked rather than asserted**: validator check `debt-reproduces-ratio` recomputes the ratio from the stock and fails if it does not land back within 0.001 of the figure the retired field held. Nothing but that check reads the table. The United States was converted twice: its retired 1.2 was gross debt, and its stock was then re-based to debt held by the public, CBO's 101% of output for FY2026 (`RETIRED_DEBT_TO_GDP.USA` 1.01, the stock 27,773,835), because that is what the Treasury borrows against in the market; what the government owes its own trust funds is counted against the debt limit instead (`debt-limit.ts`'s `intragovernmental`).

**`security.crimeIndex` is re-annotated as a macro-owned rate.** It was an authored constant a one-off nudge could move; it is now the level a national rate law reverts toward, alongside growth, inflation and unemployment. The authored value is its opening reading, not a permanent fact about the country.

**`security.intelCapability`** is what this country's own foreign collection can reach. Read by `state.hasIntelOnNation`, `state.hasIntelOnRelationship`, `npc-turn.feedVisibility` and the covert contest's collection layer. The player's own value is what both visibility gates spend, through `outreads()`, less a margin: **0.20** for a third-party tie (`hasIntelOnRelationship`) and **0.42** for a government's own counsel (`hasIntelOnNation`), because a tie leaks at both ends and a cabinet only to sources inside it. Shared organization membership alone grants no access to private government information. Issued reports can be shared with selected recipients without unlocking every fact about the target.

**`security.counterIntel`** is what stands between a foreign officer and this government. Read by `bands.stabilityScore` (the `writ` term), both visibility gates, `npc-turn.feedVisibility` and the covert contest. At the shipped dataset the United States founds at `intelCapability: 0.9`, so a government is unread when its `counterIntel` reaches 0.70 and its NPC actions also drop out of the feed at 0.90. On the 196-government roster, 21 of 195 foreign governments (10.8%) are unread at founding and 174 are read, and four are dark to the feed (CHN 0.92, PRK 0.95, RUS 0.90, ISR 0.90). The gate is `scripts/verify-foreign-truth.mjs`: it ratchets the read count so it can fall but not rise (`scripts/baselines/foreign-truth.json`) and prints how far the roster sits from the one-third target rather than failing on it; most of the governments added with the roster sit under the US collection margin, so the paid read sells almost nothing, and that is an open dataset question. A raise here moves `stabilityBand`, which is the content targeting surface — check the band before changing a value.

### 3a. `military.orderOfBattle` — decomposition, not replacement

`military` keeps every aggregate it had (`activeHeadcount`, `branchStrength`, `readiness`, the lift capacities) and gains an optional authored decomposition beneath them. `MILITARY_BRANCHES` is fixed at six — `army`, `navy`, `air`, `marines`, `space`, `cyber` — because a schema that can nest anything can be validated as nothing. Each branch carries a required `headcount`, plus optional `capabilities` (`{ name: ForceAssetId, count, label? }` — what the service *is*: carrier strike groups, airborne divisions, fighter squadrons) and `units` (`{ type: ForceAssetId, count, readiness, label? }` — what it can *send*: the finite bench a deployment draws from, with `readiness` the 0..1 share that could move this month).

**Every holding is a catalog id, not prose.** `packages/world/src/schemas/force-asset.ts` is the closed vocabulary of things a service can count — `army.brigade-combat-team`, `navy.aircraft-carrier`, `air.fighter`, `space.missile-warning-constellation` — and each `ForceAssetSpec` declares the service that owns it, the contest `domain` it fights in (`ground` | `sea` | `air` | `space` | `cyber`), its `echelon` (`platform` | `unit` | `formation`), what one of it `counts` as (`hulls`, `airframes`, `formations`, `teams`, `satellites`, `batteries`), a rough `nominalPersonnel` to field one, and a description. Until this landed a holding was a free string and every rule that read one matched the spelling: a force package wanting eight brigades wanted the characters `Brigade combat teams` to appear on the record, a commitment drew its bench down by string equality, and a dataset that spelled one formation two ways carried two benches nobody could add. Now `ForcePackageSpec.unitType`, `CommitmentSource.unitType` and the instrument ladder's fallbacks are all `ForceAssetId`. A `ForcePackage` (branch, unitType, count) carries an optional `preferred`: the size the staff proposed against the order's target, which billing and resolution scale the count against; it is persisted on `Campaign.issuedActs[*].request.force` and absent on earlier saves, where the package's fixed size stands in, so no schema version changes. Meanwhile the engine resolves the roster label from the catalog (`forces.ts` holdings carry `id`, the label as `type`, and `personnel = count × nominalPersonnel`). The optional `label` on a record is the service's own name for the thing — Russia's `marines.brigade` reads "Naval infantry brigades" — and is presentation only.

**Depth is tiered, and the schema says so.** `orderOfBattle` is optional on the nation record and `capabilities`/`units` are optional within a branch, so a nation can ship nothing, headcounts only, or the lot. Which nation must ship which is a fact about the *dataset*, not about the shape, so the validator carries it: the playable nation must have an order of battle with named capabilities *and* units in all six services. The United States ships the full decomposition; China and Russia ship named formations on the branches a war against them actually counts; the rest ship none until a sequence reads units.

Three validator checks hold the decomposition to the aggregate above it:

| Check | What it catches |
|---|---|
| `order-of-battle-sum` (`unit-mismatch`) | Branch headcounts summing more than **3%** away from `activeHeadcount` — a service entered on the wrong scale, or one left out |
| force assets (`shape`, `unit-mismatch`; `validate/force-checks.ts`) | A holding filed under a service that does not own it (`navy.frigate` under the army), the same holding listed twice in one list (so counts cannot be added), or `units` whose `count × nominalPersonnel` exceeds the service's own `headcount` — a count on the wrong scale, or a headcount that is. One-directional by design: the establishments are rough, and a service whose named units cover a tenth of its people has simply left its staff and support off the roster |
| branch/strength presence (`plausibility`) | A service with no people that still claims `branchStrength`, or people with a strength of zero. `land` answers for the army *and* the marines |
| playable depth (`shape`) | The played nation shipping headcounts where the engine and every screen need formations |
| fidelity depth (`shape`) | A `full` record with fewer than two agendas of its own, a `standard` record with none, or a `full` or `standard` record with no red line. A `thin` record may author no agenda (it rolls its aims from its archetype's pool) and no red line |

**Fidelity is how much of a country the dataset authors, not what shape it is (2026-09-22).** `fidelity` sits beside `playable` and defaults to `full`, so an authored record says nothing new. It scales only numbers that were a constant or a special case before it existed: `personPoolSpecs` reads it for depth per role (8 for the playable nation, 3 for `full` and `standard`, 1 for `thin`, where a bench of three is people nobody meets carried in every save), and the validator reads it for what the authored layer owes (the table above). It also scales NPC initiative (`initiativeRate` in `packages/engine/src/npc-turn.ts`: 1, 0.6 and 0.25 of a government's temperament) so the three weekly NPC slots stay with capitals the dataset researched, and the trade partner cap (above). The roster's growth to 196 added 135 `thin` records composed from the vendored seed (§9.1), promoted to `standard` as the content room authors them; the 61 authored records are `full`. The first promotion (2026-09-22) took 59: the EU and NATO members not yet written, the Caucasus and Western Balkans, the hemisphere's neighbors, the Gulf and Levant, and the regional anchors of Asia and Africa. Each carries two agendas whose ids land on an agenda theme, a red line whose id is one of the engine's red-line rules, and an alignment pull (toward Washington for the treaty allies, none for the neutrals and hedgers); figures and temperament stay the bake's.

**`branchStrength` is checked against the decomposition, not derived from it.** Branch strength is a quality reading — equipment, training, doctrine — and it is not a function of mass: a Space Force of nine thousand is one of the strongest arms in the world, and an army of a million conscripts can be one of the weakest. Deriving strength from headcount would replace an authored judgment with an arithmetic that is wrong in both directions. What headcount genuinely determines is whether a service *exists*, so that is what is checked, and it fails loudly rather than reconciling silently — the dataset can tell that the two disagree, never which of them is wrong.

The US branch headcounts are the published service strengths **re-based onto the `activeHeadcount` beside them**. Sources that quote per-service strength and sources that quote a department total fold the reserve components and the Coast Guard in differently, so the raw figures sum about six percent short of the total already in the record. Re-basing keeps the real proportions and lets the sum tolerance stay tight enough to catch an actual authoring error.

Every field in this schema is expected to have a named consumer somewhere in `packages/engine` (DECISIONS.md §4.2) — a stat nothing reads doesn't belong here.

The registry that enforces it is `packages/world/src/dataset/stat-consumers.ts` (with its two subject files, `stat-consumers-people.ts` for traits, people, bands and parties and `stat-consumers-places.ts` for geography, cities, states, districts, sectors, seas, installations and seats of government), and it covers **every authored surface a rule can read**, not just `NationStats`: `STAT_CONSUMERS`, `GEO_CONSUMERS` (`Nation.geo`, including the `disasterExposure` kinds), `TRAIT_CONSUMERS` (`NationTraits`), `PERSON_CONSUMERS` (`Person`), `BAND_CONSUMERS` (the derived `NationBands`), `CITY_CONSUMERS`, `STATE_CONSUMERS`, `DISTRICT_CONSUMERS`, `SECTOR_CONSUMERS`, `PARTY_CONSUMERS` (`Party` on a charter), `MARITIME_ZONE_CONSUMERS`, `INSTALLATION_ANCHOR_CONSUMERS`, `FEDERAL_SITE_CONSUMERS` and `STATE_CAPITOL_CONSUMERS`, collected in `CONSUMER_REGISTRIES`. That widening was the structural fix for the dynamism audit's "inert authored field" class: every field it found dead — `geo.disasterExposure`, `traits.transparency`, `Person.integrity` — was authored *outside* the one surface the contract covered, which is exactly why nothing caught them.

**An entry names its readers as sites, not prose (2026-10-13).** `StatConsumer` = `{ path, kind?, writePolicy?, readBy: ReaderSite[], purpose }` or `{ path, kind?, writePolicy?, owedBy }`. A `ReaderSite` is `repo-relative-path#top-level-declaration` — the key `scripts/rule-seam-census.json` already used, so moving code inside a file never invalidates it — and `purpose` says in words what those readers do with the field. The prose claims this replaced had rotted: `demography.medianAge` and `demography.literacy` were credited to two rate laws, `macro.deathBaseline` and `macro.birthBaseline`, that were never written, and the paired test only checked that a claim was longer than three characters. The paired test (`stat-consumers.test.ts`) now walks each registry's own zod schema and fails on a leaf nobody registered, an entry for a leaf that no longer exists, or a reader that does not resolve: every site must name a file that exists and a value (a function, constant, class or enum, never a type) declared at its top level, and a planted fixture proves a missing file, a missing declaration, a type and the old prose form are each refused. The migration resolved every claim against source; where a claim named something that does not exist or does not read the field, the real reader was traced from the field's path or the field was marked owed. Among the claims that changed: the vital rates are read by `vitals.ts#vitalsPhase` and `#populationIdentity`, not by a macro rate law; the player "vitals reading" was `player-core/src/world/state-vitals.ts#stateVitals`; `State.character.vitalAnchor` has no macro reversion target; `trade.topExports` has no player dossier reader; and the three people writers once listed as readers (`rollBirthplace`, `rollBackground`, the party stamp) are now named as writers in the purpose.

**Owed, pinned per registry.** `NationStats` owes exactly `demography.literacy` and `demography.medianAge`. Elsewhere the pin is `NationBands.militaryMetrics.sustainability.industrialMobilization` (the contest's sustainment layer recomputes the same quantity live rather than reading the stored band), `Person.affiliation` (no generated person carries the authored badge; the rules read the runtime one a cabinet challenge writes) and every `District.cohortSkew` leaf (its only reader, the previous player's district dossier, was deleted with that player). A field may only join these lists by an edit to the pin.

**Kind and write policy.** Every numeric `NationStats` leaf declares `kind`, a `QuantityKind` from `packages/world/src/metrics/definitions.ts` — the vocabulary the reference-series comparison already used, extended by `level` (a reading in its own unit that is neither summed nor a share: median age, output per person) and `ordinal` (a step compared by order, never by difference: a credit grade, an instrument rung). Every `NationStats` leaf declares `writePolicy`: `founding-only` (set by the dataset or at founding; nothing in a campaign writes it), `derived-only` (recomputed by its own derivation, written by nothing else), `phase-written` (moved only by the turn phase that owns it) or `effect-written` (moved by effect packets from acts, events and phases alike). The test holds both. The engine's `METRIC_REGISTRY` carries the same two fields; see `ARCHITECTURE.md` §3.

**The report and the gate.** `scripts/report-metric-registry.mjs` builds one TypeScript program over the world, engine, content, player-core and web sources (workspace imports mapped to source, because through the built `d.ts` each zod shape is inlined again into every schema that embeds it) and lists, per registered field, the declarations the type checker sees reading and writing it, keyed `path#declaration`. It writes `scripts/metric-registry-report.json`, whose diff is the audit. A read through a helper is followed one call deep: a caller handing a helper a registered record (`ideologyCloseness(person.ideology)`) is listed as reading the leaves the helper reads off it `(through name)`, and a helper handed a field through a parameter of its own type, or a caller handing a helper the record it reads, confirms a claim naming it (`claimedThroughCall`) without being listed as a reader. A typed patch is followed down to the leaves it writes, across a union (`budget.ts#BUDGET_STAT_PATCH` indexed by a path union). `scripts/verify-metric-registry.mjs` is the gate on it, after `pnpm run build`: it fails on a field stored under a registered root type with no entry, a registered field with no declaration that is not a named blind spot, a field with no reader seen and not owed, a `phase-written`, `effect-written` or `derived-only` field with no writer, any `leaky` patch (typed to admit every key, so the site cannot say what it writes), a claimed reader the walk cannot confirm beyond the ratchet baseline `scripts/baselines/metric-registry.json` (89 at R1, may only shrink), and a committed report the tree no longer writes. Its proof twin plants each. Named blind spots, printed on every run: the cohort shares (`State.character.cohorts`, `District.cohortSkew`), whose schemas are built from a computed key list so no access resolves to one share; claims in files outside the typed packages (the studio console, `scripts/`); and scenario gates in the JSON corpus. A record of amounts keyed by a metric's name (the ledger entry's `cost` and `mood`, a treaty plan's `onSigning`, a silence price's `metrics`) is typed `Partial<Record<keyof MetricSet, number>>`, not `Partial<MetricSet>`: it is a record of what moved, not a patch of the metrics, and the walk reads the difference.

### The unit registry

A second registry answers a different question about the same 78 leaves: not who reads a field, but **what it is measured in**. `packages/world/src/dataset/units.ts` declares a `UnitSpec` for every `NationStats` leaf, and `units.test.ts` walks the schema the same way the consumer registry's test does — a field added without a unit fails the build, and a unit declared for a path the schema dropped fails it too.

The unit was previously a convention recorded in a file-header comment. `trade.exports: 2_100_000` meant USD millions because a sentence above said so; nothing refused a value authored on the wrong scale, and no screen could print a unit it was never told, so a player read `2,100,000` against a label that did not say whether that was dollars, containers or tonnes.

`UnitSpec` carries a `kind` (`currency`, `count`, `share`, `index`, `rate`, `years`, `energy`, `text`), an optional `scale` as a power of ten (authored currency is USD millions, so `scale: 6`), and optional `suffix`/`noun` for the words a reading puts beside the figure. **`share` and `index` are deliberately distinct**: a share has a denominator somebody could count (literacy is the fraction of people who read) and may be shown as a percentage; an index is a 0..1 assessment with no denominator (doctrine quality) and must not be, because dressing an assessment as a proportion claims a precision nobody measured.

**An index prints as a graded word, not as a decimal.** `formatFigure` renders it through `gradeOf` — `Negligible` / `Limited` / `Moderate` / `High` / `Exceptional`, cut at 0.25 / 0.45 / 0.65 / 0.85. The distinction above was right about what an index is not; its original conclusion — print it on the scale it is recorded on — handed the player the storage format. Twenty-seven leaves carry `index`, including the readings a player most wants an opinion about (`politics.legitimacy`, `politics.pressFreedom`, `politics.factionalism`, `military.doctrineQuality`, `military.readiness`, and all six `security.*` fields), and every one of them reached the screen as `0.50`. The ladder is **magnitude, not quality**, deliberately: roughly half the indexed fields are ones where a large number is bad news (crime, corruption, insurgency and protest pressure, factionalism), and a `poor`→`excellent` ladder would invert on every one of them — "Exceptional corruption" and "Exceptional intelligence capability" both say the same true thing about the size of the number, and leave the surface to say whether that is good news. The word is the whole reading, with no figure beside it; where a player needs to separate two governments inside one grade, the surface draws the raw value as a track (`MetricRow`), which is the app's existing shape for exactly that.

`formatFigure()` and `describeFigure()` ship beside the registry as the single place a scale word is chosen, so `2_100_000` renders `$2.1T` on every surface. A screen that formats a stat itself is a screen that can disagree with the next one about what the same number means, which is what this exists to make impossible.

### The metric registry

A third registry joins the two: `packages/world/src/dataset/metrics.ts` is **one record per figure the game keeps**, saying what it is, what it is measured in, what values it may take, where it comes from and who reads it. `MetricSpec` = `{ surface, path, recordPath, group, label, description, valueType, optional, unit?, min?, max?, options?, scale?, provenance, derivedBy?, kind?, writePolicy?, readBy?, purpose?, owedBy?, editable }`, over nine surfaces (`record`, `identity`, `geo`, `stats`, `traits`, `doctrine`, `bands`, `person`, `relationship`).

It is a join, not a fifth place to write things down. `valueType`, `min`, `max` and `options` are read off the zod schema through one shared walker (`dataset/schema-walk.ts`, which `statLeafPaths` now uses too), so a field cannot be listed that the schema lacks; `unit` comes from `units.ts`; `kind`, `writePolicy`, `readBy` (sites), `purpose` and `owedBy` from `stat-consumers.ts`; `provenance` is `authored` (written in `packages/world` source; what the console edits), `derived` (written at assembly — `growthRate`, `topPartners`, `tradeDependence`, `geo.neighbors`, `geo.chokepoints`, `memberships`, every band — each naming its step in `derivedBy`) or `runtime` (people and relationships, which exist only inside a campaign). Only two things are authored, in `metric-copy.ts`: the `description`, and for every reading with no natural unit — every `index`, every 0..1 or −1..1 number without a unit — the words for what its two ends mean (`scale: { low, high }`; magnitude words, never quality words, for the reason `gradeOf` gives). `metrics.test.ts` fails the build on a metric without a description, a description for a path the schema dropped, or a scaled reading without its ends. `metricRejection(metric, value)` is the one judgment of whether a proposed value fits — bounds, integer-ness, enum options, and whether the field is authored at all — and the studio's overlay refuses through it at save time rather than at publish. The player's stat-definition page reads its "what it measures" prose from the same registry.

## 4. NationTraits — personality, rolled per campaign

```ts
NationTraits = {
 aggression, opportunism, legalism, isolationism,
 riskTolerance, vengefulness, reliability, transparency: number // 0..1
 agendas: Agenda[] // 0–3 authored, each { id, description, weight }; a thin record authors none and rolls its archetype's
}
```

Authored as a *range* per nation; the actual per-campaign value is rolled within that range (world.traits-and-jitter) — one of the five seeded divergence sources `NORTH_STAR.md` §1 names.

### 4b. NationTraitProfile — the authored range the roll draws from

```ts
TraitRange = { min, max: number } // 0..1, min <= max
TraitRanges = { <each of the eight NationTraits keys>: TraitRange }

NationTraitProfile = {
 nation: NationId
 ranges: TraitRanges
 candidateAgendas: Agenda[] // >= 3
 agendaCount: 1..3 // how many the campaign roll selects
}
```

`packages/world/src/dataset/traits.ts` derives one profile per nation (`nationTraitProfiles`, `traitProfileByNation`) from that nation's authored trait midpoints, widening the window by `stabilityBand` and `archetype` — a settled state behaves much the same whoever holds it, a collapsing one does not. Institutional traits (`legalism`, `reliability`, `transparency`) get a narrower window than mood-like ones. Candidate agendas are the nation's authored agendas plus an archetype pool, so every nation has at least three to draw from, and a thin record that authors none draws its two from the pool alone.

`packages/world/src/dataset/agenda-themes.ts` classifies each agenda id into one of five themes — `security`, `economic`, `prestige`, `autonomy`, `internal` — via an override table checked before an ordered keyword ruleset. It is not part of the `Agenda` shape: an agenda stays `{ id, description, weight }`, and the theme is derived so authoring an agenda never means authoring a mechanical hint beside it. `agendaTheme(id)` returns `undefined` for an id it has never seen, and a paired test fails if any agenda a nation can roll classifies to `undefined`. The consumer is the NPC utility agent's `agendaFit` term (`packages/engine/src/npc-agent.ts`), which maps theme to instrument affinity.

The roll itself is engine-side (`packages/engine/src/traits.ts`: `rollNationTraits`, `rollWorldTraits`), because `packages/world` holds no logic and the seeded RNG lives in the engine. Each draw is keyed `("traits", nationId, traitKey)`, so adding a nation or a trait never shifts anyone else's roll for an existing seed.

## 5. NationBands — derived, never hand-authored

```ts
NationBands = {
 archetype: NationArchetype // 10 values, e.g. "great-power", "resource-exporter"
 powerTier, wealthTier, militaryTier: 1..5
 alignmentBand: "aligned" | "leaning-aligned" | "neutral" | "leaning-opposed" | "opposed"
 stabilityBand: "stable" | "fragile" | "volatile" | "collapsing"
 militaryMetrics?: { forceIndex, mobilizationCap, sustainability: { warDuration, industrialMobilization }, downtime }
}
```

**Derivation is shipped** (`packages/world/src/dataset/bands.ts`). Bands are computed from `stats` at dataset assembly and are *not* authored on the nation record — `NationInput` (`Nation.omit({ bands, memberships })`) is what the 51 nation files actually construct, and `applyBands(applyMemberships(applyGeography(...)))` in `dataset/index.ts` fills in the derived half. A hand-authored band could disagree with the stats beside it; a derived one cannot.

| Band | Derived from |
|---|---|
| `militaryTier` | `militaryScore` — budget and headcount for mass, readiness and doctrine quality for how much is usable, lift/bases/warheads for reach |
| `wealthTier` | `gdpPerCapita`, discounted by `sovereignRating` and the **derived** debt burden `debtToGdp(stats)` (`dataset/fiscal-identities.ts`), cushioned by `reserves` — per capita, never aggregate. The burden used to be read off a stored `economy.debtToGdp` field; the computed value is unchanged by construction, but *how* a band is derived is a doc-sync surface in its own right because it is what all content targets |
| `powerTier` | `powerScore` — `gdp`, `militaryScore`, soft-power reach, aid leverage, population as a floor |
| `stabilityBand` | `stabilityScore` — whether the state's writ runs, as `holding^0.6 × writ^0.4 × friction`. `holding` is regime stability and legitimacy; `writ` is border control and counter-intel scaled by `1 − insurgency`, less crime; `friction` discounts loud politics (factionalism, protest — an order of magnitude below insurgency), corruption, and unusual inflation/unemployment. Cut at 0.56 / 0.34 / 0.21 into stable / fragile / volatile / collapsing (15 / 17 / 9 / 9 across the shipped roster). Multiplicative because the earlier subtractive form saturated at zero for ten nations at once, and because full-weight factional and protest pressure rated the United States, the United Kingdom and France as `fragile` — which content then targeted |
| `alignmentBand` | Membership of the aligned vs opposed compacts, plus declared `alignmentPull` at half weight |
| `archetype` | Decided by exception — microstate, post-conflict, trading-hub, resource-exporter, isolationist, and client-state claim a nation first; the power tiers catch the remainder |
| `militaryMetrics` | `computeMilitaryMetrics` — force index, mobilization ceiling, sustainability (war duration, industrial mobilization) and downtime, read by `engine/warfighting.ts` |

There is no `relationshipBand`. One used to sit here, documented as "always `neutral` in the dataset — the engine moves it per campaign", and the engine never did: it was assigned the literal `"neutral"` for every nation and read by nothing outside fixtures. How a nation stands toward the player is a fact about a *pair*, and `Nation.bands` is a static per-nation record built before any player exists, so the field had nowhere true to live. Content that wants a friend or an enemy uses the `relationship` condition (`trust`, `alignment`, `tension`, `tradeDependence`, `militaryThreat` — finer than five buckets) or the `closest`/`tensest` casts, both of which read the directed edge where the fact belongs.

`memberships` is likewise derived: organizations author their own member lists, and `applyMemberships` mirrors them onto each nation so the two cannot disagree.

Content targets these bands, never a specific `NationId`, except for deliberate audit-flagged set-pieces. This is the mechanism that makes adding nation 51 a data-only change.

**The set-piece audit** (`packages/content/src/scenario-audit.ts`) enforces that exception. It returns a finding for content that pins a `NationId` without declaring itself a set-piece (`undeclared-set-piece`), for a declaration whose stated reason is too short to be a reason (also `undeclared-set-piece`), and for a set-piece declaration on band-targeted content, where it buys nothing (`pointless-set-piece`).

**The stat-consumer registry** (`packages/world/src/dataset/stat-consumers.ts`) is the other half of the scalability claim: an authored stat nobody reads is research spent on a number that cannot change an outcome. `STAT_CONSUMERS` names, for each `NationStats` leaf, either the declarations that read it (`readBy`, as `path#declaration` sites, with a `purpose`) or the rule that owes it one (`owedBy`), plus its `kind` and `writePolicy` (§3). `statLeafPaths()` walks the zod schema rather than a hand-kept list, and the paired test fails if a field is missing from the registry, if the registry names a field the schema dropped, if a reader does not resolve, or if the owed set differs from the pinned list. The eighteen order-of-battle leaves are *generated* from `MILITARY_BRANCHES` rather than written out six times — in both this registry and the unit registry — because eighteen hand-copied lines are eighteen chances for one service to be documented differently from its five siblings.

**Two debts are owed again.** `demography.medianAge` and `demography.literacy` were once recorded as paid by "the nation's own rate law" — `macro.deathBaseline` aging the country and `macro.birthBaseline` reading schooling. Neither function was ever written, and nothing in the simulation reads either field: the vital rates are authored constants. Both are `owedBy` again, pinned. `demography.growthRate` is derived at assembly from the three rates beside it and read by the cohort age drift and `nationalCohortShares`. Content that gates or flavors on an owed field would compile and validate and change no outcome, which is exactly what the pin exists to make visible.

`energy.mix` is `readBy`: keys are the energy-* `SectorId`s and they seed `Nation.sectors` plus embargo/sector overlays. `trade.topExports` is `SectorId[]` (max 10), read by the embargo rung's sector hits (`embargoedShare`); no player surface reads it.

**`BAND_CONSUMERS` extends the same contract to the derived half.** The original argument was about research effort, which a derived field costs none of — and that is exactly how `relationshipBand` survived as a dead five-value enum for as long as it did, sitting outside every walk. Bands are now a registry like the rest, so a band nothing reads fails the build. Two entries are currently owed rather than read: `wealthTier`, which no live rule consults, and `militaryMetrics.sustainability.industrialMobilization`, which is computed for a wartime production model that does not exist yet. `statLeafPaths()` also unwraps optional *objects* now — left alone it stopped at the `militaryMetrics?` wrapper and let a whole sub-record of derived numbers hide behind a single registry entry.

## 5b. The person pool — generated per campaign, not stored

The pool that staffs every human role is **not** a fixed list of records. Which people exist has to differ between playthroughs, so what is authored is the *shape of the draw* and the pool itself is generated at campaign start from the seed. `WorldDataset` carries no `people` at all (§7); the studio console shows a seed's roll under Cast and never on a nation's page, and edits the role profiles that produce it rather than any person.

```ts
NameForm = "given-family" | "family-given" | "patronymic" | "malay-patronymic" | "mauritanian-patronymic"
NameGroup = {
 id?: string // the tradition's own name ("russian", "nguni"); every shipped group carries one
 form?: NameForm // how a name is written; absent reads as "given-family"
 given: { masculine: string[], feminine: string[], neutral: string[] } // >= 60/60/30
 surname: string[] // >= 60; a father's-name tradition holds its men's given names here
 weight: number // relative draw weight; default 1
}
NameBank = { groups: NameGroup[] } // >= 1 group
RoleProfile = {
 role: RoleId
 cabinet: boolean
 attributes: { competence, loyalty, ambition, integrity, charisma: TraitRange }
 likelyTraits: PersonTrait[] // >= 2
 ageRange: { min, max }
 ideologySpread: 0..1
}
PersonPoolSpec = { nation: NationId, nameCulture: string, roles: RoleId[], depthPerRole: 1..12, roleDepth?: Record<RoleId, 1..80> }
```

A person takes one trait from their seat's `likelyTraits`, and a second from the rest of the list with `SECOND_TRAIT_CHANCE` (0.6, exported beside `roleProfiles` in `dataset/people.ts` and drawn with by the engine's `people.ts`). A listed trait therefore appears in `(1 + 0.6) / n` of a seat's people, 0.533 for a three-trait list, and an unlisted one never does. The engine's disposition reader (`person-disposition.ts`) reads that incidence to center its axes on the typical cabinet member, which is why the figure is exported rather than written into the draw.

Authored in `packages/world/src/dataset/`: `name-banks.ts` (the hand-authored naming conventions), `name-banks.vendored.ts` (conventions counted from Wikidata by citizenship, baked by `scripts/bake-name-banks.mjs`), `name-banks-regional.ts` (traditions split out of a bank several peoples shared: Russian and Ukrainian; Kazakh, Uzbek, Tajik, Kyrgyz and Turkmen; Gulf Arabic, Maghrebi and Mauritanian; Tigrinya, Chadian, Comorian, Maldivian; Malay; Burmese; and three nations drawn from several traditions by population share, South Africa's Nguni, Sotho-Tswana, Afrikaans and English, Malaysia's Malay and Chinese, Nigeria's Hausa, Yoruba and Igbo). Every group carries an `id` and, where its tradition does not write the given name first and a family name after, a `form`: `family-given` for the Chinese, Korean, Vietnamese and Khmer banks; `patronymic` for Ethiopia, Eritrea, Chad, the Comoros, the Maldives, Somalia, South Sudan, Niger, Mongolia and Iceland, whose second name is the father's; `malay-patronymic` ("bin" or "binti") for Malaysia and Brunei; `mauritanian-patronymic` ("Ould" or "Mint"). `composeName(form, given, second, presentation)` (`name-forms.ts`) writes the name; `readName` reads one back into a tradition for a gate or a test. `LEAD_TRADITION` names the tradition a multi-tradition nation's lead chair is drawn from (`leadNameGroup`). `ROYAL_HOUSES` holds the reigning house of the twelve monarchies whose lead chair the monarch or heir holds (Saudi Arabia, the UAE, Qatar, Kuwait, Bahrain, Oman, Jordan, Morocco, Brunei, Eswatini, Monaco, Liechtenstein) and whether its members are named by their fathers; `royalHouseFor(nation, role)` returns it for the lead chair only, and `royalName` writes "{given} bin {father} {house}" or "{given} {house}". `nameGroupById` reads any group by id, and `packages/world/sources/leader-table/name-traditions.tsv` is the research record of which traditions each government's people carry, which the leader-fidelity test and gate hold every drawn name to and `people.ts` (a role profile for every seat any charter can create, plus `role-opposition-leader`, `role-journalist`, the Hill's two, the governors and the bureau chiefs; the nation → name-culture map; one `PersonPoolSpec` per nation — **depth 3 for NPC nations, depth 8 for the player's own**, with `roleDepth` overriding the seats that are held one to a chair rather than shortlisted: 50 governors, 60 bureau chiefs, 34 committee chairs). **`US_ONLY_ROLES` is the other asymmetry**: thirteen seats are American constructions no other charter fills — the attorney general, the Hill's congressional leader and committee chair, and the ten department secretaries — alongside the governors and bureau chiefs that were always US-only. A foreign nation used to generate three people for each of them, thirty-nine per capital who could never hold the chair they were drawn for and whose career prose was written in American institutional vocabulary. It no longer generates any: the fifteen remaining seats give every NPC nation a full cabinet under its own charter, an opposition leader and a journalist. A person's gender presentation (masculine/feminine/androgynous, rolled once and shared with their portrait so the two agree) picks which given-name bucket of their drawn group they're named from — `masculine`, `feminine`, or the unisex/non-binary-coded `neutral` bucket. The asymmetry is the point: an NPC nation only ever shows the player whoever currently holds its seats, while the player's nation is the only pool a campaign ever hires out of, for the whole campaign — every dismissal, every resignation, every minister who dies in office draws from that one bench. The founding shortlist is capped separately at `SHORTLIST_DEPTH` (3, `founding.ts`): a deep bench and a long menu at character creation are different things, and only the first is wanted. The rest of the bench is met later, on the cabinet screen, when there is a vacancy and a reason to care.

Generation is engine-side (`packages/engine/src/people.ts`: `generateNationPool`, `generateWorldPool`, `candidatesForRole`), keyed `("people", nationId, roleId, index)` so adding a nation or a seat never shifts anyone else's cast for an existing seed.

**An American is drawn by seat and party (WLD-2).** The United States' name culture (`american`, `name-cultures.ts`) is six sourced groups, one per heritage: white, black, hispanic, east-asian, south-asian and southeast-asian (`US_NAME_GROUP_BY_HERITAGE`, `nameGroupForHeritage`), built from Census 2010 surnames, SSA 1945–1995 given names by sex and Tzioumis 2018 (CC0) surname ethnicity, with the Asian American split read from ACS 2023 B02015 (`ASIAN_AMERICAN_ORIGIN`). How many women and which heritages a bench holds are read per office group and party from the vendored table `seed/women-in-cabinet.tsv` (`women-in-cabinet.ts`: `US_BENCH_ROWS` for population, cabinet, House, Senate and governors; `usBenchGroupFor`, `usBenchRow`, `usBenchGenderWeights`, `usBenchHeritageMix`), reached through `genderWeightsFor(nation, role, party)` and `seatHeritageMixFor(nation, role, party)` in `portraits.ts`. The engine draws one presentation roll per American, reads it once the party is known, draws the face from the seat's heritage mix and names the person from the group that face was drawn from. Party is one draw under role-scoped weights (`usa.ts` `usPartyWeightsFor`: no independents among cabinet chairs, congressional leaders or governors, 0.02 among committee chairs), and each major party gets a floor (`usPartyQuotaFor`: cabinet 3, congressional leaders 5, committee chairs 17, bureau chiefs 8) appended on keys past the bench's depth, so the floor members differ by seed and the cast is a few people larger than the specs' depth.

**A foreign government is drawn against the leader table.** `LeaderConstraint` (`schemas/leader-constraint.ts`) is what a foreign government's leader must match as of a stated date: `{ nation, asOf, seat: "head_of_state" | "head_of_government", title, sex: "M" | "F", ageBand: "30-39" … "80+", secondSeatTitle, regime: RegimeType, nameCulture, nameForm: NameForm, governing: PartyId[], partyless, womenCabinetShare: 0..1, shareFlagged }`, refined so `partyless` holds exactly when `governing` is empty. `RegimeType` is the table's nine values (`full_democracy`, `flawed_democracy`, `hybrid_regime`, `authoritarian`, `one_party_state`, `military_junta`, `absolute_monarchy`, `constitutional_monarchy_executive`, `theocracy`). There is no name field and no source prose: the leaders are fictional and faithful, an invented person who shares the real officeholder's sex, age band, title, regime and name culture. The table is `seed/nation-leaders.tsv`, vendored from `packages/world/sources/leader-table` as of 2026-09-23 (195 rows) by `scripts/vendor-nation-leaders.mjs` into `dataset/nation-leaders.generated.ts` (`NATION_LEADER_CONSTRAINTS`, `LEADER_TABLE_AS_OF`) and parsed by the schema. The lead chair (the charter's `cabinetRoles[0]`, `nationLeadRole`) reads the table's sex for every candidate and its age band for candidate 1 (80+ is drawn as 80–92), and carries `governing[0]` or no party; its title comes from `nationRoleTitle` (`leaderConstraintFor` reads the row). Ministries draw only from governing parties and the opposition leader only from the others. The other chairs read `cabinetWomenShareFor`, where a flagged share (one that predates the sitting cabinet) is floored at the regional mean of the unflagged rows; journalists draw 50/50, and nobody abroad draws androgynous. `nameForm` is the table's `surname_rule` read into a `NameForm` by the vendor script's rules (family name first, Malay patronymic, Ould or Mint, or a father's given name as the second name); Hungary's rule writes the family name first in Hungarian and stays `given-family`, the order every English-language page uses. Names come from `nationNameBank` (the south-asian bank is split by nation into Hindu, Muslim and Sikh traditions; the nordic bank into Swedish and Dano-Norwegian surname sets; Iceland takes patronymics built on the father's name; North Macedonia draws Macedonian names without the Albanian ones, and Albanian names for its Albanian quarter) with `surnameFor` (`surname-forms.ts`) giving women's surnames their language's feminine form in fifteen name cultures, written by `composeName` in the group's `form`. A multi-tradition nation's lead chair draws from `leadNameGroup`, and a monarchy's from its house. **The real-name guard** (`real-names.ts`) holds hashes only. `carriesRealName` refuses any name with an ordered word pair in `REAL_NAME_HASHES` (salted fnv1a keys of the word pairs in every person-page title the table's sources cite, 884) or in `OFFICEHOLDER_NAME_HASHES` (43,100 pairs from `scripts/vendor-officeholders.mjs`: every foreign cabinet in the CIA World Leaders directory, every member of Congress since 2007, every President and Vice President, every Supreme Court justice, every sitting governor and federal officer since 2017, and every head of state and government since 1945 and of the UN, NATO and the EU). Words are compared accent-, case- and particle-blind, with one spelling per romanization ("Mohamed", "Mohammed", "Muhammad") and "Abd" joined to the word after it. `carriesLeaderSurname(nation, surname)` refuses, outside a royal house and outside a father's-name tradition, a family-name word in `LEADER_SURNAME_HASHES` for that nation: every word of the names of its heads since 1945 and, abroad, of the American presidents' and vice presidents' family names, less the nation's twelve commonest family names for a head since 1990 and forty for an earlier one (and none in a bank counted from Wikidata, where the head's own family is why a name ranks). Both lists are packed big-endian uint32 in base64 (`unpackHashes`); `OFFICEHOLDER_ROSTER_COUNTS` records each roster's size. `scripts/verify-leader-fidelity.mjs` re-vendors the table and the cited blocklist in memory, holds five founded campaigns to the research table, holds each lead chair's form to the table's `nameForm`, checks fifty seeds of names, the American bench included, against the cited names and the roster, reads every drawn name back into a tradition `name-traditions.tsv` lists for its nation, and refuses short rosters; given the plaintext roster the vendor can write outside the repository (`--roster`), it also compares words and checks the committed hashes still cover every name on it.

**`role-agency-head` staffs the branch below the cabinet.** One role for the forty-five non-cabinet leadership offices in the engine's institution catalog — bureaus, operating components, intelligence elements, independent commissions — because what those seats have in common is what they select for: long service, subject-matter standing, and a loyalty that runs to the statute rather than to the administration, which is why its loyalty window is the narrowest in the table. **US-only**, the rule `role-governor` already follows, and **appended last in `ALL_ROLES`**, so adding it moved no cast that had already been dealt. Pool depth 60, held above the catalog's office count by the engine's founding test from the other side. Like the fifty governors, these are seated one to a building rather than shortlisted six at a time, so they share one bank of eight career lines by design and are exempted by name from the bench-depth and bio-repetition rules that protect a shortlist. The engine seats them at founding and on load (`career-incumbents.ts`; see `ARCHITECTURE.md`).

**Two benches are drawn only when a seat opens: `role-federal-judge` and `role-general-officer`** (`dataset/vacancy-bench-roles.ts`). The pooled cast holds no sitting judges and almost no serving officers, because no charter seat is drawn from either life. Their `RoleProfile`s are `VACANCY_BENCH_ROLE_PROFILES`, looked up through `roleProfileByRole` like any seat but **not in `ALL_ROLES`** (and so not in `roleProfiles`), so no seed's pooled cast changed; their portrait attire is `VACANCY_BENCH_ATTIRE`, read by `attireOptionsFor` after `ROLE_ATTIRE`. `ROLE_BACKGROUND_RULES` (`background.ts`, read by `backgroundRuleFor`) fixes what a bench's life must hold: a judge read law and sits on a court today (last post `judiciary`), an officer read military science and serves today (last post `military-command`); every pooled seat has no rule and draws exactly as before. `CareerArchetype` gains `judiciary` (a state or federal trial bench; `CAREER_EFFECTS` competence and integrity up, loyalty and charisma down; tie provenance `law`). Generated people live only in a campaign: the engine writes them as `person` effects with ids `PER-USA-federal-judge-<n>` and `PER-USA-general-officer-<n>` (see `ARCHITECTURE.md`, the appointment pool). No save version moved: an older save carries no such person and no `judiciary` post.

**Background comes first, and the attributes are derived from it.** A person is schooled and employed before they are scored. `Person` carries two further fields — both optional only so a save written before this existed still loads:

```ts
School = { id: SchoolId, name, nation: NationId, tier: SchoolTier, fields: StudyField[] }
SchoolTier = "global-elite" | "national-flagship" | "regional" | "service-academy"
StudyField = law | economics | engineering | military-science | medicine
 | international-relations | public-administration | journalism
 | theology | natural-sciences
Education = { school: SchoolId, field: StudyField, postgraduate: boolean }
CareerPost = { archetype: CareerArchetype, years: 1..40, startAge: 18..90 }
```

Authored in `packages/world/src/dataset/schools.ts`: real institutions, at least one per nation and at least one civilian one per nation, both asserted by the paired test. `schoolsFor` throws on an uncovered nation rather than falling back — a fallback would quietly graduate a whole government from another country's list. Named `School` rather than `Institution` because a charter already uses that word for an organ of government.

`packages/world/src/dataset/background.ts` holds the derivation: `CAREER_EFFECTS`, `FIELD_EFFECTS` and `TIER_EFFECTS` are nudges in [-1, 1] per attribute, summed across the school's tier, the field studied, and every post held (damped by `tenureWeight`, a square root, so thirty years in one post is more than five and not six times more), then squashed logistically into a position in [0, 1].

**The seat still owns the range.** `atPosition` places the person at that derived point *inside* the role window, so every existing role invariant holds exactly — an opposition leader is still never loyal, an intelligence director is still selected for competence. What the background decides is where inside the window a particular candidate lands, which is what makes two people up for the same chair differ for a reason the player can read. Service academies are drawn only for seats whose career pool includes military or intelligence work, and the same archetype never appears in two consecutive posts.

**Bios are composed, not templated** (`packages/engine/src/bio.ts`). `Person.bio` is the one piece of prose a player reads about a person, and it used to be `"{name} — {nation} {seat} candidate."` — the same sentence for everyone in the pool with three words swapped. `composeBio()` writes two sentences instead: a career line drawn from a bank of eight per seat (`BIO_ROLES` covers every role a `RoleProfile` exists for, and the composer throws rather than falling back on an unknown seat), then a line about whichever of the person's five attributes landed furthest from `0.5`, in the direction it landed. The second sentence is the point — those are the same readings `stewardSway` and `counselOnReply` consume, so the prose beside the bars agrees with the bars. The career line is chosen by shortlist position offset by a per-`(nation, role)` draw (stream key `("bios", nationId, roleId)`), so the candidates for one chair never open on the same sentence while different nations still open on different ones. `Person.bio` carries a `min(60)` floor, set under the shortest hand-authored bio in the dataset, so what it refuses is filler rather than brevity. `BIO_CAREER_LINES` (8) is held equal to the deepest `depthPerRole` by a paired test: the composer walks the bank by shortlist position, so one line short of the bench and two candidates for the same ministry come out with the identical opening sentence.

**The long-form record is composed on demand, not stored** (`packages/engine/src/dossier.ts`). A card bio is two sentences because a card has room for two; `composeDossier(person, nationName, role?)` returns the five sections the person dossier screen draws — Origins, The road here, On the record, Temperament, Where they stand. Nothing is added to `Person` and nothing reaches the save: every section is a pure function of the record already there, so a save carries five numbers and a trait list rather than five paragraphs per head across a cast of ~2,400, and a person's history can never drift out of agreement with the person. Selection is by FNV-1a hash of `"{personId}:{section}"` rather than by an RNG stream, because the dossier is composed at founding, on the cabinet screen, and forty turns later, and has to come out identical every time with no stream threaded through.

Three of the five sections are selected by the person's own data rather than drawn freely, which is what makes a dossier read as one life instead of five adjacent sentences: **Origins** is keyed to `ideology.economicLeftRight` (a left figure comes out of a mill town or a co-operative valley, a right one out of a merchant or officer household — generated independently, the two contradict each other); **On the record** is keyed to the same `standoutAttribute` the card bio names, in the same direction, so the two cannot disagree about the person in front of the player; **Where they stand** is keyed to the loyalty × ambition quadrant (rival / lieutenant / servant / passenger). **Temperament** voices every `PersonTrait` the person carries — until this existed, `Person.traits` was read by `cabinet.ts`'s risk nudge and drawn as a tag, and nothing anywhere said what it *meant*.

## 5c. Portraits — the ref *is* the coverage record

Here a `portraitRef` is the facet tuple itself, so anything holding a person can tell what their portrait shows without a lookup, and coverage gaps are visible from the cast alone.

```
npc/<heritage>/<gender>/<ageBand>/<skinTone>/<attire>/<hairStyle>/<hairColor>/<facialHair>/<eyewear>/v<variant>
```

| Facet | Values |
|---|---|
| `heritage` | 15 — `africa-west/east/southern/central`, `mena`, `europe-north/south/east`, `central-asia`, `south-asia`, `east-asia`, `southeast-asia`, `latin-america`, `pacific`, `mixed` |
| `gender` | `masculine`, `feminine`, `androgynous` — presentation, weighted 0.47 / 0.47 / 0.06 |
| `ageBand` | `early` 18–33, `emerging` 34–44, `established` 45–59, `senior` 60–74, `elder` 75–100 |
| `skinTone` | `tone-1`…`tone-6` — **its own axis**, drawn from a per-heritage sub-range that is never narrower than three tones |
| `attire` | `business`, `military`, `ceremonial`, `religious`, `academic`, `field` — drawn from a **weighted** per-role table |
| `hairStyle` | `short`, `cropped`, `long`, `tied-back`, `covered`, `bald` — weighted by presentation **and** age band |
| `hairColor` | `black`, `brown`, `fair`, `red`, `graying`, `white` — drawn from heritage, then overridden by age |
| `facialHair` | `none`, `stubble`, `mustache`, `trimmed-beard`, `full-beard` — `none` for feminine presentation |
| `eyewear` | `none`, `glasses` — more likely with age |
| `variant` | 1–8 — distinguishes two people who draw the same facets; changes the face, never the shot |

Schema and ref codec: `packages/world/src/schemas/portrait.ts` (`PortraitFacets`, `portraitRef()`, `parsePortraitRef()`, `ageBandFor()`). `parsePortraitRef` reads exactly the eleven-part current ref and refuses anything else, the pre-split nine-part form included: that shape only ever arrived on a save this build now refuses, and reading it would put a face on a person assembled by rules nothing here holds.

Data: `packages/world/src/dataset/portraits.ts` — a **weighted heritage mix per nation** (multi-heritage by default: a cast drawn from one heritage would be wrong for most of the 51 and worse than wrong for several), the per-heritage tone ranges, `ROLE_ATTIRE`, `HAIR_STYLE_WEIGHTS`, `NATION_HEAD_COVERING`, `HERITAGE_HAIR_COLORS`, `AGE_BAND_GRAYING`, `FACIAL_HAIR_WEIGHTS`, and `AGE_BAND_EYEWEAR`. Faces are drawn from these facets at render time (§5c-i); no image is generated for a person.

**Every appearance draw is weighted, and three of them were not.** A uniform pick over each table put pattern hair loss on one in six feminine-presenting officials, a head covering on a fifth of every nation's cabinet from Iceland to Iran alike, and ceremonial dress on **52%** of the world's heads of government. Weighting is therefore part of the contract, not a refinement of it: attire by role, hair style by presentation × age, head covering by **nation** (`NATION_HEAD_COVERING`, default 0.02), graying by age over a heritage base. `packages/engine/src/people.test.ts` holds each of those three at a bound measured on the generated cast rather than on the table.

**The United States draws from six weighted heritages, none exclusive.** Its cast reads as the country's population rather than a single face repeated.

**Build and `portraitClass` are gone.** `build` never reached the screen — it set a shoulder width invisible at the sizes a portrait renders at, and a facet the viewer cannot see is one the generator pays to satisfy for nobody. `RoleProfile.portraitClass` was declared, populated on all 16 roles, and read by nothing; its schema comment claimed a portraitRef was composed from it, which was never true.

Facets are rolled independently of a person's seat and attributes, so nothing in the game ties appearance to competence, loyalty, or seniority — attire excepted, which is a fact about the seat rather than the person.

### 5c-i. The face genome and the authored face reference

`Person.portraitRef` resolves in one of two forms. The facet tuple above is what every generated person carries; the face is drawn from it at render time, seeded by the person's id, so the facets stay the coverage record and the face adds the individual. An authored face (the President's, built on the founding person stage) is stored instead as

```
face:<version><fixed-width base-36 slots>
```

a whole `FaceGenome` (`packages/world/src/schemas/face.ts`) encoded by `packages/world/src/schemas/face-code.ts` (`encodeFace`, `decodeFace`, `faceRef`, `isFaceRef`, `parseFaceRef`). Version `1` is 90 characters. Continuous genes are stored at 36 steps; choices are indices into append-only registries, two characters wide for the registries expected to grow (hair cuts, head coverings, garments, cloth). A later version appends slots and never reorders them.

| Group | Genes |
|---|---|
| identity | `presentation`, `heritage` (the grouping a draw or a suggested name starts from; shapes nothing drawn) |
| `skin` | `tone` 1–6, `shade`, `undertone` (`neutral`, `warm`, `pink`, `olive`) |
| `head` | `shape` (`oval`, `round`, `square`, `oblong`, `heart`, `diamond`, `triangle`, `rectangle`), `width`, `jaw`, `chin`, `length`, `cheekbones`, `forehead`, `build` |
| `eyes`, `brows` | lid `shape` (5), iris `color` (7), `size`, `spacing`, `tilt`, `openness`; brow `style` (5), `thickness`, `arch`, `tilt`, `height` |
| `nose`, `mouth`, `ears` | `length`, `width`, `bridge`, `tip`; `width`, `upper`, `lower`, `bow`, resting `expression`; `size`, `set` |
| `hair` | `cut` (24), `texture` (4), natural `color` (8), `grayOnset`, `recession`, `part`, `volume`, `dyed` |
| parts | `facialHair` (10), `eyewear` (8 styles, 6 frame colors, `tinted`), `makeup` (7 lip colors, 4 eye styles, `blush`), `headwear` (12 with color and accent), `attire` (15 garments; primary, secondary and accent cloth from 34; lapel `pin`), `jewelry` (earrings, necklace, brooch), `marks` (`freckles`, `mole`, `lines`) |

Age, mood and treatment are render inputs, never genes. A person's current age is their founding `age` plus the whole years since the campaign's founding date (`currentAge`, `packages/player-core`); the portrait and the dossier's age line read the same number.

## 5d. Geography — a mechanic, not flavour

```ts
Chokepoint = {
 id: ChokepointId, name: string, lat, lon: number
 connects: RegionId[] // >= 2 — whose sea traffic transits
 globalTrafficShare: 0..1 // how loud a closure is worldwide
 closableByOneState: boolean // leverage vs. a mere detour cost
}
NationGeography = {
 nation: NationId
 neighbors: NationId[] // land borders, symmetric
 chokepointDependence: { chokepoint: ChokepointId, weight: 0.05..1 }[]
}
```

Authored in `packages/world/src/dataset/geography.ts`: 12 real maritime chokepoints and a `NationGeography` per nation. Geography is applied to the nation records at dataset-assembly time (`applyGeography`, called from `dataset/index.ts`) rather than typed into each record, so a border or a lane dependence is stated in exactly one place. Adjacency symmetry is test-enforced. `applyGeography` writes both `geo.chokepoints` (the ids) and `geo.chokepointDependence` (the same rows with their weights): the authored row where one exists, otherwise the region's or the country's prior (`REGION_CHOKEPOINTS`, `COUNTRY_CHOKEPOINTS`). The weights used to be dropped at this step, so a reader saw only the list; `chokepointDependence` is optional on the schema because a record that has not been through assembly carries no lanes.

**What passes a strait** (`dataset/chokepoint-flows.ts`, vendored as `seed/chokepoint-flows.tsv` and held to it by `seed-tables.test.ts`):

```ts
ChokepointFlowRow = {
 chokepoint: string // a ChokepointId
 commodity: string // a CommodityId: "crude" (EIA's broad petroleum-liquids series) or "lng"
 littoral: string[] // the governments on its shores; a war with any of them as a party contests the strait
 share: 0..1 // of world supply of the commodity that passes the strait
 bypass: 0..1 // of the strait's own flow a pipeline or a sea detour can carry when it is shut
 basis: string // the arithmetic from the figures on the page
 source: string // the EIA pages read, space-separated URLs
 opened: string // YYYY-MM-DD
}
```

Three rows, each sourced on both figures: Hormuz crude (share 0.2, bypass 0.124: 20.9 of 104.4 Mb/d in 1H25, 2.6 Mb/d of spare Saudi and UAE pipeline capacity), Hormuz LNG (0.2, bypass 0: about one-fifth of world LNG trade; EIA's bypass pipelines carry crude only) and Bab el-Mandeb crude (0.085, bypass 0.68: 8.7 Mb/d in 2023 of 102.6, and in 2024 the Cape took 3.2 of the 4.7 Mb/d the strait lost). A strait with no row has no price effect: Malacca, Suez, Panama and the Turkish and Danish straits have EIA flows but no stated capacity for their alternatives, and are left out rather than given an invented bypass. Re-read on 2026-10-13 (WOTC, updated 2026-03-03): Malacca's alternatives (Sunda, Lombok, the Myanmar-China pipeline) carry no capacity on the page; Suez and SUMED's only stated capacity is SUMED's own 2.5 Mb/d, which is part of the route, and its Cape detour is the one the Bab el-Mandeb row already counts in the 2024 episode; Panama's crude (0.1 Mb/d) fits inside the Trans-Panama pipeline's spare capacity (864,000 b/d, about 400,000 used) while its products, nearly all its flow, have only sea detours of up to 8,000 miles with no capacity; the Turkish straits' alternatives (Baku-Tbilisi-Ceyhan, Iraq-Türkiye) are named without capacities and carry other crude; the Danish straits' Kiel Canal took about 0.2 Mb/d of products in small tankers.

**Where a strait's cargo is bound** (`dataset/chokepoint-cargo.ts`, vendored as `seed/chokepoint-cargo.tsv` and held to it by `seed-tables.test.ts`; `naval-tables.test.ts` holds each destination to the roster or a bucket and each row to a priced strait):

```ts
ChokepointCargoRow = {
 chokepoint: string // a ChokepointId with a row in CHOKEPOINT_FLOWS
 commodity: string // a CommodityId
 destination: string // a NationId, or a CARGO_BUCKETS entry ("other-asia-oceania", "europe", "other") the source does not split by country
 mbd: number // million barrels a day
 period: string // the period the volume covers, e.g. "1H25"
 source: string
 opened: string // YYYY-MM-DD
}
```

Nine rows: Hormuz crude and condensate by destination in the first half of 2025 from EIA's World Oil Transit Chokepoints Figure 5 data (China 5.400, India 2.049, South Korea 1.747, Japan 1.705, other Asia and Oceania 2.257, Europe 0.586, the United States 0.427, Saudi Arabia 0.018, other 0.557). No other strait has a split on file, and a strait without one is closed to every flag alike (`engine/strait-passage.ts`).

**The US hull register** (`dataset/us-hulls.ts`, vendored as `seed/us-hulls.tsv`): `UsHullRow = { unitType, hull, name, hullClass, source, opened }`, one row per hull in commission from the Naval Vessel Register (site updated 2026-08-11, opened 2026-10-13): 11 under `navy.aircraft-carrier`, 83 cruisers and destroyers under `navy.surface-combatant` (the order of battle counts 90), 31 under `navy.amphibious-ship` and 47 active attack submarines under `navy.attack-submarine` (it counts 49). `name` is as the register prints it, capitals without punctuation. Littoral combat ships and ballistic-missile submarines are left out because no force package sends them. A hull lost once the class's names are spent is lost by class alone.

**`StateDisasterEligibility`** (`packages/world/src/schemas/state-hazards.ts`, authored in `dataset/state-hazards.ts` as `STATE_DISASTER_ELIGIBILITY`) is which US states a domestic disaster condition of a given `DisasterKind` may land in — a gate, not a weight: the engine (`placeUsOnset`, `packages/engine/src/conditions.ts`) picks uniformly within the eligible set, so a state on the list can be hit as often as any other state on it, but a state left off cannot be hit at all. Grounded in real seismicity/hydrology/climatology/storm tracks; `pandemic` has no geography and is eligible everywhere. `z.record(DisasterKind, z.array(StateId).min(1))` guarantees every kind resolves to a non-empty list, so the engine never needs an empty-list fallback. A standing condition (unrest, recession, ...) is political/economic rather than geographic and is not gated by this table at all.
