**The United States election tables** (`packages/world/src/schemas/election.ts`, authored in `dataset/{senate-classes,governor-terms,election-rules}.ts`) are what the federal calendar could not say. `SenateClassRow = { state, senateClass: 1 | 2 | 3, party, asOf }` is which party holds the seat of a given class, on the day the table was taken; the class assignment itself is structural and stays in the engine's own `SENATE_CLASSES`. `HouseDelegationRow = { state, democrat, republican, independent, vacant, source, asOf }` is how a state's House seats were held on the day the roster was taken, with an empty seat counted as `vacant` rather than handed to the party that last held it; `source` is the roster's URL. `GovernorTermRow = { state, termYears: 2 | 4, nextElection, asOf }` is how long a governor serves and when the term runs out. `ElectionRule = { state, runoffThreshold, allocation: "winner-take-all" | "by-district", twoYearTerm, consecutiveTermLimit: number | null, asOf }` is the rule a contest inside one state is decided under; `consecutiveTermLimit` is how many terms in a row a governor may serve before sitting one out, `null` where the statehouse imposes none. `UsPartyId` (`"democrat" | "republican" | "independent"`) is spelled in the world package rather than imported, because the engine holds the union the simulation reads and the world cannot depend on the engine; the three strings are the engine's own, so a row parses without translation. Every row is dated for the reason `LEADER_TABLE_AS_OF` exists: a seat's party and a term's end are facts as of a day, and a table that stated them without one would read as permanent and go quietly stale. All four tables are sourced and filled — 100 rows in `senate-classes.ts` (`SENATE_CLASS_AS_OF` "2026-09-27"), 50 in `house-delegations.ts` (the Clerk of the House's member data, `HOUSE_ROSTER_SOURCE` `https://clerk.house.gov/xml/lists/MemberData.xml`, 119th Congress, published `HOUSE_ROSTER_AS_OF` "2026-09-02" and opened 2026-09-28: 214 Democrats, 218 Republicans, one independent (California's 3rd) and two vacancies (Florida's 20th, Texas's 23rd); DC's non-voting delegate has no row), 50 in `governor-terms.ts` (`GOVERNOR_TERMS_AS_OF` "2026-09-27"), 51 in `election-rules.ts` (`ELECTION_RULES_AS_OF` "2026-09-27") — and `dataset/people.ts`'s `US_GOVERNOR_BENCH_PER_PARTY` (8) is the bench depth the governor roster stands each party to. `validate/election-checks.ts`'s `checkElectionTables` is registered in the dataset validator so the tables cannot be filled without a check that reads them; for the House table it holds one row per state, each row's seats (all four columns) to the state's `houseSeats`, and the chamber to 435.

**`US_SERIES`** (`dataset/us-series.ts`, baked by `scripts/vendor-us-series.mjs` from `dataset/seed/us-series.tsv`) is the published United States record as the agencies report it: `unemployment` (BLS LNS14000000, percent, seasonally adjusted, monthly from January 2017), `cpi` (BLS CUUR0000SA0, index 1982–84=100, not seasonally adjusted, monthly from January 2016 so its twelve-month change starts with the others) and `gdp-growth` (BEA NIPA Table 1.1.11 line 1, A191RO, percent change from a year earlier, quarterly from 2017 Q1). Dates are `YYYY-MM` or `YYYY-Qn`. A month the agency did not publish has no row: October 2025 is absent from both BLS series because the lapse in appropriations stopped collection. `US_SERIES_SOURCES` names each series' source, URL and the day it was opened; `US_SERIES_RELEASES` the publication date of each series' latest release; `US_SERIES_VINTAGE` the latest of those. Vendored 2026-09-28: releases 2026-09-04 (Employment Situation, August), 2026-09-11 (CPI, August), 2026-08-26 (BEA flat file).

**`WORLD_SERIES`** (`dataset/world-series.ts`, baked by `scripts/vendor-world-series.mjs` from `dataset/seed/world-series.tsv`) is a small real-world international reference from the World Bank's World Development Indicators: annual `unemployment` (SL.UEM.TOTL.ZS, modeled ILO estimate, from 1991), `inflation` (FP.CPI.TOTL.ZG, annual percent change in consumer prices, from 1960), `population-growth` (SP.POP.GROW, exponential growth of midyear population, derived from UN World Population Prospects, from 1961) and `gdp-growth` (NY.GDP.MKTP.KD.ZG, annual percent change in constant-price GDP, from 1961), for the United States, United Kingdom, Germany, France, Japan and Canada, to 2025. Keyed series then ISO-3 country; dates are `YYYY`. `value: null` is a year inside coverage the source did not publish (none today); years outside coverage are absent. Vintage on every row is the World Bank's own last-updated date for WDI (2026-10-08), retrieved 2026-10-11. License: Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 per the World Bank Data Catalog entry for WDI; attribute The World Bank Group and the original providers (ILO via ILOSTAT, IMF International Financial Statistics, national statistical offices and OECD files). The seed file's header carries the source, license and country names, and the loader refuses a row naming one it does not state. These are published figures and are never mixed with the campaign's simulated countries.

**Comparable metrics (`packages/world/src/metrics/`).** `MetricDefinition` (`definitions.ts`) says what a series measures precisely enough to check two against each other: `concept` (shared by every series of the same thing), `kind` (stock, flow, rate, index, count, modeled-score), `unit` (percent, fraction, index, usd-millions, count), `denominator`, `frequency`, `reference` (point, period-total, change-on-year-earlier, annual-rate), `adjustment`, `priceBasis`, `scope`, `indexBase` and `origin` (reference or simulated). `WORLD_DEFINITIONS` (`wb.unemployment`, `wb.inflation`, `wb.population-growth`, `wb.gdp-growth`) states the World Bank series on their own terms: an annual average (`period-average`), a calendar-year change (`annual-change`), a total-labor-force denominator. Because those readings differ from the monthly agency series they resemble, `checkComparison` refuses a World Bank line against an agency line, and the international view uses the World Bank row for the United States too. `WORLD_REFERENCE_OBSERVATIONS` (`world-pack.ts`) holds them by metric then country, with the ILO unemployment and the UN-derived population growth marked `estimated-reference` and the other two `observed-reference`, `WORLD_REFERENCE_MANIFEST` carries the license, entities and coverage, and `worldWindow(metric, country, first, last)` answers a window or refuses it with the covered range. `REFERENCE_DEFINITIONS` covers the four United States series the pack holds (`us.unemployment`, `us.cpi`, `us.inflation`, `us.gdp-growth`); `SIMULATED_DEFINITIONS` the campaign's three rates as fractions. `checkComparison(a, b)` (`compatibility.ts`) returns `compatible`, `transform` with the declared steps and whether they are approximate, or `incompatible` with the first reason found, checked from the most fundamental difference down: concept, kind, scope, price basis, denominator, index base, seasonal adjustment, then unit, reference and cadence. Percent against fraction is a declared multiply by 100; an annual rate against a twelve-month change is a declared approximate reading; a finer cadence is brought to a coarser one by taking the last reading (stocks, points and per-year rates) or summing (flows), never by averaging a ratio; a coarser series read finer is incompatible. `MetricObservation` (`observation.ts`, zod) carries `metric`, `entity`, `period` (`YYYY`, `YYYY-MM` or `YYYY-Qn`), `released`, `vintage`, `evidence` (observed-reference, estimated-reference, simulated, forecast), `provenance` and a `result` that is either a value or one of missing, not-applicable, not-modeled, suppressed, not-yet-observed. A zero is a value; none of the others are. `resample` (`aggregate.ts`) leaves a period missing when any constituent is, and drops a period the series does not cover in full. `US_REFERENCE_OBSERVATIONS` and `US_REFERENCE_MANIFEST` (`reference-pack.ts`) are built from `US_SERIES`: every period from first to last, with an explicit missing observation where the agency printed none (October 2025), twelve-month inflation derived only where both months exist, and latest vintage only (`vintage: 1`; the pack does not hold earlier vintages). `referenceWindow(metric, first, last)` answers a window inside coverage and refuses one outside it with the covered range. International series are in the World Bank pack below.

**`FOUNDING_DYADS`** (`dataset/dyads.ts`, seeded from `dataset/seed/dyads.tsv`: pair, kind, intensity, since, source, opened) is the particular relationships the United States has, one sourced pair at a time. `DyadRow = { pair: [id, id], kind: "close" | "cool" | "hostile" | "dependent" | "rivalrous", intensity, since, source, opened }`, the lower id first so a pair is written once. The founding graph derives every edge from the two countries' shapes, which gets the average pair about right and every pair a player recognizes wrong; these rows are the exceptions, and they are data rather than rules because there is no formula under which Washington and London come out close and Washington and Havana come out distant. 34 rows: the United States' pairs with China (rivalrous 0.5), Cuba (hostile 0.7), Iran (hostile 0.9), North Korea (hostile 0.7) and Russia (hostile 0.7), and 29 more pairs, each citing a source someone opened. `DyadRow.levels?` (`trust: [ab, ba]`, `tension`, `threat: [ab, ba]`, in pair order) is the reading a row moves each edge toward, never past, hardening only for hostile/rivalrous/cool and softening only for close/dependent. A sourced pair is a standing link, so it has an edge even with no border, compact or trade. `applyFoundingDyads` (`packages/engine/src/founding-dyads.ts`) lays them over the derived graph as the return of `buildInitialRelationships`: each row moves both directions' trust, tension and military threat toward the reading its kind and intensity give, never past it and never the other way, and scales an adversarial row's threat by the counterpart's authored doctrine posture. An edge no row names is exactly the derivation's.

**Zones: where a force can stand** (`schemas/zone.ts`, `dataset/maritime-zones.ts`).

```ts
ZoneId = string // a nation id (three capitals) or "SEA-<slug>"; branded
LonLat = [lon: -180..180, lat: -90..90]
MaritimeZone = {
 id: ZoneId // SEA-<slug>
 name: string
 ring: LonLat[] // >= 3, one unclosed ring, the game's own coarse outline
 littoral: NationId[] // >= 1, principal first; the governments whose coasts frame it
 provenance: string // how the ring was drawn
}
InstallationAnchor = {
 anchor: "sourced" | "zone-level"
 lat?, lon?: number // present iff sourced
 anchorSource: string // the opened record (a URL) behind the point; empty when zone-level
 anchorOpened: string // YYYY-MM-DD; empty when zone-level
}
```

A zone is a government's territory under its own nation id, or one of 13 `MARITIME_ZONES`: North Atlantic, Caribbean Sea, Eastern Pacific, Mediterranean Sea, Baltic Sea, Black Sea, Red Sea and Gulf of Aden, Persian Gulf and Gulf of Oman, Arabian Sea, Indian Ocean, South China Sea, Western Pacific and Sea of Japan. Rings are drawn on whole and half degrees, are not IHO limits or territorial waters, and run over coasts freely: land inside a ring is still its nation's zone. Neighboring seas share edges. Helpers: `isMaritimeZoneId`, `maritimeZone(id)`, `nationZone(nation)`, `nationOfZone(zone)`, `zonesForBelligerents(principals, joined)` (a war's zones, principals first, each once) and `zoneForTheater(theater)`, which reads a zone id as itself and maps the two theater strings the engine has written into saves through `THEATER_ZONES`: `"Persian Gulf"` to `SEA-persian-gulf` and `"Red Sea and Gulf of Aden"` to `SEA-red-sea`. The engine's `CommitmentLocation.theater` is a `ZoneId` (save v56); the v56 load step reads an earlier save's two strings through this table.

`UsBasingRow` (`dataset/us-basing.ts`) is `InstallationAnchor` plus `country`, `base`, `service`, `personnelBand`, `source`, `opened`. `BASING_COUNTRY_TOTAL` is the country-total label (never anchored), and `BASING_SPLIT_FROM` maps each retired composite label to the installations it was split into.

**Outlines on the globe** (`@admin/world/maps`). `unprojectFromPlate(x, y)` inverts Natural Earth I (Newton on latitude, then longitude by division). `nationOutline(nation)` reads a government's baked outline back to longitude and latitude rings, folding in every shape `TERRITORY_ADMINISTRATOR` gives it (Greenland for Denmark), and returns undefined where the map bakes none (Kosovo, the Marshall Islands). `onLand(point)` tests a point against every baked shape. The plane geometry those read (`ringsContain`, `ringSelfCrossings`, `ringsOverlap`, `distanceToRingsKm`, `ringsBounds`) is `geo/polygon.ts`, exported from the main entry.

**Outlines of the states** (`@admin/world/maps`, `maps/state-outlines.ts`). The state shapes are baked already projected through d3-geo's default `geoAlbersUsa()`, so a point is taken into that plane rather than the outline back out of it. `projectToUsPlate(state, [lon, lat])` is the same composite (the lower 48, and the Alaska and Hawaii insets, chosen by the state named), matching d3 to floating-point noise. `stateOutline(state)` reads the baked rings, `stateContains(state, point)` tests containment, `distanceToStateKm(state, point)` measures to the nearest edge, and `kmPerUsPlateUnit(state)` converts (about 6 km per unit; Alaska's inset is coarser).

### Federal sites and state capitols

```
FederalSite = {                  // schemas/site.ts; dataset/federal-sites.ts, seed/federal-sites.tsv
 institution: string             // the engine institution id, INST-…
 site: string                    // neutral label; a building named for a person is labeled by place or function; "" with no location
 location: "sourced" | "none"
 state?: StateId                 // present iff sourced
 lat?, lon?: number              // present iff sourced
 hqSource: string                // the record that the institution is headquartered there; "" with no location
 reason: string                  // why there is no single point; "" when sourced
 source: string                  // the record the coordinates were read from (a URL) or the provenance note
 opened: string                  // YYYY-MM-DD
}

StateCapitol = {                 // schemas/site.ts; dataset/state-capitols.ts, seed/state-capitols.tsv
 state: StateId
 capitol: string                 // the building; DC's seat of government is labeled by function
 city: string                    // the capital city
 lat, lon: number
 source: string                  // the capitol item's coordinate location (a URL)
 opened: string                  // YYYY-MM-DD
}
```

`FEDERAL_SITES` has one row per engine institution (76): 63 sourced points and 13 `none` rows with their reasons. A location is never inherited from a parent; a component stands at its parent's building only where its own record says it is headquartered there. `CABINET_DEPARTMENT_INSTITUTIONS` names the fifteen departments, and the engine's `institution-sites.test.ts` holds both lists to `INSTITUTION_CATALOG`. `federalSite(id)` returns a row, and `federalSitePoint(id)` returns `{ lat, lon }` or undefined where the row records no location. `STATE_CAPITOLS` covers the 50 states and DC; `stateCapitol(state)` looks one up. Sources and caveats are in `packages/world/sources/founding-data/federal-sites-sources.md`.

The mechanics live in `packages/engine/src/geography.ts`:

| Function | What it prices |
|---|---|
| `distanceKm` | Great-circle distance between two nations |
| `forceProjectionCost` | Distance penalty from the nearest staging point (`staging.ts#stagingPoints`: the basing table's installations and treaty allies' capitals, an ally's lead-in charged at the share lift cannot carry), waived at a shared border, damped by airlift/sealift; returns `{ multiplier, distanceKm, stagedKm }` |
| `projectionOdds` | Base odds divided by that multiplier, floored at 0.02 |
| `tradeVulnerability` | Compounding per-nation loss from a set of closed chokepoints, capped at 1 |
| `globalClosureSeverity` | Worldwide traffic disruption, independent of who it hurts |
| `chokepointExposure` | How much of a nation's trade runs through a strait one government can shut: `geo.chokepointDependence` over the closable straits, compounded like `tradeVulnerability` (Iran reads its Hormuz weight, 0.9). Read by `blockades.blockadeCut` |
| `orgEligibility` / `eligibleOrgs` | Cradle prerequisites and compact conflicts, enforced at founding |
| `cradleThreatMix` | The military pressure a founding placement wakes up next to |

`geo.coastline`'s own reader is the same rung's gate: a landlocked nation cannot be blockaded at all, so the military-3 capability gate (`instruments-military.ts`, re-exported by `instruments.ts`) refuses before `chokepointExposure` is ever priced. Both consumers were pinned in the authored-field registry (`stat-consumers.ts`) before either existed in code; `coastline`'s reader was found in the existing gate and `chokepointExposure` was written for it. The registry now names `chokepointExposure` as `chokepointDependence`'s reader and the scenario locus as `chokepoints`'.

## 5e. Organizations and cradles

`packages/world/src/dataset/orgs-cradles.ts` authors eleven organizations and one cradle. Organizations are the real bodies, and since the roster became every recognized government each list is the body's real full membership (as of 2026, cross-checked against Wikidata's current member-of statements): NATO 32, the EU 27, the African Union every African government on the roster, ASEAN 11, OPEC+ OPEC's twelve and the Declaration of Cooperation producers. An observer, associate or partner is not a member; Armenia is left out of the CSTO, having frozen its participation in 2024.

**What a body binds, and where.** `Organization.tradeCompetence?: "customs-union" | "fta" | "none"` says whether a body removes the tariffs between its members, and how far; absent reads as `"none"`. `Organization.defenseScope?: { kind: "parties-territory" | "area", area?: RegionId[], source }` is where a defense treaty's promise holds, from the treaty's own text, and is read only on `kind: "defense"`; absent means the body counts as defense wherever a member is attacked, which is how every defense body read before the scopes were written. Fifteen bodies. Four are the United States' Pacific treaty alliances, each a two-member `defense` body with the `defenseScope` its text sets: `ORG-US-JAPAN` (`parties-territory`; the text covers only territory under Japan's administration, which the scope vocabulary cannot yet say), `ORG-US-KOREA` (`area`: east Asia, southeast Asia, Oceania), `ORG-US-PHILIPPINES` and `ORG-ANZUS` (the US-Australia leg; `area`: those three plus North America, for Article V's metropolitan territory). NATO carries Article 6 as `parties-territory`. `tradeCompetence`: EU, Mercosur and GCC `customs-union`; CPTPP and ASEAN `fta`; the rest absent (none). Mercosur's members are the five seated (ARG BOL BRA PRY URY). Every shipped roster has a source and an as-of date in `ORGANIZATION_SOURCES`.

**The United Nations is deliberately not an `Organization` (2026-09-22).** `hasStandingLink` (`packages/engine/src/relationship-graph.ts`) treats any shared organization as a standing relationship, so a 193-member body would link every pair on the roster: about 38,000 edges and some 9 MB more per save, to say nothing a relationship can act on. A voting forum with a P5 veto is a different mechanism and is later work. Two integrity rules are test-enforced: every `conflictsWith` is declared mutually, and no nation is seated in two mutually-exclusive compacts. Joinability lives in one field, read against the single homeland cradle: `requiresCradle` absent means a global club reachable from anywhere (BRICS, OPEC+), `[CRADLE-POTOMAC]` means a body the US could plausibly seek or hold membership in from home (NATO, CPTPP), and an empty list means invitation-only — no founding qualifies (G7, and the regional bodies the US can never join from home: EU, CSTO, GCC, AU, ASEAN, Mercosur).

Each organization authors `history`: one paragraph (160–720 characters, no blank line) for the organization dossier's lead (DESIGN.md, `/world/org/:id`). It covers founding, what the body binds, and standing relevance to the US presidency. Campaign seat ("We sit" / "Outside") is live UI and is never baked into `history`. The paragraph must not treat dataset `members` as complete real-world membership or invent in-game members absent from that list; standing world facts that match the roster (the United States is a founding member of NATO) are allowed. The dataset validator rejects a double newline (`org.history must be a single paragraph`).

The one cradle is `CRADLE-POTOMAC` ("The Union", region-north-america): its `geo` equals the US record's own anchor and its `startingNeighbors` are Canada, Cuba (by the Guantanamo Bay land border) and Mexico, so sealing a founding through it is an identity on the nation record — the founding contract keeps its cradle field and replay determinism keeps its shape. Chosen compacts are still applied to the player nation at sealing (`applyCompactsToNation`): they become real `memberships` on top of the US's standing real ones, the player's `alignmentBand` is re-derived under the same ALIGNED/OPPOSED scoring as every nation, and the opening relationship graph reads fellow members as allies through the shared-membership and bloc-gap arithmetic.

## 5f. FlagDesign — the player's own banner, as data

Every nation on the board is a real sovereign state and flies its real flag (a Unicode regional-indicator glyph — no asset pipeline, no licensing, correct on every surface). The flag-design vocabulary below is world data that founding accepts as an optional legacy field (`FoundingChoices.flag`); the United States flies its real flag, so the US founding never reads it.

```ts
FlagLayout = "horizontal-bicolor" | "horizontal-triband" | "vertical-bicolor" | "vertical-triband"
 | "nordic-cross" | "saltire" | "canton" | "hoist-triangle" | "bordered" | "quartered"
FlagEmblem = "none" | "star" | "crescent" | "sun" | "wing" | "wheat" | "tower"
 | "anchor" | "flame" | "laurel" | "mountain" | "key" | "trident" | "book"
FlagTincture = "crimson" | "vermilion" | "ochre" | "gold" | "bone" | "ash" | "slate"
 | "ink" | "sea" | "pine" | "verdant" | "plum" | "sand" | "copper"

FlagDesign = {
 layout: FlagLayout
 tinctures: [FlagTincture, FlagTincture, FlagTincture] // three always carried
 emblem: FlagEmblem
 emblemTincture: FlagTincture
}
```

Four ids, not an image: it survives a JSON round-trip byte-identically, it diffs like the rest of campaign state, and it draws at any size on any surface because it is drawn rather than loaded. Three tinctures are always carried even where the construction consumes two, so switching construction never silently drops a color the player chose and cannot get back.

The construction set is deliberately vexillological rather than a free canvas — a flag builder that offers arbitrary gradients produces logos, not flags. `packages/world/src/dataset/flags.ts` authors the palette (`FLAG_TINCTURES`, id → name + hex), what each construction draws (`FLAG_LAYOUTS`: how many of the three tinctures it uses, which one the charge sits on, and which drawn areas share an edge), and the charge names.

**Legibility is a rule, not a suggestion.** `flagObjections(design)` is heraldry's oldest rule expressed as numbers: relative luminance is derived from the pigment itself (never authored beside it, so a palette edit cannot leave a stale brightness behind), and a design is refused when two touching areas fall within `BAND_CONTRAST_FLOOR` of each other, or when a charge falls within `EMBLEM_CONTRAST_FLOOR` of the band it sits on. `validateFounding` folds these into its ordinary `FoundingRejection` list, so an unreadable flag is refused in-fiction on the same terms as a contradictory compact.

**The vocabulary is world data.** The ids and the palette live in `packages/world` because they are the pigments a country in this setting has, which do not restyle when the app's theme does. No renderer draws a `FlagDesign` today; every flag the player sees is a real one.

## 5g. Dispatch articles — composed on read, not stored

`campaign.feed` items are one sentence each, and were a list with nowhere to go: a player who read that a neighbor had moved against them had no next step, because the headline was the whole article.

`composeArticle(item, names, context?)` (`packages/engine/src/article.ts`) returns `{ outlet, headline, standfirst, paragraphs, byline }` on the same terms `composeDossier` returns a person's record: nothing is added to the stored shape, a hundred-turn campaign's save does not grow a newspaper, and selection is by FNV-1a hash of the item's own turn and headline so a dispatch opened, closed, and reopened three turns later reads identically. What is authored is the *reporting* — how a paper writes up an event as against how an intelligence read does — and the facts come from the item. The composer never sees the campaign, so the screen passes the filing date it already prints (`context.filedOn`) and the byline carries that day; without it the byline falls back to the turn's week number.

`names` is passed rather than inferred: the caller resolves the actor and target to display names (a standfirst reading "COD and USA" is a database row in a serif face) and supplies the player's own nation id, which is the one thing a masthead genuinely would say — whether this was aimed at the government the reader is running.

**`FeedItem.facts` (`ArticleFacts`) is what makes a piece specific.** The first composer had three banks of standalone sentences — an opener, a reading, a closing — none of which named the event, so the same three paragraphs sat as comfortably under a leak as under a trade dispute. `facts` carries the particulars a body needs: `subject` (the person or body the story turns on), `figure` (a magnitude, already in its printed units), `stake` (what turns on it), and `instrument` (the catalogue name of the rung or program — "Trade Advisory", "Trade program" — kept off `subject` so it cannot be run through the person/institution banks). Optional wire metadata extends the same contract without growing the save: `dateline` (where the piece filed from, printed in the byline), `topicTags` (topic slugs for the wire header — when absent, `feedTopics()` derives them from `FeedItem.kind`), and `sceneFacet` (`{ kind?, register? }`, aligned with content scene-class vocabulary, for dispatch hero photography when kind alone is too coarse). Every field is optional because feed items are produced in around twenty places, and the composer degrades rather than invents — with no particulars it leans on the headline's own claim, which is at least the event, and it never fabricates a figure it was not handed.

The standfirst is the scan caption a list row can show: outcome and why the room should keep the item, not a generic "why the United States is reading it" clause and not a bolted-on turn number. NPC action producers hand over the rung and whether it landed; foreign governance headlines name the domain ("Italy adopts continuity as its trade program") and carry the stance summary as `stake`.

Three distinctions are load-bearing in the composer and each one was a bug first. A **person** subject (detected by shape: two or three capitalized words) gets sentences about careers and resignations; an **institution** gets sentences written for a body, because "Committee has not resigned" is a template announcing it was handed something it was not written for. A **nation is never promoted into the subject's chair** — it is a party to a story, not its center, and defaulting `subject` to the acting country produced "China's account was unhelpful". And an **intelligence read is not a newspaper**: it takes an `Assessment:` line rather than the `Why it matters:` turn, and closes on circulation rather than on publication, which is the one thing a restricted document is guaranteed never to get.

## 5h. City — major metros, a mechanic (this slice)

```ts
CityKind = "capital" | "port" | "interior" | "border" | "industrial" | "financial"

City = {
 id: CityId // CITY-{ISO3}-{slug}, e.g. CITY-USA-pittsburgh
 nation: NationId
 name: string // real name: "Pittsburgh", "Hamburg"
 kind: CityKind
 lat, lon: number // absolute WGS84, not a capital offset
 population: number // metro population, same unit as NationStats.demography.population
 employment: number // jobs, integer >= 0
 taxBase: number // USD millions, annual local/federal-relevant corporate capacity
 demShare?: number // 0..1 two-party Democratic presidential share; required on USA, forbidden elsewhere
 state?: StateId // required on USA, forbidden elsewhere; primary state for multi-state CBSAs
}
```

`City` is a world-dataset row, not map flavour. Money unit matches nation GDP (USD millions). `taxBase` is a capacity figure so a tax holiday is a local fact, not a second treasury. Live city state is `campaign.cities` (`CityRuntime`); `CityEffect` is the only mutator.

**Assembly.** `dataset/index.ts` sets `worldDataset.cities` from the baked table `dataset/cities.ts`. Capitals are assembled from `worldNations` identity + geo so a capital cannot drift from the nation record: `name === identity.capital`, coordinates are `Nation.geo`. US metros are the top 200 CBSAs (Census 2023 vintage population). `demShare` is 2020 two-party Democratic presidential share (MIT Election Lab county returns, CBSA-weighted; smaller metros use regional approximations, game-plausible like `nations.ts`). Global extras are 2–5 major places for large nations (Iceland may be capital-only). Seed extracts live under `dataset/seed/`; `pnpm bake:cities` writes the tracked output. CI does not re-fetch Census or MIT.

**Validator** (`dataset-validator.ts`): unique ids; `nation` exists; US cities >= 200 when USA is in the dataset; every nation has exactly one `kind: "capital"` city whose name matches `identity.capital` and whose coordinates sit within 0.5° of `Nation.geo`; `demShare` and `state` present iff `nation === USA`; every US `city.state` exists in `dataset.states`; lat/lon in range; population > 0; employment >= 0; taxBase >= 0.

**`usaPlaces`** is `usaPlacesFrom(cities)`: US city rows as `{ name, kind, dLat, dLon }` from the US capital. The situation map now labels a handful of metros from `campaign.cities` at real lat/lon (and the bound locus when a scenario pins a `city` id). Pittsburgh remains `CITY-USA-pittsburgh`, kind `industrial`, state `PA`.

**`CITY_CONSUMERS`** (`stat-consumers.ts`) walks the City schema the way `GEO_CONSUMERS` walks `Nation.geo`. Every authored City field has an entry, including `state`.

## 5i. State — US overlay, electoral college (this slice)

Player overlay on the playable nation only. Not fifty legislatures on NPC nations.

```ts
State = {
 id: StateId // "OH", "TX", "DC", …
 name, fips
 electoralVotes: int // sum 538 with DC
 houseSeats: int // EV - 2; DC = 0
 population: > 0 // the mass the character shares apply to
 grp: >= 0 // gross state product, USD millions
 lean: -1..1 // Dem ← → Rep, authored Cook-PVI-ish; engine may drift
 region: "northeast" | "midwest" | "south" | "west"
 character: StateCharacter // authored economic signature
}
```

Fifty states plus DC. EV is 2020-census / 2024–2030 apportionment. Validator: unique ids, 51 rows when USA is present, EV sum 538, and the three mass checks below.

**Mass and output.** `population` and `grp` live in their own table, `dataset/state-figures.ts` (Census Vintage 2023 estimates; BEA GDP-by-State 2023 rescaled onto this dataset's national GDP), because those are the two columns a vintage refresh re-bases and a refresh should be one file's diff rather than 51 edits threaded through the cohort mixes beside them. **One level owns a fact:** the states own the playable nation's mass and output, and `USA.stats.demography.population` / `USA.stats.economy.gdp` are written from the sums at assembly by `applyStateMass` (`dataset/state-mass.ts`, innermost in the pipeline so `applyBands` reads the derived figures). `gdpPerCapita` is deliberately *not* recomputed there — it stays authored so the existing unit-consistency check can still fail on a bad edit. Validator additions (`checkStateMass`): state populations must sum exactly to the nation's population; state `grp` must sum exactly to the nation's GDP; and `checkCitiesFitTheNation` refuses any nation whose metros hold more people than the nation. Containment is checked at the *nation*, not per `City.state`, because `City.population` is a metropolitan figure and metros straddle state lines — Washington's spans MD and VA, New York's spans NJ/CT/PA, Providence's reaches MA; all three are correct and all three failed a per-state rule.

**What the mass changed.** `cohortGeography` (`engine/route-cohorts.ts`) weighted a state's contribution by `|lean delta|` alone, so an act on California and one on Wyoming were the same size of act; it now weights by `|lean delta| × population`. `nationalCohortShares` (`engine/demos.ts`) weighted by `electoralVotes` — a proxy its own comment named as one, and a poor one given the floor of three — and now weights by `population`. **The two had to move in the same change:** they are numerator and denominator of one ratio, and splitting them biases every cohort strain figure toward large states. Live overlay is `campaign.states` (`StateRuntime { base, lean, marks, claims, … }`). `StateEffect { kind: "state", state, deltas?: { lean }, reason, …geography ops }` is the only mutator; lean clamps −1..1. `statePhase` each week: (1) decays marks via `replaceMarks`, (2) expires unanswered `PendingClaim`s into `omitted:` marks weighted by `federalDependence`, (3) mean-reverts lean toward `base.lean + grievanceLean(marks)` by `STATE_LEAN_DRIFT` (0.06 of the gap per turn, proportional — not a capped absolute step). At most one packet per moved state. Claim expiry covers only explicitly opened claims — never a scan of live disasters. The proportional drift rate is what makes a held mark stack reachable inside ~20 turns while a single decaying mark is not permanent. Mark decay rates: `BASE_MARK_DECAY = 0.06`, `OMISSION_DECAY = 0.03`, `GRIEVANCE_LEAN_SCALE = 0.12`, cap ±0.25. Governors: `role-governor` is last on `NON_CABINET_ROLES`; US pool depth 50; founding seats one per state except DC and stamps `PersonRuntime.state`.

**`StateCharacter`.** A closed six-axis economic signature plus two cross-cutting scalars — the state's authored answer to "who does a decision made in Washington actually hit":

```ts
STATE_ECONOMY_AXES = "industrial" | "agricultural" | "energy" | "tech" | "services" | "defense"

StateEconomy = {
 industrial, agricultural, energy, tech, services, defense: number // each 0..1
} // sums to 1 ± 0.002, validated by dataset-validator.ts checkStateEconomies

StateCharacter = {
 economy: StateEconomy
 urbanization: 0..1 // share of population in metro areas
 federalDependence: 0..1 // share of state income through federal outlays
}
```

`character` is **required.** All 51 states (50 states plus DC) are authored with plausible real-world economic composition: each state's six-axis economy is grounded in its actual sectoral mix (e.g. Michigan weighted toward industrial, Iowa toward agricultural, DC almost entirely services). The dataset validator checks that all axes sum to 1 ± 0.002.

`SECTOR_AXIS` (`packages/world/src/dataset/sector-axis.ts`) is the one place the closed 23 `SectorId`s fold into the six axes above — `Record<SectorId, StateEconomyAxis>`, exhaustive by type, so a 24th sector fails the build until someone says which axis it belongs to.

**Registry.** `population` is read by `route-cohorts.ts#cohortGeography` and `#cohortPeople`, the validator's state-mass and district checks, `states.ts#openingStates` (which seeds the live headcount the vitals phase moves) and the population lens; `grp` by the validator's state-mass sum, assembly's `applyStateMass`, and the state economy board and dossier (the domestic signature does not read it). The six `character.economy.*` leaves are read by `domestic-signature.ts#stateAffinity` and the policy portfolio's state industry shares; `character.urbanization` and `character.federalDependence` by the domestic signature's urban and federal terms and the state dossier, and `federalDependence` also by the state phase's omission-mark weight.

**`District`.** One row per US House seat:

```ts
District = {
 id: DistrictId // "TX-07"; at-large is "-AL" (AK, DE, ND, SD, VT, WY); DC's non-voting seat is "DC-DL"
 state: StateId
 number: int // 0 for at-large / DC's delegate seat
 population: > 0 // owned by the state (Law 1): sums exactly to State.population
 lean: -1..1 // Dem ← → Rep, finer than State.lean, which stays the state average
 incumbentParty: "democrat" | "republican"
 cohortSkew?: Partial<StateCohortMix> // optional; omitted reads as the state's own mix
}
```

436 rows (435 apportioned seats + DC's delegate). `dataset/district-figures.ts` carries the authored mass and lean, mirroring `state-figures.ts`'s split between the political texture (this file) and the state's own mass (`states.ts`) — a redistricting-cycle refresh is a diff of one file, not 436 edits threaded through a schema. `dataset/districts.ts` assembles `District.parse` records from it; `districtById` / `districtsOfState` are the selectors. `dataset/district-places.generated.ts` (`DISTRICT_PLACES`, baked by `scripts/bake-map-lod.mjs` from the Census 118th district shapes, the gazetteer's US metros and Natural Earth's US populated places in `scripts/vendor/ne-us-places.tsv`, public domain) names the place each seat is known by, as `{ name, how }` with `how` one of `in` (the most populous place inside the seat), `area` (no place inside; the nearest metro within 80km) or `near` (the nearest place of any size); `districtPlaceName(id)` writes it as a reader expects ("Allentown", "Chicago area", "near Faribault"). It is a presentation table beside the schema, not a `District` field, so it carries no consumer-registry entry; the district dossier and the map's labels both read it.

**What reads a district's lean.** The House's seats are read at district level: `house-seats.ts` ranks each state's districts by lean, seats the roster's Democrats at the Democratic end and its Republicans at the other, and flips the closest seats first when an election moves the chamber (see `apportionDelegations` below). The authored leans are a spread around the state's lean rather than measured partisanship, so the engine moves each state's leans together (order and spacing kept) until the roster's holders sit on their own side of even; the dataset itself is unchanged. Validator (`checkDistricts`): a state's district count equals its `houseSeats` (DC's one exception: 1 district against 0 apportioned seats, per `states.ts`'s own convention); a state's district populations sum exactly to `State.population`; no duplicate district id. `cohortSkew` is registered as sixteen `DISTRICT_CONSUMERS` leaves naming the district dossier's cohort mix as the reader; no screen reads an authored skew today (`cohortSkew` is authored on 0 of 436 districts, which is why the map-first player shows no district approval). `routeCohorts` (the engine's cohort router) still reads a district's parent state's `StateCohortMix` uniformly across every district inside it — the skew has a player-facing reader, not yet a simulation one.

**Cohorts.** Ten overlapping axes cutting the population twenty-nine different ways — a person is in exactly ten cohorts at once, one per axis, never partitioned into one of twenty-nine exclusive cells (DECISIONS 5.8; the first five axes are the original sixteen cells, the last five were added 2026-10-11):

```ts
// packages/world/src/schemas/cohort.ts
COHORT_AXES = "age" | "class" | "schooling" | "place" | "work" | "race" | "sex" | "nativity" | "disability" | "veteran"

COHORT_IDS =
 "age-under-35" | "age-35-64" | "age-65-plus" |
 "income-struggling" | "income-comfortable" | "income-affluent" |
 "no-degree" | "degree" |
 "urban" | "suburban" | "rural" |
 "goods-producing" | "frontline-services" | "professional-technical" |
 "public-and-uniformed" | "not-in-the-workforce" |
 "race-hispanic" | "race-black" | "race-asian" | "race-white" | "race-other" | // non-Hispanic single race, except Hispanic; other = AIAN, NHPI and two or more
 "sex-female" | "sex-male" |
 "foreign-born" | "us-born" |
 "with-disability" | "no-disability" |
 "veteran" | "non-veteran"

COHORT_AXIS: Record<CohortId, CohortAxisId> // exhaustive by type — a 30th cohort fails the build until this map names its axis
cohortsOfAxis(axis): readonly CohortId[] // derived from COHORT_AXIS, never a second list
```

**Where the five demographic axes come from.** The first five axes are authored by hand in `dataset/states.ts`. The race, sex, nativity, disability and veteran axes come from `dataset/state-demographics.ts` (`STATE_DEMOGRAPHICS`), baked by `scripts/vendor-state-demographics.mjs` from `seed/state-demographics.tsv` and merged into each state's `cohorts` in `worldStates`. Race, ethnicity and sex are US Census Bureau Vintage 2023 population estimates by characteristics (July 1 2023; non-Hispanic single race, with `race-other` holding American Indian and Alaska Native, Native Hawaiian and Pacific Islander and two or more races). Nativity, disability and veteran status are approximate hand-rounded shares (`seed/state-demographics-approx.tsv`) from public ACS 2023 summaries, because the Census API that serves those tables now needs a key, rescaled so the population-weighted national means match the published 14.3 percent foreign-born and 13.6 percent with a disability; veteran share of civilian adults (national 6.1 percent) is converted to a share of residents with the state's 18-and-over share. Disability is the civilian noninstitutionalized rate applied to residents. Not exact, and not meant to be: see below.

**Policy channels (`cohort-channels.ts`).** `DomesticSignature` gains optional `channels?: Partial<Record<DomesticChannel, number>>`, `DomesticChannel = "civilRights" | "immigration" | "healthAccess" | "disabilityPrograms" | "veterans"`, each -1..1 (positive extends what the channel delivers, negative withdraws or restricts it). `CHANNEL_REACH: Record<CohortId, Partial<Record<DomesticChannel, number>>>` is exhaustive over the 29 cohorts, each reach 0..1 and zero where unlisted. The content package's `DomesticSignatureShape` mirrors the field. No stored state changes: channels only change what a routed act writes into `campaign.cohorts`.

**Opening variation (`demographic-jitter.ts`).** Founding calls `openingStates(streams)`, which samples every state's ten axes around the authored values: a national factor per cell, drawn once per campaign from `stream("founding", "demographics", "national")`, and a state factor on top from `stream("founding", "demographics", "state", <id>)`, each a lognormal relative change with the standard deviations in `JITTER_SPREAD` (race 4 and 6 percent, nativity 7 and 9, disability and veteran 6 and 8, the original five 2 to 4, sex under half a percent), then each axis renormalized to sum to one. Population, lean and the sector mix are never sampled. The result lives in `campaign.states[id].base.character.cohorts` and is saved with the campaign, so reopening never rerolls it; `openingStates()` with no streams returns the authored values, which is what fixtures use. An older save with only the original five axes is filled from the unsampled authored table on load (`withDemographics`, `save.ts`) and its own five axes are left as they were.

The per-state composition hangs off `StateCharacter`, additive beside `economy`:

```ts
StateCohortMix = {
 age: { "age-under-35", "age-35-64", "age-65-plus": number } // 0..1 each
 class: { "income-struggling", "income-comfortable", "income-affluent": number }
 schooling: { "no-degree", "degree": number }
 place: { "urban", "suburban", "rural": number }
 work: { "goods-producing", "frontline-services", "professional-technical",
 "public-and-uniformed", "not-in-the-workforce": number }
 race: { "race-hispanic", "race-black", "race-asian", "race-white", "race-other": number }
 sex: { "sex-female", "sex-male": number }
 nativity: { "foreign-born", "us-born": number }
 disability: { "with-disability", "no-disability": number }
 veteran: { "veteran", "non-veteran": number }
} // each axis sums to 1 ± 0.002 — `checkStateCohorts` enforces axis-sum; urbanization and age reconciliation are not checked yet

StateVitalAnchor = {
 unemployment: -0.08 .. 0.12 // deviation from the national rate, a fraction
 crimeIndex: -0.25 .. 0.35 // deviation on the nation's own 0..1 index
 costOfLiving: -0.25 .. 0.60 // deviation on the nation's price index
} // .strict(); population-weighted mean of each column is 0 ± 0.005

StateCharacter = {
 economy: StateEconomy
 urbanization: 0..1
 federalDependence: 0..1
 cohorts: StateCohortMix // required
 vitalAnchor: StateVitalAnchor // required
}
```

**`vitalAnchor` stores deviations, never absolutes.** A state's reading is `nation + deviation` — `clamp01(nation.unemployment + anchor.unemployment)` and likewise for the other two — so the state figure is never stored beside the national one it would have to be reconciled against. 153 absolute state figures would drift out of agreement with the country and need a reconciliation pass every turn; a deviation is stable under any national move, sums to a checkable zero, and makes the double-billing question at this level trivial, because the national aggregate *is* the national figure rather than a sum over states. It is the same argument `State.population`'s own doc comment makes in the other direction, and the one `bands` and `memberships` are derived on.

The anchors live in their own table, `dataset/state-vitals.ts`, on `state-figures.ts`'s precedent: these are three columns a BLS or BEA vintage refresh re-bases together, and a refresh should be one file's diff rather than 51 edits threaded through the cohort mixes. Provenance is BLS state unemployment (2024 annual averages), FBI Uniform Crime Reporting violent-crime rates rescaled onto the nation's 0..1 index (2023), and BEA regional price parities less 100 — every figure approximate and hand-rounded for game balance, as everywhere else in this dataset.

**Each column is population-weighted centered on zero**, which is why an individual figure sits a little off the published gap it came from: the residual of centering is spread across every row rather than dumped on one. Centering is what stops 153 hand-authored numbers from silently re-baselining the whole country — a table where every state sits half a point above the nation is a claim that the nation's own figure is wrong, and nothing else in the dataset would notice. Validator check `state-vital-anchor-centers` holds the three weighted means to 0 ± 0.005.

A vital anchor **steers and never bills**: it never stamps a grievance, writes `lean` or moves opinion of its own — the act that caused the deviation was already billed at the nation. It does not yet steer placement either. A nationally-billed condition lands in the United States by a seeded roll over the eligible states and cities (`placeUsOnset`, `us-placement.ts`), which reads no anchor, and a national vital edge routes to cohorts nationally, because routing by a state's deviation is not built (`vital-edges.ts`, `vitals.ts`).

`cohorts` is **required**: the 51 authored shares (50 states + DC) are complete, each state grounded in real demographic patterns (age, class, schooling, place, work). The schema enforces that every state carries the full five-axis cross-cut; there is no national cohort table to disagree with the state-specific data. Every runtime cohort figure in the campaign derives from this table.

**Motivations.** The balance table that gives each cohort its politics lives in `packages/engine/src/demos.ts`, not here, on the precedent the retired coalition lenses set:

```ts
// packages/engine/src/demos.ts
CohortMotivation = {
 tilt: Record<OpinionDriver, number> // [LENS_TILT_FLOOR, LENS_TILT_CAP] = [0.55, 1.8]
 beliefs: IdeologyVector // same three axes as Person.ideology / Party.beliefs
 spread: number // >= COHORT_MIN_SPREAD (0.35)
 exposure: Record<StateEconomyAxis, number> // sums to 1 — routing target
 programReliance: number // 0..1
 attention: number // 0..1
 baseTurnout: number // TURNOUT_FLOOR..TURNOUT_CEILING
 name: string
}
COHORT_MOTIVATION: Record<CohortId, CohortMotivation> // twenty-nine entries
cohortLens(cohort) = normalize(OPINION_WEIGHTS ⊙ tilt) // derived, never authored
```

Law 3 bounds and the `cohort-lens-centered` check (share-weighted mean lens within `LENS_CENTERING_TOLERANCE` 0.02 of `OPINION_WEIGHTS` on every driver, per axis) are unit-tested against the real table.

**Registry.** Twenty-nine `STATE_CONSUMERS` leaves, `character.cohorts.<axis>.<cohort>`, generated from `COHORT_AXIS` rather than hand-copied (the same shape `orderOfBattleConsumers()` uses), each read by `route-cohorts.ts#cohortGeography`, `demos.ts#nationalCohortShares` and `state-cohort-opinion.ts#axisProblem`. The "Country panel" once cited beside them does not exist. The district `cohortSkew` leaves are owed: the district dossier that merged them over the state mix was deleted with the previous player.

Presidential win is EV ≥ 270 (`electoralVotesWon`), not popular share alone.

**State approval (`regional-opinion.ts`).** `stateApprovals(campaign)` returns every state's approval, 0..1, keyed in `STATE_IDS` order; `stateApproval(campaign, id)` reads one. It is a pure read and display only: nothing decides an outcome on it. Each state's term is `STATE_APPROVAL_LOGIT_SLOPE` (4, the slope of the logit at one half) × (its map shift + its economic shift). The map shift is `stateShareShifts` (`states.ts`): `administrationShare(lean, 0.5, standing) − 0.5` plus the ticket's home-state tilt, the same alignment and tilt `callMap` adds to the national share, so a state the map puts three points above the country reads three points above it at an even headline. The economic shift is `−(anchor.unemployment × the national unemployment's relative change from the authored economy × STATE_UNEMPLOYMENT_STRAIN 2 + anchor.costOfLiving × priceLevelScar) × the week's prosperity weight`; the anchors are population-centered, so it moves states against each other rather than the country. Grievance marks are not read: they already move lean through `grievanceLean`. One offset `k` is solved by bisection (bracket ±40 log-odds, 100 halvings, which converges to a double's spacing) so that the population-weighted mean of `logistic(k + term)` equals `stateApprovalTarget` = the stored headline, clamped to 0..1. The honeymoon is part of the valence every bloc reads (`HONEYMOON_VALENCE`, below), so the headline carries it and the states average to it; a state's own bloc reading (national shares tilted by the state's lean) is not built, so a state is the headline moved by its map and its economy and by nothing else. The clamp comes after the solve, so the mean holds at every headline, including the removal-pressure range; at a target of exactly 0 or 1 every state reads the target. There is no district approval: `cohortSkew` is authored on no district, so a district figure would be invented.

`stateApprovalHistory?: readonly StateApprovalSample[]` (v55) is the weekly record, oldest first. `StateApprovalSample = { turn, approval: Partial<Record<StateId, number>> }`, values rounded to a thousandth. `closeWeek` appends `WeekOpening.stateApproval` — sampled from the campaign the week opened on, labeled with that week's turn, so a row lines up with the `metricHistory` row appended beside it — bounded at `METRIC_HISTORY_WINDOW`. Founding opens it empty; it has no inherited rows, unlike the national series. A `WeekOpening` stored mid-week by an older build carries no sample, and that week appends nothing. player-core's `world/state-approval.ts#stateApprovalReading(campaign, id)` reads a state's approval beside the call `projectedStateCalls(campaign, projectVote(campaign).share)` makes, with one `stateApprovalDivergence` line where they point opposite ways. House midterm `seatSwing` weights each state's `houseSeats` by competitiveness (`1 - |lean|`).

**State-by-group support (`state-cohort-opinion.ts`, `state-cohort-sensitivity.ts`).** `stateCohortOpinion(campaign)` returns, for each of the ten cohort axes, an `AxisFit`: `support[state][cohort]` (0..1), the groups' `national` figures (`cohortSupport`), the axis's `reconciliation` step and its `residuals`. `stateCohortSupport(campaign, state, cohort)` reads one cell. Derived on every read, never stored (DECISIONS 1.10), display only, memoized per campaign object in a `WeakMap` (campaigns are immutable values, so the key cannot go stale). Each axis is fitted on its own because each partitions a state's people: `logit p(g, s) = a_g + b_s + beta_g · z_s + tilt(g, s)`, where `z_s = logit(0.5 + stateShareShifts[s])` (the state's partisan context toward the administration at an even country), `beta_g` the group's sensitivity, and `tilt(g, s) = STATE_APPROVAL_LOGIT_SLOPE × STRAIN_SCALE × Σ valence · weight · (intensity − 1)` over the group's strains, where intensity is the strain's `where[s]` (0 if the state is absent, 1 if the strain has no `where`). `a_g` and `b_s` are raked in log-odds: each sweep solves every `b_s` so the state's share-weighted group mean equals `stateApprovals[s]`, then every `a_g` so the group's people-weighted mean over states equals its target; Newton steps bounded at ±4 per step, at most `RAKE_MAX_SWEEPS` (200) sweeps, stopping when both margins are within `RAKE_TOLERANCE` (1e-7). The group targets are `cohortSupport` moved by one shared log-odds `reconciliation` per axis, solved so the axis's people-weighted mean equals the states' population-weighted mean; the two margins are read by different rules and raking cannot meet totals that differ. Measured 2026-10-11 on founded campaigns of both parties: 2 to 8 sweeps per axis, state residual under 1e-7 and national under 1e-8, reconciliation under 0.006 log-odds; about 6 ms for all ten axes in Node.

`STATE_COHORT_SENSITIVITY: Record<CohortId, { beta, se, stateYears, voters }>` is fitted, not hand-authored. Source: Cooperative Election Study Common Content 2020 (Harvard Dataverse doi:10.7910/DVN/E9N6PH, `CES20_Common_OUTPUT_vv.csv`, version 4) and 2024 (doi:10.7910/DVN/X11EP6, `CCES24_Common_OUTPUT_vv_topost_final.csv`, version 9), downloaded 2026-10-11 through `https://dataverse.harvard.edu/api/access/datafile/4949558` and `/12050325`. Sample: validated general-election voters (`CL_2020gvm` 1–5, `TS_g2024` 1–6) reporting a two-party presidential vote (`CC20_410`/`CC24_410` = 1 or 2), weighted by `vvweight_post`. Method: per group, a binomial regression (IRLS, Kish effective sizes as trials, cells under five respondents dropped) of the group's two-party share in each of 102 state-years on the logit of that state-year's two-party share in the same survey; `beta` = slope − 1; `se` = state-cluster bootstrap, 300 resamples, seed 20261011; rounded to three decimals. Group definitions, the industry mapping and the limits are in the file's source block; the script is `docs/audits/2026-10-11-simulation-depth/state-cohort-sensitivity-fit.py`. Limits: the CES has no disability question, so `with-disability` is fitted on the permanently-disabled-for-work respondents; the Asian, Black, Hispanic and foreign-born rows rest on 49 to 83 state-years. Only within-axis differences in `beta` move a reading; a shift common to an axis is absorbed by `b_s`.

`Campaign.cohortSensitivity?: Record<CohortId, number>` (v60) is the campaign's own draw, made once at founding by `openingCohortSensitivity(streams)` on the stream `("founding", "electorate", "state-cohort-sensitivity")`: `beta + N(0, 1) × se × SENSITIVITY_JITTER_SHARE` (0.5), rounded to thousandths. `cohortSensitivity(campaign)` reads it, falling back to the unjittered `beta` per cohort on a save founded before v60; nothing is drawn at read time.

## 5j. Sectors — closed 23 (this slice)

Not the original 16-set. ISIC-section / GICS-broad. Writers cannot invent ids. No `retail-wholesale`.

```
auto, aerospace, semiconductors, steel-heavy, defense-industrial,
ports-shipping, telecom-cyber, finance, housing-construction, public-infrastructure,
energy-oil, energy-gas, energy-nuclear, energy-renewables,
agriculture, textiles, mining, chemicals, food-processing,
tourism-hospitality, healthcare, pharma, utilities
```

`Nation.sectors: SectorMixEntry[]` — `{ sector, mix 0..1, employment, laborPressure 0..1 }`. Mix sums to 1 (±0.002). `trade.topExports` is `SectorId[]` max 10. `energy.mix` keys are the energy-* subset. Coal-as-fuel in mix → `energy-oil` (fossil thermal; there is no energy-coal). Coal as a commodity / topExport → `mining`.

Live overlay is `campaign.sectors[nation][sector]` (`SectorRuntime`). `SectorEffect { kind: "sector", nation, sector, deltas?: { employment, laborPressure, mix }, reason }` is the only mutator. Employment rolls into unemployment; laborPressure rolls into protestPressure. Embargoes emit `SectorEffect`s on the *target's* `topExports`, not only a generic export haircut. Unions are `laborPressure`, not objects.

`ScenarioLocus` may carry optional branded `city`, `state`, `sector`, and `bind?: "condition"`, which binds the place to the thing that is happening rather than rolling one — without it a scenario about a live condition picked its own state, so a week about the wildfires could open in a state that was not burning. Absent is the ordinary case. On home ground a `city` / `coast` / `interior` / `border` site with no `city` resolves inside the authored `state`, then the state the story's earlier scene happened in, then a state drawn by fit from the seed and the scenario id (`seededState`, `scenario-locus-draw.ts`), rather than at an offset from Washington; only a `capital` site, `state: "DC"` or a DC city is Washington (ARCHITECTURE.md §5, "Locus resolution"). A home `chokepoint` site resolves as a `coast` site in a nation mapped by state: the United States' only dataset strait is the Panama Canal, which is not its ground. `ResolvedLocus.state` can therefore be an inherited or drawn state; the map, the reply's state-scoped effects (`gazetteer.stateLean`, the impact model's `populationToll.place`) and the decision's causal stamp read it, while gates and prose tokens read only the authored `locus.state`. `ResolvedLocus.city` is the dataset city the resolver stood the event in, whichever way it was chosen; `resolveLocus` pins exact lat/lon/name when `locus.city` is authored, and `placeName` is the dataset name (`Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania`). Abroad, a `coast` / `interior` / `city` site takes a non-capital dataset city of the site's kind, drawn by population, where the nation has one, and otherwise keeps its nation-level area label; it is never placed in the capital. The content audit `unplaced-domestic` is an **error**: domestic plant/factory/industrial-zone/tax-holiday copy without `locus.city` (state or sector alone is not enough); strike/Midwest copy without city, state, or sector. `generic-place` refuses stand-in metros (`industrial zone`, `the rust-belt city`, …). Domestic economy with `locus.city` must write `{{city}}` (`missing-city-token`). `unknown-city` is an **error** when `locus.city` is set to an id not in `WorldDataset.cities`, or when the city's nation is not the locus ground (home / `cast: none` → USA). Position replies may carry a closed `gazetteer` recipe (`cityEmployment`, `taxBase`, `adminLean`, `taxHolidayYears`, `stateLean`, `sectorEmployment`, `sectorLaborPressure`) resolved into one `CityEffect` per reply at cast (`taxHolidayTurns = years * TURNS_PER_YEAR`). Position **and** instrument replies may also carry optional `domestic?: DomesticSignatureShape` (`packages/content/src/domestic-signature.ts`) — a zod mirror of the engine's `DomesticSignature` (magnitude enum and `STATE_ROUTE_CAP` / `STATE_ROUTE_TURN_CAP` / `MAGNITUDE_STEP` imported from the engine, not restated). `values?: Partial<IdeologyVector>` is the same optional term the mirror now carries: a reply about an identity or rights question declares it exactly like `pull`, and `routeDomestic`/`routeCohorts` fold it against `State.lean` and `COHORT_MOTIVATION[cohort].beliefs` respectively, so the act lands with real state and cohort texture instead of only moving the national alignment score `pull` already moves. At cast, `domesticSignatureEffects` calls `routeDomestic` and splices the resulting `StateEffect`s into the reply's `resolved` list beside any gazetteer effects; instrument commitments carry the same optional `resolved` for that splice. A reply may author gazetteer and domestic together (locus-specific vs systemic). Foreign scenarios must not grow US-state gates.

**`STATE_CONSUMERS`**, **`SECTOR_CONSUMERS`**, and **`PARTY_CONSUMERS`** sit in `CONSUMER_REGISTRIES` beside City.

## 5k. CoverPackage — fail-closed article covers

A dispatch cover is not a topic label. `CoverPackage` (`packages/content/src/cover-package.ts`) is one object the studio article form must complete before publish can stamp. Missing any part fails closed: no brief, no cover.

| Field | Rule |
|---|---|
| `brief.situation` | Required. Helper: "who is this for, and what are they stuck in? A person, not a topic." |
| `brief.metaphor` | Required. Helper: "the picture that asks the question. If you can google it, start over." |
| `brief.refuse` | Required. Helper: "name the stock photo so we don't ship it." |
| `recipe` | Closed enum: `object-as-metaphor` \| `scene` \| `character` \| `doodle-diagram` |
| `plate.assetPath` | Uploaded plate sitting next to the brief |
| `plate.lockCite` | Must name a versioned lock in `content/cover-locks/` whose cited scan file is on disk and non-empty. The lock is that scan (dry felt-tip on cream paper), not a prompt. |
| `kicker`, `headline` | Required. They sit under a full-bleed plate, not beside a topic thumbnail |

Publish also refuses when the plate could be a stock photo: no lock citation, a citation whose scan is absent, or a metaphor/situation that matches the named refuse cliché (`plateLooksLikeRefusedStock`). `judgeCoverPackage` / `publishCoverPackage` are the only accept path. This contract is editorial; it is not stored on `FeedItem` and does not grow a campaign save.

## 6. Relationship, Person, Organization, Cradle, Charter

- **`Relationship`** is directed (`from`/`to`), carrying `trust`/`alignment`/`tension`/`tradeDependence`/`militaryThreat`, a `treaties: TreatyId[]` list — **derived at founding, not opened empty**: `relationship-graph.ts` seeds one id per shared `kind: "defense"` `Organization` via `compactTreatyId(org)` (`compact:<OrgId>`), so a pair that both sit in NATO opens holding the North Atlantic Treaty and a pair that share only the single market opens holding nothing. Ids minted this way are distinct from the `treaty:<kind>:<counterpart>:<signedTurn>` records `signTreaty` writes into `campaign.treaties`, and an inherited compact has no `TreatyRecord` behind it, because nobody in this administration signed one — `grudges: Grudge[]` (each with a `decayRate`), and an append-only `history: RelationshipEvent[]`.
- **`Person`** is the one pool for every human role: cabinet candidates, NPC heads of state and ministers, opposition figures, journalists. `eligibleRoles: RoleId[]` gates which seats a person can fill; `portraitRef` binds into the kept imagery manifest; `bio` is player-facing prose with a `min(60)` floor and is composed from the person's own attributes rather than templated (§5b). **`education` and `career` are required**, not optional: they are the record the five readings are derived from, and a person carrying neither has no explanation for the numbers beside them. Every generator writes both, the player's own leader included (§ "The player is composed, not drawn"). Optional `party: PartyId` is the named ticket on that person's national charter (`Charter.parties`) — Democrat / Republican / Independent on the US constitution, omitted when a charter authors no tickets (a junta, a one-party council). It is optional because a junta person carries none — that charter authors no tickets — never for save compatibility. Hill display maps caucus through `Congress.caucusAnchor` so a majority leader cannot read as the other party even if a generated `Person.party` disagrees — and so the words stay Democrat / Republican under a president who is neither. `birthplace` is the one optional prose field: it is drawn from the person's own country's cities, and is absent only where the dataset maps too few distinct places to tell two people from that country apart — a nation given one city would otherwise seat its whole cast in it, which is a fact about the country wearing a person's clothes. The dossier's reading of a person carries no stored prose at all: `strengths[]`, `weaknesses[]` and `priorOffice` were optional fields no generator ever wrote and no dataset ever authored, and they are deleted. What the block prints is derived at render time by `personAssessment` from the readings already on the record, placed inside the window the seat recruits from, with the part of the education or career that pushed each reading furthest named beside it (`packages/engine/src/person-assessment.ts`).
- **`IDEOLOGY_AXIS_POLES`** (`schemas/person.ts`) is the one table of what each `IdeologyVector` axis means and which way is positive: `economicLeftRight` (caption "Economic policy", Left −, Right +), `authLibertarian` ("State authority", Libertarian −, Authoritarian +) and `nationalistGlobalist` ("World view", Globalist −, Nationalist +). `IdeologyAxis` names the three keys. Every surface that draws an axis (the dossier's ideology bars, a justice's file, `metric-copy`'s scales) reads its words and polarity from here; `metric-copy` once printed the third axis inverted.
- **`Plank`** (`schemas/platform.ts`) is one plank of the platform the President runs on: `{ id: PlankId, title, summary, courts: CohortId[] }` (`courts` at least one). `PlankId` is a branded string. The dataset carries only what every surface needs; what a plank moves is the engine's (`platform-deliverables.ts`), against the policy and order catalogs it already has, so the dataset holds no stance or act id it cannot check. `platformPlanks` (`dataset/platform.ts`) ships ten, five on each party's usual ground: `plank-balanced-budget`, `plank-rebuild-infrastructure`, `plank-fair-share`, `plank-energy-dominance`, `plank-clean-energy`, `plank-fair-trade`, `plank-secure-border`, `plank-rebuild-military`, `plank-reform-policing` and `plank-open-government`; `plankById` indexes them.
- **The White House posts** (`dataset/white-house.ts`) are the offices around the President that are not cabinet chairs: `VICE_PRESIDENT_ROLE_ID` (`role-vice-president`) and `WHITE_HOUSE_STAFF_ROLE_IDS` (`role-chief-of-staff`, `role-national-security-adviser`, `role-press-secretary`), titled by `WHITE_HOUSE_ROLE_TITLES` ("Vice President", "White House Chief of Staff", "National Security Adviser", "White House Press Secretary"). `WhiteHouseStaff` is `Partial<Record<WhiteHouseStaffRoleId, PersonId>>`, a post nobody was picked for absent rather than null. They are in `US_ONLY_ROLES` and in no charter's `cabinetRoles` and not in `ALL_ROLES`, because none is a cabinet chair: the Vice President is elected on the ticket and the staff are the President's own hires, so seating them as chairs would put them through confirmations, break rolls and department ownership they do not face. The charter's `roleTitles` cannot carry the titles (the validator refuses a title for an unseated chair), so the engine's narration reads `WHITE_HOUSE_ROLE_TITLES` directly.
- **`PersonConnection`** (`schemas/person-network.ts`) — one edge of the acquaintance graph over the campaign cast: `{ from: PersonId, to: PersonId, kind: PersonTieKind, provenance: TieProvenance, reason, strength }`. **What a tie *is* and where it *came from* are two questions, and one enum could not answer both.** `PersonTieKind` is the meaning half, kept deliberately small — `mentor`, `peer`, `ally`, `rival`, `former-colleague`, `prior-counterpart`. `TieProvenance` is the shared history the tie rests on — `education`, `party`, `civil-service`, `military`, `intelligence`, `diplomatic`, `law`, `business`, `academia`, `local-government`, `press`, `activism`, `medicine`, `labor` — every member a fact a `Person` record already carries. The retired single enum mixed the two, so it could not express "a rival from the same party" or "a former colleague out of the diplomatic corps": it had to pick one of the two facts and drop the other, and it grew a synonym every time a new source of acquaintance appeared. `PROVENANCE_OF_ARCHETYPE` maps every `CareerArchetype` onto a provenance and is total in both directions, so a new archetype fails to compile until somebody says what a tie built on it is called, rather than falling into a catch-all that names the wrong working life. **`mentor` is the one directional kind**: the edge runs `from` the senior figure `to` the junior one and carries both readings at once, so a person's file prints "Mentor" when the edge is read at the `to` end and "Protégé" when it is read at the `from` end. There is no `protege` kind, because a second edge would be one fact stored twice — the retired enum declared one, no rule ever emitted it, and every junior half of a mentorship therefore read "Mentor" on their own file. For every other kind the pair is unordered and sorted by id. `reason` (12–240 characters) must cite the deterministic fact the rule actually read — the years two careers overlapped, the seniority one already had when the other arrived, the school they both name — never a claim the person record cannot support. `prior-counterpart` is the only kind that may cross a nationality boundary, and the only one that must; every other tie joins two people of the same country. The graph is **not stored on the campaign save**; `packages/engine/src/person-network.ts` derives it deterministically from `(seed, living cast)` on demand, the same contract as the dossier and cabinet tie graph. See `ARCHITECTURE.md` §3.
- **`CountryReference`** (`schemas/country-reference.ts`, `packages/world/src/dataset/country-reference.ts`) is static dossier prose for a nation: `nationId`, `executiveSummary` (3–6 paragraphs as one string with `\n\n`), `background`, `governmentSystem`, `economyNarrative`, optional `societyNarrative` and `securityNarrative`, and `whyItMatters`. It is **not** part of `WorldDataset` and is **not** covered by `stat-consumers.ts` — live stats, relationships, and posture always come from the campaign. Authored stubs ship for the United States and selected major allies or rivals; `countryReferenceFor(nationId)` in world and `selectCountryReference(nationId)` in the engine return a reference or `undefined` when no stub exists yet.
- **`Organization`** carries `members: NationId[]`, `conflictsWith: OrgId[]` — the mechanism behind "a NATO member cannot also sit in BRICS" — `kind` (`OrganizationKind`: `defense` | `economic` | `political`), what the body actually binds its members to, and `history`, the authored one-paragraph lead for organization dossiers (DESIGN.md, `/world/org/:id`): founding, what the compact binds, and standing relevance to the US presidency, never the live campaign seat. `kind` is required, not optional: the opening relationship graph reads it to tell a mutual-defense guarantee from a customs union from a standing summit club, and a membership count that cannot tell them apart reads a trade partner and a treaty ally as the same country. It also grades bloc rivalry — two defense pacts that exclude each other are a standing confrontation, while a club whose premise is sitting outside somebody else's order is a disagreement about the world. Of the eleven shipped bodies, NATO and the CSTO are `defense`; the EU, CPTPP, Mercosur and OPEC+ are `economic`; BRICS, the GCC, the AU, ASEAN and the G7 are `political`.
- **`Cradle`** is a founding placement option: `startingNeighbors` derives from it (compact reach is authored on the organization via `requiresCradle`), and `geo: { lat, lon }` relocates the founder onto the cradle's own coastal point — required, not optional, so a cradle that changes region and neighbors can never leave the founder narrating a region it does not visibly occupy (`packages/engine/src/founding.ts`'s `applyCradleToNation`).
- **`Charter`** (`player.charters`) — the government system the player founds under, as data rather than a code branch: `id`, `name`, `removalMechanism` (one of `impeachment` / `no-confidence` / `coup` / `clerical-council` / `electoral-defeat`), `cabinetRoles: RoleId[]`, `institutions: Institution[]` (at least one), `decisionConstraint` (the one binding limit on the player's power under this charter), `successionRule` (what happens to power absent a removal), optional `roleTitles: Record<RoleId, string>` — authored display titles for the chairs this charter seats — and optional `parties: Party[]`, the named tickets that legislature actually runs. A `Party` is `{ id: PartyId, name, shortName, kind: "major" | "independent", beliefs: PartyBeliefs, cohesion: number }`, where `PartyBeliefs` is the same three-axis vector as `Person.ideology` (`economicLeftRight`, `authLibertarian`, `nationalistGlobalist`, each −1..1) and `cohesion` (0..1) is how alike the ticket's members read the same act. `beliefs` alone cannot tell a party from a bloc: a major party whips, so its platform predicts its members, while independents are everyone who joined neither and are heterogeneous rather than centrist. Every reading taken against a ticket (`ticketReading`, `ticketSplit`) is scaled by `cohesion`, so a low-cohesion bloc moves little in either direction instead of reliably to the middle. The US tickets ship at `0.8` / `0.8` / `0.3`, and the Independent ticket carries real politics on the two axes ticket assignment does not read — `authLibertarian: −0.35` (markedly more anti-institutional than either caucus) and `nationalistGlobalist: +0.15` — with `economicLeftRight: 0` because that is what averaging a bloc containing both ends produces and because ticket assignment is read off that axis. A generated person's ticket is drawn first, majors weighted double, and their ideology is placed near that ticket's `beliefs` at a distance `cohesion` sets (`rollPolitics`, `packages/engine/src/people.ts`); a president carries the ticket the player chose, and `presidentPartyFromIdeology` (the nearest major on `beliefs.economicLeftRight`) is only the fallback when the charter authors no such ticket; all three axes feed `partyReading` / `beliefSplit` (`packages/engine/src/party-beliefs.ts`). Two majors map onto the engine's administration/opposition caucuses once the president's ticket is known; `independent` is everyone who caucuses with neither. Absent `parties` is honest: inventing Democrat/Republican analogues for a junta would be a lie. Role ids are shared vocabulary across charters and the engine; `roleTitles` is presentation only, the id-derived title remains the fallback, and the dataset validator rejects a title for a chair the charter's `cabinetRoles` does not seat. Named-ticket charters must author two majors (or none) and at most one independent; the two majors must differ by ≥ 0.3 on at least one belief axis (`party-beliefs-distinct`); an independent bloc must be less cohesive than the least cohesive major (`party-cohesion`) — a bloc that is not a party cannot be more disciplined than one that is; `Person.party` must resolve to a ticket on that person's national charter. `PARTY_CONSUMERS` walks every `Party` leaf. The reusable catalogue (`packages/world/src/dataset/charters.ts`) ships seven charters spanning all five `removalMechanism` values; the United States' constitutional charter (`charter-usa-constitution`, `packages/world/src/dataset/usa.ts`) is the same shape, with impeachment as removal, American cabinet titles ("Secretary of State", "Director of National Intelligence", "Attorney General") via `roleTitles`, the three US tickets (Democratic Party, Republican Party, Independent — economic leans −0.4 / +0.4 / 0, cohesion 0.8 / 0.8 / 0.3), and a `kind: "judiciary"` institution named "the Federal Judiciary" so `institutionalCheck` can apply the 0.55 judiciary term. Interior stays Interior (lands, water, tribes). `role-attorney-general` is last on `CABINET_ROLES` so existing pool casts do not reshuffle. The US's named places are the US rows of `WorldDataset.cities`; `usaPlaces` is a view of those rows as offsets from the capital, not a parallel hand list.

- **`Institution`** — `{ name, role, kind? }`. `name` and `role` are what the charter calls this body and what it does. `kind` (`InstitutionKind`: `assembly` | `executive` | `judiciary`) is what the body *is*, independent of any charter's naming, and it is what content resolves institution tokens through: a scenario writes `{{assembly}}` and the founding charter supplies "National Assembly", "Officer Corps Council", or "Sanctuary Council". It is optional because a charter may name a body that fills no general slot (an Electoral Commission, a set of Regional Assemblies) — such a body is real, but no token can reach it, because no other charter has anything comparable.

 Across the ten shipped charters, `assembly` is present in all ten (and the validator requires it, §7), `executive` in seven, `judiciary` in five (the US constitution now among them). That asymmetry is deliberate and load-bearing: a junta has no independent court and a confederal council no single executive seat, so content naming those bodies must gate on a `charterInstitution` condition rather than assume them. See `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §5.

## 7. WorldDataset and the validator

`WorldDataset = { nations, cities, states, districts, relationships, organizations, cradles, charters }` is the shape every data change (world.usa, world.nations, world.orgs-cradles-geography, world.cities, world.states, world.districts) lands in. **There is no `people` array.** Every person a campaign meets — the starting cabinet shortlist included — is generated from the seed, the nation's name bank and the role profiles (§5b), and the twenty hand-named Americans the dataset once carried were read by nothing but the validator and their own test. A named person stored on a country is a person no campaign draws, so the field is gone rather than empty.

`packages/world/src/validate/dataset-validator.ts` gates it on these, all with test coverage in `dataset-validator.test.ts`:

1. **Shape** — zod parse; catches malformed data and out-of-range stats (e.g. `unemployment > 1`).
2. **Referential integrity** — every `NationId`/`OrgId`/`CharterId`/`CityId` reference (neighbors, alignment pulls, memberships, trade partners, relationship endpoints, org members, city `nation`) must resolve to a record that exists in the same dataset.
3. **Plausibility** — zod's own range constraints per field (§3–§5 above).
4. **Unit consistency** — the `gdpPerCapita`-vs-`gdp`/`population` check described in §3.
