After `INABILITY_SUSTAIN_DAYS` (91) under the line, and `INABILITY_RETRY_DAYS` (182) after a failure, the Vice President and a majority of the cabinet declare the President unable (Twenty-fifth Amendment, §4) and `countInability` takes two thirds of each chamber (`supermajorityOf` the seats) through `voteOn` with the same caucus break the grave-charge path uses, read off the President's own identifiers, and `INABILITY_APPEAL` (0.3) for members outside the caucus. A passing count sets `resolved` and `endingSummary` (`INABILITY_ENDING`); a failing one records `failedTurn` and the President resumes the office. No roll call has ever been taken under that section, so the line and both clocks are design judgment, labeled in source.

**`campaign.cities`** (`Record<CityId, CityRuntime>`) is live metro state, keyed by the dataset `CityId`. A `CityRuntime` is `{ base: City, employment, taxBase, taxHolidayTurns, adminLean?, demShare?, lastMovedTurn?, lastMovedReason? }`. `base` is the authored snapshot and does not move. `adminLean` is signed −1..+1 toward the sealed `caucusAnchor`, present on US cities and **omitted** (not stored as undefined) on foreign ones — how a city votes is a fact about the city, and the two tickets on its ballot are the same two whoever won the presidency. Opening lean is `2 * demShare - 1` if the anchor is Democrat else `1 - 2 * demShare`. Founding seeds the table from `worldDataset.cities` via `openingCities`.

`CityEffect` (`{ kind: "city", city, deltas?: { employment?, taxBase?, demShare?, adminLean? }, set?: { taxHolidayTurns? }, reason }`) is the only mutator. `applyCity` throws on an unknown city, clamps employment ≥ 0, taxBase ≥ 0, demShare 0..1, adminLean −1..1, taxHolidayTurns ≥ 0, writes last-moved, ignores foreign adminLean deltas, and rolls employment into that nation's `economy.unemployment` in the same handler (`laborForce = round(nationPopulation * 0.62)`, `unemployment' = clamp(unemployment - deltaJobs / laborForce, 0, 1)`). Player metrics are untouched. `cityPhase` runs after `conditionPhase` and before `capacityPhase`: tax holidays decrement by one week where remaining > 0 (narration names the city; no extra treasury drain), and US lean mean-reverts toward opening at `CITY_LEAN_DRIFT` (0.004) per turn, skipping a packet when the rounded delta is 0.

**`campaign.states`** and **`campaign.sectors`** carry state lean and sector mix, seeded from the dataset at founding by `openingStates` and `openingSectors`. `StateEffect` and `SectorEffect` are the only mutators. `statePhase` runs immediately after `cityPhase`. US onset of a condition prefers a named state (and a city in that state when scope is city). `NationCondition` may carry optional `city` / `state` / `sector` ids, omitted rather than undefined so JSON round-trips.

**`StateRuntime`** (`state-geography.ts`) carries four fields beyond `base` and `lean`: `delegation?: StateDelegation`, `marks: readonly GrievanceMark[]`, `claims: readonly PendingClaim[]`, `governor?: GovernorStanding`. `GrievanceMark` is `{ turn, signature, weight, valence: 1 | -1, decayRate, reason }` — the state's own memory, on the same shape as `Grudge`. `PendingClaim` is `{ signature, openedTurn, expiresTurn, weight, reason }`: a promise the state is owed an explicit answer to. **The obligation ledger (`obligations.ts`) is that enumerator.** `obligationPhase` opens one claim per condition per state it names on the turn it opens (never afterward: re-opening would reset its own deadline every week, so no claim could ever expire and the neglect could never be charged), signed `relief:<kind>`, weighted by severity, with a window inverted against severity. A nation-scope condition names no state at all, so it opens a claim against every state at `NATIONAL_CLAIM_SHARE` of local weight — without that the broadest emergencies in the game would have been the ones it was free to ignore. Answering removes the claim and writes the mirror-image favorable mark `answered:relief:<kind>`; ignoring lets `statePhase` write `omitted:relief:<kind>`.

`grievanceLean(marks, standing)` takes the administration's standing (`{ administrationParty, caucusAnchor }`) because a mark's valence is administration-relative while `lean` is partisan (Dem ← → Rep): it signs by the anchor and scales by reach, so a president with no party still earns credit and blame but banks less of it on an axis neither caucus moved along for them. Without it the function returned the Republican reading always, so under the default Democratic administration a state that had been abandoned drifted *toward* the president's own party: neglect was rewarded and relief punished on every Democratic run. `SenateSeat` is `{ party, seatClass: 1 | 2 | 3, disposition }`; `StateDelegation` is `{ house: ChamberComposition, senate: readonly [SenateSeat, SenateSeat], disposition }`; `GovernorStanding` is `{ person, party, disposition }`. Eviction bounds `MAX_STATE_MARKS` (8) and `MAX_STATE_CLAIMS` (3) live beside the shapes.

`openingStates` seeds `marks: []` and `claims: []` because nothing writes either at founding. `apportionDelegations` (`delegations.ts`) fills `delegation` at founding via `withApportionedDelegations` inside `sealFounding`, and again inside midterm `recompose`. `withSeatedGovernors` (`governors.ts`) stamps every non-DC state with a `GovernorStanding` from the seated `PersonRuntime` (`state` + `Person.party`), opening at the delegation's disposition; `selectGovernor` reads it, and `governorPhase` drifts toward the live delegation at `DELEGATION_DRIFT` (0.02/turn). **`routeDomestic`** (`domestic-signature.ts`) compiles a `DomesticSignature` into per-state `StateEffect`s (lean + `addMark`); **`partyReading` / `beliefSplit` / `selectPartyReading`** (`party-beliefs.ts`) turn the same signature into caucus disposition and opinion-polarization terms (no `partyBelief` scenario condition). Scenario replies author the same shape via content's `DomesticSignatureShape` and splice at cast; optional `domestic` on `ExecutiveOrder` and `LegislativeAct` catalogue entries splices `routeDomestic` (plus `beliefSplitEffects`) into `signOrder` and every `enactmentEffects` path (signed, desk-lapsed, overridden), so a defense-production priority and an infrastructure act leave the same kind of mark as a scenario reply. **`statePhase`** (`states.ts`) decays marks, expires unanswered `PendingClaim`s into `omitted:` marks (`weight × (0.5 + federalDependence × 0.5)`, `OMISSION_DECAY`), and drifts lean toward `base.lean + grievanceLean(marks)` — at most one packet per moved state.

**`apportionDelegations(congress, states)`** is the one writer of `StateDelegation`. House seats start from the seats as held — each state's live delegation, or at founding the Clerk's roster (`HOUSE_DELEGATION_TABLE`) — and `electHouse` (`house-seats.ts`) moves them to the chamber's party counts one seat at a time, closest seat first: a Republican gain takes the most Republican seat a Democrat holds anywhere, a loss gives up the least Republican seat a Republican holds, and Democrats then fill from the most Democratic open seat. Every seat is on the ballot, so the roster's vacancies are filled at the founding election and whatever neither party holds is the chamber's independents. A reseat with unchanged counts moves nothing, so delegations change only through elections, and `Σ delegation.house.{administration,opposition,independent} === congress.chambers.house.{…}` exactly. The share-of-lean split this replaced tied sixteen even delegations exactly in half (Idaho 1-1, Texas 19-19). Senate: two seats per state except DC (excluded from the chamber sum); classes from a fixed table; parties by the same remainder pass against the senate composition. The invariant is tested at founding and after recompose.

**Delegation disposition.** `delegationTarget` is `congress.disposition + CAUCUS_TILT_STATE × partyAlignment(lean) + GRIEVANCE_WEIGHT × grievanceReading(marks) + BELIEF_WEIGHT × beliefReading` (belief defaults to 0 until a caller passes a `partyReading`). `delegationPhase` drifts every apportioned bloc at `DELEGATION_DRIFT` (0.02/turn) after `congressPhase`. `chamberDisposition(congress, chamber, states?)` adds a third displacement term — `DELEGATION_LEVERAGE × seatWeightedMean(disposition − room)` — never written back into `congress.disposition`. With `states` omitted or every `delegation` absent, the term is exactly zero and `countVote` matches pre-delegation counts byte-for-byte. Live projections pass `campaign.states`.

**`selectGovernor(campaign, state)`** returns the seated `GovernorStanding` (absent for DC). Founding seats one for every other state; disposition drifts weekly toward `delegation.disposition` on the same `DELEGATION_DRIFT` terms (sibling of `delegationPhase` until T21 composes them).

US city lean **change since founding** is a `projectVote` driver beside the bloc ballot, not a replacement for it: `delta = (currentPopWeightedLean - openingPopWeightedLean) * CITY_VOTE_LEVERAGE` with leverage 0.25. State lean change is a second driver (`how the states have moved`) weighted by electoral votes. Opening lean is recomputed from dataset `demShare` and the sealed ticket — no second snapshot is stored. A zero driver is omitted so an unmoved map does not silently reweight the term-balance gates. Charters that hold no elections skip it. God mode `editCity` returns a `CityEffect` packet through `apply()`.

**`campaign.conflicts`** (`Record<conflictId, Conflict>`) is the wars the board is carrying. A `Conflict` is `{ id, aggressor, defender, joined: { aggressor[], defender[] }, startedTurn, cause, intensity, warSupport: { aggressor, defender }, exhaustion: { aggressor, defender }, status, beats, endedTurn?, opening? }`, keyed `war:<aggressor>:<defender>:<startedTurn>`; `status` is `"active" | "aggressor-victory" | "defender-victory" | "settled"`. `opening?: "blockade" | "retaliation"` names the act that opened the war where it changes how the war is read: a blockade (military rung 3, an act of war the first time), or a target answering a failed act of force with its own (the `escalation` failure branch). The aggressor of a war with an `opening` gets no rally (`opinion-war.ts#ralliesTo`); absent on every war opened the older ways. Ended wars are kept rather than deleted — a country's record of what it has done is what the world's opinion of it is built from, and `worldOpinionEffects` counts prior wars when it prices a repeat aggressor's standing.

**`campaign.emergencies`** (`Record<emergencyId, DeclaredEmergency>`) is what this administration has actually *declared*. A `DeclaredEmergency` is `{ id, scope, cause, declaredTurn, renewedTurn, endedTurn?, reason }`, keyed `emergency:<nation|stateId>:<cause>:<turn>`, with `EmergencyScope = { kind: "nation" } | { kind: "state", state }`. The shapes live in `state-emergency.ts` beside `state-condition.ts`; the rules live in `emergency.ts`. A declaration may only be continued in the ninety days before its anniversary (`EMERGENCY_NOTICE_TURNS`, 13), which is why keeping one is a public act with a date on it rather than a switch. `declareEmergency`, `endEmergency` and `renewEmergency` each return an `EffectPacket`, not loose effects, so the sentence the country reads is the engine's.

This register replaced an ambient boolean. `inEmergency` in `orders.ts` used to return true whenever *any* condition was running anywhere in the player's nation, so the orders gated on `requires.needsEmergency` (at the time detention without charge, a reporting blackout and rule by decree, all since deleted as powers no US statute grants) unlocked the moment the weather turned, with no act of the presidency behind them, no scope, no cost, and nothing to give back. Two orders gate on it today, both statutes that run only under a declaration: `order-asset-seizure` (IEEPA blocking, 50 U.S.C. §1702 under §1701) and `order-emergency-construction` (10 U.S.C. §2808). `orderAvailability` refuses them with `EMERGENCY_MISSING`, which names the National Emergencies Act. They now require a declaration that reaches the state in question, or a war. A declaration lapses `EMERGENCY_RENEWAL_TURNS` (52) after its last renewal and bills a compounding standing cost every week it stands, so declaring is cheap and *keeping* one is what costs.

**`campaign.conditions`** (`Record<conditionId, NationCondition>`) is what countries are living through. A `NationCondition` is `{ id, nation, kind, scope, severity, startedTurn, status, beats, endedTurn? }`, keyed `condition:<kind>:<nation>:<startedTurn>`. `kind` is `DisasterKind | "unrest"` — the authored `geo.disasterExposure` kinds plus the one a country generates for itself — and `scope` is `"city" | "region" | "nation"`, which scales every per-turn impact. Onset is read from `geo.disasterExposure`, which is what makes those six authored fields mechanical rather than decorative. An active condition stands a flag, `condition:<kind>:<nation>`, which authored content gates on. That flag stays until the last condition of that kind in the nation ends. A second acute disaster in another city or state also stands `condition:<kind>:<nation>:<placeId>`, and ending one place clears only that place flag.

**Home incidents and per-place keying (save v53).** `DisasterKind` gains `"tornado"`, `"winter-storm"` and `"heat-wave"` — the three hazards the United States declares against most often, which the first six could not name — and `geo.disasterExposure` gains `tornado` (0.6), `winterStorm` (0.11) and `heatWave` (0.1) beside them, each with a `stat-consumers.ts` row reading `conditions.onsetCandidates`; the United States now carries a nine-key exposure row and is the only nation with any weight on the three (weights read off the federal declaration record — OpenFEMA `FemaWebDisasterDeclarations` v1, queried 2026-09-23 — with wildfire held above its major-disaster share because the federal fire response runs through fire management grants, and earthquake, drought and heat wave at a floor because the record does not name them in quantity). `dataset/state-hazards.ts`'s eligibility lists for all three are sourced rather than provisional: tornado carries the severe-storm and tornado declarations together (broadest of the three, naming the record's most-declared states — Mississippi, Kentucky, Tennessee, Oklahoma, Kansas — and excluding only where the terrain or the season does not make them); winter storm reads snowstorm and ice storm together, the snow belt, the northern plains, the Northeast and the Southern states an ice storm shuts down precisely because they are not built for one; heat wave's list is inferred from climate rather than read off the declaration record, and the dataset's own comment says so, since the record does not declare heat waves in the quantity the other two hazards are declared at.

`NationCondition` gains two optional fields. `lifetime?: "incident"` marks a record that is not a state the country lives through for weeks: a tornado touches down, the damage is the damage, and nothing about next week is a worsening of it — one reader, one store and one id scheme for both, rather than a second kind of record. It is written by `openCondition` (`condition-onset.ts`) at onset for the kinds in `condition-kinds.ts#INCIDENT_KINDS` (tornado, heat wave). `place?: string` is the per-place key two simultaneous records of one kind in one country need, written whenever the draw names a city or a state; without a place the id is `condition:<kind>:<nation>:<turn>`, exactly what every save already carries, and with one `conditionId` appends it — `condition:<kind>:<nation>:<turn>:<place>` — so two tornadoes on one afternoon in two states never collide. Absent on every condition written before v53, which keeps the old id exactly.

**`campaign.pursuits`** (`Record<pursuitId, Pursuit>`) is one nation working on another across weeks — the pressure that comes before a war, and that most of the time never becomes one. A `Pursuit` is `{ id, actor, target, aim, openedTurn, commitment, progress, rungReached, overt, lastActedTurn, status, beats, closedTurn? }`, keyed `pursuit:<actor>:<target>:<openedTurn>`; `status` is `"active" | "achieved" | "abandoned"` and `aim` is an `AgendaTheme` taken from the actor's own heaviest rolled agenda. At most one is active per **ordered** pair: a state pressing a neighbor over two grievances is running one campaign, and modeling it as two would let the same commitment be spent twice. `commitment` is 0..1 (what the actor has put in), `progress` is -1..1 (toward the aim or away from it), and `rungReached` never falls — what has been done has been done. `overt` is false only while every move in it has been covert, which is what keeps a campaign nobody has run in the open out of the feed. Abandoned campaigns are kept for the same reason ended wars are: what a state tried and gave up on is part of how the next one reads it.

All three are plain data here and moved only by their phases (`conflicts.ts`, `conditions.ts`, `pursuits.ts`) through the `conflict`, `condition` and `pursuit` effect kinds. A `Conflict` has two further movers and no further shape. `joinConflict(campaign, joiner, conflictId, side)` (`war-entry.ts`, re-exported by `conflicts.ts`) appends a deliberate joiner to `joined[side]` and returns an ordinary `ConflictEffect` inside a packet — so a war entered on purpose stores exactly as one the weekly ally draw pulled a nation into, and `selectJoinableConflicts` is the read side a surface offers it from. The President's own join goes through the same War Powers statute check a military rung does: where the office's own authority does not reach the war it is refused with the reason, and where it does, the packet carries the same War Powers record a strike writes (`recordForceIntroduction`, the report owed within forty-eight hours, plus `recordUnilateralForce` where nothing authorizes the war). And: an authored `conflictMove` on a scenario reply (§7c) resolves through `conflicts.ts`'s `settleByAgreement` / `deescalateConflict` and reaches state as the same `ConflictEffect`, so a war ended at a table stores exactly as one ended by exhaustion. `offerTerms` writes no conflict at all — it stands the flag `terms-offered:<conflictId>` in the same `player.flags` record every other governance memory uses, so no save shape changes.

**`campaign.governments`** (`Record<NationId, PolicySet>`) is the standing program of every nation the player does not govern — the same `PolicyDomain -> stanceId` map `player.policies` holds, out of the same catalogue. The player's own stays where it was because it is a different kind of fact: charter-gated, drafted, priced and enacted by a person. `selectGovernment(campaign, nation)` (`governance.ts`) is the one read path across both, so nothing downstream has to know which of the two it is asking about, and the player's own nation is deliberately absent from the record rather than duplicated into it. `PolicyEffect` gains an optional `nation`: omitted (or the player's own) writes `player.policies`, any other nation writes that government's program.

**`campaign.orders`** (`ExecutiveOrderRecord[]`, append-only oldest first) records signed executive orders. Each record contains `{ id, order, turn, contested, challengeChance }`; the record ID distinguishes later intentional reissuance from the original signature. The stored challenge chance describes the signing-time assessment rather than today's board. Optional typed subjects identify the actual emergency, state, appropriation, rule, site, entry class or planning area; contracts and notices carry their own identified records. Optional `framing` preserves a dated public explanation. A contested order's `review` stores its due turn, pending/upheld/struck-down status and eventual ruling turn. Detailed proceedings and opinions live in `judiciary`. Final adverse relief removes only supported continuing contributions via `orderRemedy` and the affected implementation readers; it does not negate every original national delta, refund sunk spending or erase debt. A rescission/expiry ends prospective authority while preserving signature and litigation history. See the institutional-subject, contribution-lineage and prospective-authority contracts below for exact scope and legacy handling.

**`campaign.pendingRules`** (`readonly PendingRule[]`, `state-orders.ts`) is the rulemaking proceedings a rule-shaped executive order has set running and where each has got to. `PendingRule = { id, order, signedTurn, effectiveTurn, status, resolvedTurn? }`, `id` keyed to the signing order record's own id (so a rule and its record are the same entry looked up two ways) and `status` a closed `"pending" | "in-effect" | "canceled" | "disapproved"`. An `ExecutiveOrder` may declare `viaRulemaking: true` — the substance is a rule an agency has to write rather than a directive that changes the country the moment it is signed — and `signOrder` withholds that order's `nationEffects` from the signing packet, appending a `PendingRule` with `effectiveTurn = campaign.turn + RULEMAKING_TURNS` (`RULEMAKING_TURNS` equals `JUDICIAL_REVIEW_TURNS`, so the bench and the agency come back at comparable times) instead. `rulemakingPhase` applies the effects the week the proceeding finishes, once, and runs **after** `judicialReviewPhase` in the turn sequence, so a rule the bench strikes down before it is final is marked `canceled` rather than negated — there is nothing in the country yet to put back. Everything else about the signature (metric cost, alignment pull, the domestic signature, the deed flag, the challenge roll) lands at signature regardless of whether the rule ever finishes.

The Congressional Review Act (`act-rule-disapproval`, `legislation.ts`) is a bill, not a second mechanism: a `LegislativeAct` whose optional `targetsOrder(campaign)` resolves the rule Congress is moving against (stamped onto `Bill.target` at origination) and whose optional `disapproves: true` marks the act as one, both read only by this act. It arises while a `PendingRule` is `in-effect` inside `DISAPPROVAL_WINDOW_TURNS` (9 — sixty days of session) and not already the subject of a live resolution. Enacted, `rulemakingPhase` negates the target order's `nationEffects` and marks the proceeding `disapproved`, matched from the enacted law rather than from the signing packet so it holds across all four ways a bill becomes one; `lawConstraints` also merges `bill.target` into `blocksOrders`, so `orderAvailability` refuses reissuing a rule Congress has disapproved.

**`campaign.capRaises`** (`readonly AccountCapRaise[]`, `state-legislature.ts`) is every ceiling an act of Congress has lifted on what the appropriators may commit, spent ones kept rather than deleted (what an administration was authorized to buy and never bought is part of its record). `AccountCapRaise = { id, account, share, enactedTurn, throughTurn }`, `id` the bill that enacted it, `share` an annual share of GDP added to the standing per-account ceiling, `throughTurn` the expiry. `LegislativeAct` carries an optional `raisesCap?: { account, share, turns }`; `enactmentEffects` writes the `AccountCapRaise` from it (`throughTurn = campaign.turn + raisesCap.turns`) on every path that makes the act law. `accountCap(campaign, account)` (`budget.ts`) is `ACCOUNT_CAP` (the standing 4%-of-GDP ceiling) plus every live raise for that account, and it is what `selectBudget`, `budgetLine` and `projectBudgetDraft` clamp against on read rather than only on the way in — so an account funded above the standing cap under an authorization falls back to the standing cap the week that authorization lapses, with nobody re-voting anything. An authorization permits; it does not pay: `act-defense-authorization` costs nothing and delivers nothing directly on signature, only the room to appropriate into, and readiness arrives only if the money is then voted through `requestAppropriation`.

**`campaign.lapses`** (`readonly FundingLapse[]`, `state-legislature.ts`) is every time appropriations actually ran out and the government closed, oldest first. `FundingLapse = { id, begunTurn, endedTurn?, endedBy? }`, `id` keyed `lapse:<turn>`, `endedBy` a closed `"continuing-resolution" | "appropriation"`. **Whether the government is closed right now is deliberately not stored.** `unfundedTurns(campaign)` (`funding.ts`) is `max(0, turn - congress.appropriation.throughTurn)` and `inShutdown` is that count past `SHUTDOWN_GRACE_TURNS` (2) — so a stopgap that moves the date reopens the government on every surface in the same instant, with nothing to keep in step. What the record carries is the history, which is the part a passed continuing resolution would otherwise erase from the state entirely.

The continuing resolution is a bill, not a second mechanism (`act-continuing-resolution`, `legislative-acts.ts`). `LegislativeAct` gains two optional fields, both read only by it: `funds?: { turns } | { throughFiscalYear: true }`, which `enactmentEffects` turns into an `appropriation` effect on every path that makes the act law (`{ fundTurns }` for a span, `{ throughTurn }` for the end of the budget year, `selectFiscalYearEndTurn(selectBudgetYear(c))`; `throughTurn` never shortens, and either one sets `onPriorYear` true and empties `priorYear`, because a stopgap continues last year's appropriation and can now pass before the book it extends has run out), and `urgent?: true`, which lets `originate` take the act up the week it becomes eligible instead of at the drawn session gap — a Congress that waited eleven weeks to move a stopgap could not end a shutdown inside a season. It arises when the book in force, whatever it is, is within two weeks of running out or already has (`fundingRunningOut`: `turn + 2 >= throughTurn`). An enacted book for the coming year moves `throughTurn` past that window, so a book this close to its end has nothing behind it, and Congress offers the stopgap before September 30 rather than after the government has closed. It rides the reconciliation track (continuing an appropriation is spending), appropriates nothing (`cost: {}`, no `treasuryOfGdp`, no `raisesCap`), and is subject to the ordinary floor count, the desk clock, the veto and the override like every other act — so a stopgap the floor cannot carry never reaches the desk, and the lapse is the consequence of a failed vote rather than of a calendar.

Two new effect kinds carry it, both in `record-effects.ts`. `{ kind: "fundingLapse", set }` appends or amends the record. `{ kind: "appropriation", fundTurns }` patches `congress.appropriation.throughTurn` to `max(throughTurn, state.turn + fundTurns)` at fold time — a patch rather than a whole-`Congress` write because a bill's packet already carries a `congress` effect for the disposition, and it is emitted *before* the enactment for the same reason: folded the other way round, a stale appropriation would land back over the stopgap that had just been signed. `onPriorYear` is untouched, which is the point — six weeks of government at last year's numbers is still last year's numbers, and `congressPhase`'s prior-year friction goes on being charged.

**What a lapse costs.** `shutdownPhase` (`shutdown.ts`, after `legislationPhase`) is the one writer of `lapses`: it opens the record the week `inShutdown` first holds, charges `LAPSE_STABILITY_COST` (0.04 a week — ten times `congressPhase`'s prior-year friction of 0.004, which is the actual ratio between a government that cannot plan and a government that has stopped) every week it runs, and closes the record naming who reopened it. Delivery collapses through the dial the institutions already carried and nothing ever moved: `lapsedBudgetAdequacy` (`funding.ts`) walks `InstitutionRuntime.budgetAdequacy` down by 0.12 a week toward a floor of 0.2, recovering at 0.06 toward the settled 0.8 once the money arrives, and `institutionTick` applies it — so the collapse reaches the player through `deriveImplementationQuality`'s existing `BUDGET_WEIGHT` rather than through a multiplier bolted onto it. A body that is not `independence: "executive"` takes half the fall and stops at a floor half the distance down: the same halving independence already buys in both directions, and not an exemption.

**The domestic line, and a budget Congress votes line by line.** `BUDGET_ACCOUNTS` is now five: the four statecraft accounts — still declared separately as `STATECRAFT_ACCOUNTS` and still `satisfies readonly InstrumentId[]`, because a statecraft account is always an instrument — plus `domestic`, which is not one and never will be, since nothing on any escalation ladder gates on it. It is there because a budget of four coercive accounts was not a budget: a president who could only move money between the foreign service and the navy was never making the choice the office actually makes, and a carrier was paid for out of nothing. `BUDGET_ACCOUNT_SPECS.domestic` declares no stat targets and `gain: 0`, on the same shape the sovereign fund already had, because what the domestic line buys does not live on the nation.

What it buys lives on the institutions, and the join is the dial the shutdown slice already moved. `accountLevel(runtime, account, branchAdequacy?)` returns `branchAdequacy` for the domestic line — `branchBudgetAdequacy(campaign)`, the mean of `InstitutionRuntime.budgetAdequacy` across the executive branch — so the account is priced against the delivery it is actually holding up. `settledBudgetAdequacy(campaign)` turns the domestic net share into the level a body settles at (`FUNDED_BUDGET_ADEQUACY + netShare × DOMESTIC_ADEQUACY_GAIN`, floored at `LAPSE_BUDGET_FLOOR` and capped at 1), `institutionPhase` reads it once for the whole branch, and `lapsedBudgetAdequacy` takes it as a fourth argument and walks each body toward it. `institutionTick` stays the one writer of `budgetAdequacy`; no new effect kind and no second path. The loop through the sustainment line converges by construction (`DOMESTIC_SUSTAIN_AT_FULL × DOMESTIC_ADEQUACY_GAIN` is well under 1) to a legible set of fixed points: nothing appropriated settles the branch at 0.5, sustainment holds it at the 0.8 it opens on, the account cap takes it to 1. Money arriving on time still never lowers a body — only an appropriation that no longer pays for it does, which is the one case that walks downward.

Where the cap arithmetic lives moved with it. `ACCOUNT_CAP` and `accountCap` are declared in `funding.ts` and re-exported unchanged from `budget.ts`, because `institutions.ts` needs the domestic reading and `budget.ts` reaches `institutions.ts` transitively — a module graph that let `institutions.ts` import `budget.ts` would close that cycle, and `funding.ts` still imports no value at all.

**`Appropriation.priorYear` and the two scopes of a failed budget.** `projectAppropriation(campaign, candidate?)` now returns `accounts` (one `AccountVote` per account the request actually *changes* — an account nobody proposed to move is already law and is not put back on the floor), `refused`, `wouldPass` (nothing the President asked for was struck out) and `wouldEnact` (a book passes at all). Each account is counted at its own appeal: a fixed disposition per line plus a resistance term proportional to the raise requested, on the reconciliation track (`floorThreshold(congress, chamber, "reconciliation")`), which is a majority in both chambers because a budget's substance is spending. `votes` is unchanged — the whole-book count at no appeal — and with no candidate passed, `wouldPass` still means exactly what it meant, so every existing reader keeps its meaning.

`requestAppropriation` therefore has three outcomes, and `Appropriation` keeps them apart. `enactedTurn`, `throughTurn` and `onPriorYear` are facts about the **book**; `priorYear` is a fact about the **lines inside a book that passed**. Every line carries: a new book, clock reset, `priorYear` empty. Some lines carry: a book still passed, so `throughTurn` still moves and `onPriorYear` is false — a struck-out line is not a funding lapse — and the refused accounts go into `priorYear`, left at the rate they were already running at (`carriedOnly` re-projects the request through `projectBudgetDraft`, so `enactBudget` never writes a share the floor refused). Nothing carries: no book at all, `onPriorYear` true, `throughTurn` unmoved, `priorYear` empty because there is no book for a line to be missing from. Only `throughTurn` reaches the shutdown, which is what keeps the lapse about the whole funding running out rather than about one department's number.

The vote itself moved out of `congress.ts` into `appropriation.ts` on the seam `legislative-acts.ts` was split along, and the dependency runs one way: `appropriation.ts` reads `congress.ts` for the count and `budget-draft.ts` for the allocation, and neither reads it back.

**`ActionRecord`** (`campaign.ledger`) is `{ turn, actor, description, cost?, commitment?, counsel?, mood?, toll?, institutional?, assessment?, causal?, background? }`. `actor` is an `EffectActor` — `"player" | "world" | "developer"` — and it answers a question the record could not previously answer at all: which of these entries were decisions this administration took, as against a neighbor acting, an operator pressing its host, or the month's drift. It is carried on the `EffectPacket` and copied onto the entry by `apply()`, because only the caller knows: a conversation reply and an ops commit come from different subsystems and are both the player, while an NPC's action and the player's own run through the identical resolver. Both resolvers therefore derive it from the acting nation (`actorForNation`, `state.ts`) rather than from the packet's `source`, which names a subsystem and not an actor. `selectPlayerLedger` is the companion to `selectLedger`. `background?: true` (v52) marks the world's weekly bookkeeping: a phase that writes accruals, apportionments or drift sets `EffectPacket.background` on its own packet (`budgetPhase`, `capacityPhase`, `opinionPhase`, `macroDriftPhase`, `fiscalPhase`'s accrual and `orderSettling`), and `apply()` copies it onto the entry like `actor`. The entry keeps its place in the causal graph and its id, and changes nothing the simulation computes; the Record leaves it out (`player-core`'s `foregroundLedger`). A foreground packet from the same phase — a rating action, an X-date, a prioritization week — carries no mark and stays. Absent means an act.

`commitment` (`{ instrument, rung, target, landed? }`) and `counsel` (`{ role, person, stance, successChance?, room? }`) are the entry's own statement of what it committed to and what the room said about it, on the same contract as `actor`: carried on the `EffectPacket` by the path that knows them — `resolveAction`, `attemptInstrument`, `commitReply` — and copied onto the entry by `apply()`, never written by a second path. Before them, `ledger-reading.ts` recovered a commitment by parsing the narrating sentence, which meant an entry an authored consequence card had dressed carried no instrument, no rung and no outcome and was dropped: the record got blinder the more the aftermath was written, and both derivations built on it (`revealed-preference.ts`, `counsel-record.ts`) degraded silently. `landed` is optional because the ladder's own path applies a rung outright rather than rolling for it, so there is no outcome to state; `counsel` deliberately carries no `landed` of its own, because the same entry's `commitment` already does and a duplicate could only disagree with itself. `room: readonly ActionCounselVoice[]` (`{ role, person, stance }`) is optional so an existing save reloads without it, and it is additive: every chair the player was actually shown for that decision is stamped, not only the primary, so `counsel.role`/`.person`/`.stance`/`.successChance` stay the primary voice — the same as `room[0]` — rather than a second, independently-written copy. `institutional?: readonly string[]` travels the same way: the authored institutional tags a position reply declared (`ScenarioImpact.institutional`) ride the position commitment and the `EffectPacket` as opaque strings, so the engine carries no content enum, and `apply()` copies them onto the entry for the After-Action line to read through the player layer's one vocabulary for the eight tags. Every earlier one exists because an absent field would be read as a claim the save never made; a v13 entry without these is not making a false claim, it is an entry whose evidence has to be read out of its narration, which is exactly what v13 did and what `ledger-reading.ts` still does where the fields are absent. Nothing may be backfilled onto an entry that carries neither: inventing a commitment is worse than the absence of one, so both derivations fall back to reading the narrating sentence. `assessment?: PositionAssessment` (`position-assessment.ts`: `{ spent, assessed, confidence, basis, asOf }`) travels the same way for a spoken position: `positionPacket` freezes the assessment the card printed — the spent metrics as figures, the assessed metrics (stability, standing, approval) as `ProjectionImpact` bands with a `Confidence` and the one sentence they rest on — and `apply()` copies it onto the entry, so the Record can set what was assessed beside what the week measured in `cost`. The assessed half is realized, not rolled: `realizePositionCost` amplifies a debit by the administration's fragility (`amplifiedCost`) and damps a credit by the public's polarization, deterministically, which is what makes the band an assessment and not a probability.

**`campaign.judiciary`** (`state-judiciary.ts`) is `{ cases: CourtCase[], proceedings?: CourtProceeding[] }`. Completed `CourtCase` records retain the order, question, ground, dates, holding, named votes and fixed opinion. Optional `procedure` stores the deciding forum, lower holding, appeal disposition, threshold dismissal and chronological history. Old completed opinions are preserved rather than recalculated against today's bench.

A `CourtProceeding` has an occurrence ID, typed executive-order subject (record and catalog IDs), filing turn, stage, next turn, history, optional interim relief, lower judgment, `governmentReview?: "seek" | "seek-with-stay" | "accept" | "defaulted"`, and appeal disposition. Stages are threshold, interim, lower merits, appeal, Supreme merits and final. The `courtProceeding` effect is the sole writer. An adverse lower judgment records `enforcement: "in-force"`, suspends the challenged order's prospective operation, and raises a hard seven-day `court-response` case. Acceptance or default finalizes the lower remedy once. Seeking review alone does not stay the judgment. A separate `stayApplication?` records pending/granted/denied status, request/decision turns and four frozen factors (merits, irreparable harm, harm to others, public interest), with a modeled grant chance and eventual seeded draw. These scores are audit state, not ordinary player knowledge. A later court phase decides the application before discretionary review. A granted stay records `stayed-pending-appeal`; independent limits, funding, rulemaking dates and ended authority still apply. Prior contracts, delivered goods, spending, awards and refusals remain historical. Optional fields preserve older records without inventing a past request or decision.

Authored `pendingOrderCase` can require `posture: "adverse-lower"` and `ruleState: "unimplemented" | "operative"`. The frozen cast pins the appeal-stage adverse lower judgment, its signed order and present rule state; `orderReview` reply commands carry accept, seek, or seek-with-stay into the same engine `court-response` case. The authored case inherits that case's hard urgency and due date and does not add a later response window. Deferral mirrors one return date onto both cases before the original deadline. Suppressing the duplicate Desk row leaves its engine case live. Acceptance, hand-off or timed default closes the shared obligation without an additional authored silence charge. Older frozen order-case snapshots lacking a posture keep their pending-challenge meaning. `judicialVacancy` may require a `chief` or `associate` Supreme Court seat; no lower-court seat is modeled by this gate.

Supreme Court votes are individual threshold decisions under one shared case uncertainty draw; no aggregate holding allocates the votes afterward. Vacant and dead seats are excluded, and a justice recorded as the government legal reviewer of the challenged order is recused even when nomination history is unavailable. Fewer than six participating justices yields no merits disposition. Equal division preserves the recorded lower holding without a majority opinion. Threshold dismissal records that no merits judgment was reached. Other recusal grounds and broader dispute families remain pending. Legacy saves may lack proceedings; loading preserves that absence and pending order reviews acquire prospective proceedings on their next court phase, without invented prior lower judgments.

**Substantive bill text.** `Bill.text?` stores proposed, House, Senate, conference and enrolled `BillText` (`base | targeted`) plus initial chamber vote history. `HousingText` remains a compatibility alias. Missing text preserves the original bill. Housing supply, health coverage and clean-grid bills have authored narrower alternatives that change beneficiaries, provisions, fiscal flows and support. Substantive disagreement enters the `conference` stage; identical text must pass both chambers before presentment. `proposeBillText` and `recommendBillConferenceText` record presidential proposals, never enactment. `actOf` and `lawOffsetForBill` read the enrolled version for enactment and continuing fiscal flows. Other bill families retain their existing markup behavior.

**`causal`** (`CausalStamp`, `causality.ts`) is the entry's place in the graph of what caused what. It is a `CausalProvenance` — `origin` (`decision | institution | world | scheduled | scenario`), plus optional `causeIds`, `effectiveDate`, `entities`, `locales`, `systems`, `confidence` (`observed | assessed | contested`) and `visibility` (`public | intelligence | internal`) — carried onto the `EffectPacket` as the optional `provenance`, and widened by the two fields only `apply()` is in a position to supply: a deterministic `eventId` and the `createdDate` the calendar stood at. Unlike every other optional field on the entry it is written on **every** append, including for a packet that supplied no provenance at all, because an event nothing can name is an event no later effect can answer to — a graph is only closed if the orphans have ids too. The id is derived from the packet's source, its turn and the ledger's length at the moment of the append, which is why it is stamped inside `apply()` rather than by the caller: that is the only scope that knows the length, and a stamp a caller could forget is a graph with holes in it. `origin` is not a second `actor`: `actor` says whose hand it was, `origin` says what kind of thing produced it, and "the President signed it", "the agency executed it" and "the calendar reached it" have to be told apart on a trail where the actor is identical. `effectiveDate` exists because a tariff is signed on one date and lands on shelves on another, and without it the trail collapses both into the day of the signature. An entry without a stamp is not making a false claim — it is an act nothing has yet been traced to — and backfilling one would invent a cause. The read side stores nothing further: `selectCausesOf`, `selectConsequencesOf`, `selectRootCausesOf`, `selectDecisionLegacy`, `selectWhyMetricMoved`, `selectLocaleCauses`, `selectInstitutionCauses` and `findOrphanedCauses` are pure walks of the one ledger.

**`causedBy` on the long-lived records, and why the stamp moved before the fold.** A stamped graph with no edges is a list, and for as long as `apply()` stamped *after* folding its effects that is what a played campaign produced: every event named, almost nothing answering to anything. The reason is structural rather than lazy. A call site cannot name a cause it has no id for, and the two halves of a real chain are written by different functions weeks apart — the tariff by `imposeTariff`, the partner's answer by `tradePhase`, which holds only a `RetaliationRecord`. `apply()` now computes the stamp **before** `foldEffects` (it only ever needed the turn, the calendar and the ledger's length, all known on entry) and opens a fold around the reduction: `enterFold(eventId)` sets a module-scoped id in `causality.ts` and returns its own closer, and `currentEventId()` reads it. Handlers that create something the campaign will still be holding in ten weeks write that id onto the record as **`causedBy`** — `TradeMeasure`, `RetaliationRecord` (`trade.ts`), `Nomination` (`nomination.ts`), and `NationCondition`, `Pursuit`, `Commitment` and `DeclaredEmergency`, which get it in one place because `setById` (`record-effects.ts`) is the single writer for all four. The phase that acts on the record months later reads it back off and names it in its own `causeIds`. **`causedBy` is written only where the record is new**: an existing record inherits what it already had, including nothing, because stamping a record written by an older build with the week it next happened to change would claim a drought started in June because June is when it worsened — a fabricated cause, which is worse than an absent one. Measured on a played campaign, 39–48% of stamped events now name a cause; the graph is two levels rather than deep, because a condition's weekly notices all point at its onset, which is a star and the true shape of the fact.

**The cabinet is the government's fifteen departments, not a charter's five chairs.** `Charter.cabinetRoles` for the United States now seats the full table — State, Treasury, Defense, Justice, Interior, the nine remaining department secretaries, Homeland Security and the DNI — and `DEPARTMENT_SECRETARY_ROLES` (`world/dataset/people.ts`) carries the ten roles that were missing, each with its own attribute window, career route, portrait attire and bio bank so two secretaries are drawn as different kinds of person rather than as one profile under ten names. They are **appended after every existing role** in `ALL_ROLES`, the same rule `role-governor` follows, because the pool's name ledger walks roles in order and inserting them beside the ministers they sit next to would have reshuffled every cast ever seeded. `InstitutionDef.cabinetRole` joins each seat to its department and `seatCabinetOffice` writes both records at once (see `ARCHITECTURE.md`). **No save version move**: the join is derived from the catalog and the seating writer, and a v33 campaign reloads with the seats its charter gave it. Two consequences were measured rather than assumed. A department secretary chairs no legislable domain — only six domains are legislable and five roles chair them, which is true of a real cabinet — so the roster reads their delivery off their department's `implementationQuality` instead, on its own scale rather than through the program multiplier that would render a perfectly ordinary 75% department as "−25% delivery". And the opening position's *generic* administrative push is now scaled by cabinet size (`REFERENCE_CABINET_SEATS`, `opening-position.ts`): summed unnormalized across sixteen chairs rather than six it put opening stability at 1.000, a reading with no room above it — the same defect that file already records having fixed once for standing, arriving from the cabinet's side.

**The chairs a scenario may speak as, and what each one is worth at founding.** Three vocabularies were fixed at five roles when the cabinet was five and stayed there when it became sixteen, which left ten departments modeled but mute. `ScenarioRoleHint` (`content/src/scenario-speaker.ts`) is now one hint per department plus `intelligence` and `lead` — seventeen, resolved per charter through `ROLE_HINTS` rather than by id, so a charter that seats no agriculture minister resolves that hint to nothing and the beat falls to the narrator instead of inventing an official nobody seated. `scenario-role-hints.test.ts` walks the vocabulary against the playable charter and fails on any seat with no hint, any hint with no seat, and any two hints landing on one chair — two ways to write the same speaker is exactly the comparability a closed vocabulary exists to protect. `PORTFOLIOS` and `PORTFOLIO_REACH` (`engine/src/opening-position.ts`) now declare what each department's secretary is worth on the reading that department actually moves: Commerce reaches the treasury and standing, Energy and Veterans reach readiness, the rest reach stability, and a disposition still only travels through the ministry the person holds. And `CONSULTATION` (`engine/src/instruments.ts`) puts the department in the room from the rung its authority is engaged — Commerce on an export control, the Attorney General on a covert finding, Veterans before forces are committed, Health and Homeland Security on relief that is actually delivered by them.

**Which chair answers for a policy domain is a fact about the constitution, not a constant.** `PolicyDomainSpec.chair` was one hardcoded `RoleId` per domain, which asserts that one constitution's answer is every constitution's. It is not — civil liberties are the Attorney General's brief under a presidential republic and the interior ministry's under a parliamentary one, and trade runs out of Commerce in Washington and out of the foreign ministry nearly everywhere else — and the way it was wrong was silent, because `legislableDomains` simply dropped a domain whose named chair a charter did not seat. It is now `chairHints`, resolved through `matchRole` against the charter's own seats by `resolveDomainChair(seats, domain)` (and `domainChair(campaign, domain)`), the same mechanism the instrument stewardship and the scenario speaker hints already use; order in a hint list is precedence, so the specific chair wins where a charter seats both. Founding resolves through the identical function against `charter.cabinetRoles`, because two resolvers would be two answers to "who signs this". Under the United States this moves industry and trade to Commerce and civil liberties to Justice; three charters that seat no intelligence chair now resolve `security` to their interior ministry and gain a policy domain they always should have had. `PolicyStanceProjection.chair` became optional as a result: a domain no seat answers for is a real reading rather than a missing field. `policy-chairs.test.ts` pins the resolution for every charter in the dataset and fails on any domain resolved to a chair the charter does not seat. All seventeen balance and term-balance assertions and the thirteen-player population gate hold unchanged.

**`campaign.institutions`** (`Readonly<Record<InstitutionId, InstitutionRuntime>>`) is the executive branch, and it is what replaced modeling the federal government as seven charter seats. The static half is `INSTITUTION_CATALOG` (`institution-catalog.ts`), a data table of the real structure: four Tier 0 bodies (the presidency and the EOP, Congress, the federal judiciary, the states), all fifteen executive departments plus the NSC, OMB, USTR, EPA, ODNI, CIA, FBI, the Federal Reserve and eight independent regulators at Tier 1, thirty-odd operating components at Tier 2 (CBP, ICE, TSA, the Secret Service, FEMA, CISA, CDC, FDA, CMS, NIH, BIS, NOAA, OFAC, IRS, DEA, ATF, the Marshals, NSA, DIA, NGA, NRO, INR, Treasury OIA, DHS I&A, NCTC, NCSC, the three military departments and the Joint Chiefs), and the aggregate career service at Tier 3. An `InstitutionDef` carries `mandate`, `jurisdictions`, `authorities`, `independence`, `offices`, `capabilities`, `domains`, `foundingCapacity` and `staffScale`; the catalog holds real institutions and never a real officeholder, because the people are the campaign's own cast. Tier 1 also carries nine single-office institutions for the Supreme Court (`INST-SCOTUS-SEAT-1`..`INST-SCOTUS-SEAT-9`, seat 1 the Chief Justice, `institution-catalog.ts`'s `SCOTUS_SEAT_INSTITUTION_IDS`), each `independence: 'independent'` and `removal: 'term-protected'` — the only shape in this catalog that can hold nine distinct named occupants, since `InstitutionRuntime` carries one `leader` per institution. No new field or type: this is the same generic `Record<InstitutionId, InstitutionRuntime>` growth every other catalog addition already produces.

`independence` is the field the rest of the model turns on. `executive` takes direction; `semi-autonomous` takes direction but has a statutory mission that makes bending it cost something; `independent` does not take direction on its core function at all, and the President's only lever is the seat that opens. That is why `policy-rate` is an authority held by exactly one institution, why every office of an independent body carries a fixed `termYears` and a removal rule that is never `at-will`; the Federal Reserve's two offices differ, because 12 U.S.C. § 242 makes a governor removable for cause and writes no way at all to end the four-year chairmanship — and why `selectExecutiveCapacity` excludes independent bodies entirely — the Federal Reserve running well is not the administration executing well.

`InstitutionRuntime` is the live half: `leader`, `actingLeader`, `actingExpiresDay`, `vacancy`, `vacantDays`, `capacity`, `staffing`, `morale`, `integrity`, `politicization`, `budgetAdequacy`, `backlog`, `directives`, `implementationQuality` and `oversightPressure`. `directives` is a list of `DirectiveId`s into `campaign.directives`, not free text. `investigations` is gone: it was a second list of sentences describing what an agency was being looked into for, and what an agency is being looked into for has lived on `campaign.oversight` as an `OversightMatter` — with a stage, a clock, a direction and a public flag — since v40. Everything is 0..1 except `backlog`, which is an unbounded count of work owed, because a backlog with a ceiling is not a backlog. `implementationQuality` is **never written by an effect**: `applyInstitution` re-derives it from the other fields on every write, so a surface cannot claim a delivery the state does not support. At founding, career staff are present and the political offices are empty, which is the actual opening position of an American presidency and the reason the first months of a term are about appointments. The Supreme Court is the one exception seated at founding rather than left empty: see ARCHITECTURE.md's executive-orders section for how its nine seats are read, and its "branch below the cabinet" paragraph for how they are (for now) seeded.

**The departments' services** (`services.ts`, v57). A `ServiceDefinition` is `{ id, name, unit, portfolio, work, standardWeeks, shortcut? }`: the Secretary who answers for it (`portfolio`, a role), the `WorkDef` that names who does it (`executive-work.ts`), the age at which a case is late (4, 13 or 26 weeks), and, only where a late backlog tempts the building to cut a corner, the `ServiceShortcut`: `{ kind: "refusal", share }` (claims refused unweighed; `share` is what an ordinary week refuses on the merits) or `{ kind: "plea" }` (prosecutions closed by forced pleas; nothing is refused). `SERVICES` (`service-catalog.ts`) holds thirteen, one per department, each rolled up to a distinct department: `consular-visas` (State, owner `INST-STATE` under `visa`, standard 4), `tax-returns` (Treasury, owner `INST-IRS` under `tax-administration`, standard 4), `arms-transfers` (Defense, owner `INST-DOD` under `procurement`, with an `approval` edge to `INST-STATE` under `foreign-assistance`, standard 13), `disability-claims` (Veterans Affairs, owner `INST-VA` under `veteran-benefits`, standard 13, refusal share 0.3), `federal-cases` (Justice, owner `INST-DOJ` under `prosecution`, `approval` edge to `INST-JUDICIARY` under `adjudication`, standard 26, plea shortcut), `farm-payments` (Agriculture, owner `INST-USDA` under `farm-support`, standard 4, refusal share 0.1), `export-licenses` (Commerce, owner `INST-BIS` under `export-control`, standard 4), `wage-complaints` (Labor, owner `INST-LABOR` under `workplace-enforcement`, standard 13), `drug-applications` (Health and Human Services, owner `INST-FDA` under `regulatory-approval`, standard 26), `fair-housing-complaints` (Housing and Urban Development, owner `INST-HUD` under `fair-housing`, standard 13), `student-aid` (Education, owner `INST-ED` under `student-loans`, standard 4), `disaster-assistance` (Homeland Security, owner `INST-FEMA` under `disaster-recovery`, standard 4) and `drilling-permits` (Interior, owner `INST-INTERIOR` under `leasing`, standard 4). The Federal Judiciary's catalog authorities gain `adjudication` (its mandate already read "decide cases and controversies"), so the courts' stage is a lawful edge. A `ServiceRuntime` stores source quantities only: `{ id, openedTurn, pending, returning, last? }`. `pending` is a cohort array of `OLDEST_COHORT + 1` (27) entries, index = whole weeks waited, the last holding 26 weeks or more; `returning` is the rework pipeline (8 weeks, index 0 returns next week); `last` is the week's `ServiceWeek { arrivals, rework, decided, pressured, refused, errors, withdrawn }`. Quantities are in a common unit where a normal week's intake is `WEEKLY_INTAKE` (100); readers show them as weeks of intake and shares, never as real counts. Each week `serviceTick` ages every cohort by one, adds intake plus returning rework at age 0, decides oldest first up to `WEEKLY_INTAKE × CLEARANCE_PER_ARRIVAL × delivery.capacity` (the institution tick's own break-even), withdraws 2% of the 26-plus cohort, and books errors at `0.02 + 0.2 × (1 − ownerQuality)`, half of which return as rework after 8 weeks. Where the service has a shortcut and somebody runs the portfolio (`selectChairRunner`), backlog pressure (the late share above 0.2, scaled to 1) lets the Secretary push up to half a week's capacity again through the shortcut, scaled by `1 − integrity`; 60% of those are wrong. A refusal shortcut books them as refused as well; a plea shortcut does not. Identity held every week: prior pending + arrivals + rework − decided − withdrawn = next pending. Derived on read: `pendingTotal`, `ageBands` (under 4, 4 to 13, 13 to 26, 26 and over), `lateCases`, `lateShare`, `backlogPressure`. `serviceDepartment` rolls a service up to exactly one department (the owner, or its parent: the IRS rolls to Treasury). No rate is pooled across services: each is on its own intake scale, so a cross-service average would weigh them by an intake the engine does not know.

**The departments' grant programs** (`projects.ts`, v58). Project delivery, where a queue is the wrong model: a department picks projects, each clears a review another body owns, then is built. A `ProjectDefinition` is `{ id, name, unit, portfolio, work, awardEveryWeeks, reviewWeeks, buildWeeks }`; the `WorkDef`'s owner builds and its `approval` edge is the review. `PROJECTS` holds two: `highway-grants` (Transportation, owner `INST-DOT` under `infrastructure-grants`, review by `INST-EPA` under `permitting`; an award every 4 weeks, 26 weeks of review and 78 of construction) and `reactor-demonstrations` (Energy, owner `INST-DOE` under `research`, review by the independent `INST-NRC` under `nuclear-licensing`; an award every 26 weeks, 78 of review, 156 of construction). Stage weeks are at `PLAN_PACE` (0.7): a body delivering at 0.7 keeps to plan, faster finishes early, slower runs late. A `ProjectRuntime` stores `{ id, openedTurn, underway, completed, completedLate, last? }`; each `Project` is `{ awarded, review, build }` (shares 0..1; construction starts only when review is 1); `last` is the week's `ProjectWeek { awarded, readyAtAward?, reviewed, completed, completedLate }`. Each week `projectTick` advances every project one stage step (review at the slowest approving body's delivery, zero below `APPROVAL_FLOOR`; construction at the owner's delivery, support edges blended), then adds the award if one is due, arriving with `review = awardReadiness`: `READY_AT_AWARD_MAX` (0.6) × the competence of whoever runs the portfolio (`selectChairRunner`), 0 with nobody in the chair. Identity held every week: underway + awarded − completed = next underway. A project is late when the weeks since its award exceed `plannedWeeks` (review + build); late and stage counts are derived on read. `openingProjects` gives a program with no record one award per interval back across the plan, each where the plan would have it and none late; `openedTurn` marks where the record begins. `projectDepartment` rolls a program up to its owner's department. No dollar figure is carried; readers count projects.

**`campaign.directives`** (`Readonly<Record<DirectiveId, Directive>>`, optional) is what the President told a body to do and what the body did about being told. A `Directive` is `{ id, institution, kind, subject, issuedTurn, status, memorandum? }`. `kind` is one of `investigation`, `targeting`, `enforcement-posture`, `sanctions-tempo` or `monetary` — cut where the statutes are cut rather than by department, because directing the general posture of enforcement is lawful nearly everywhere and pointing that enforcement at a named party is barred nearly everywhere it matters. `status` is `carried`, `slow-walked` or `refused`.

Three rules run through `directives.ts`, and they are `intelligence.ts`'s rules generalized off one agency onto the rest of the government. **A refusal is a missing executor, not a difficulty check** — nothing rolls against a hidden number; the instruction has nobody who could lawfully carry it out and what comes back is a `RefusalMemorandum` carrying the `authority`, the statutory `reason` printed unaltered, and who signed it. **The asking is the event** — a barred instruction is not rejected by `issueDirective`, it succeeds, and what succeeds is the refusal, so a president cannot try every agency for free until one says yes. **The contrast is the lesson** — the same function that refuses the Federal Reserve carries the EPA.

`directiveRefusal(def, kind)` is deliberately **one function**, not one per family. A `STATUTORY_BARS` table names the law enforcement family (Justice, the Bureau, DEA, ATF, the Marshals — `investigation` and `targeting`, on one shared authority), the IRS (`investigation` and `targeting`, on the taxpayer-specific bar) and the Federal Reserve (every kind, on the Federal Reserve Act); anything else whose `independence` is `independent` gets a memorandum built from its own catalog entry — its office's `termYears` and its for-cause removal are what independence *is*, and eight hand-written commission memoranda would have been eight chances to say one thing eight ways. An instruction addressed to a body that holds no matching `capabilities` or `authorities` is rejected as addressed to nobody rather than refused as unlawful, which is a different and more honest answer.

A refusal costs the administration through channels that already exist and adds no new one. The directed body's `politicization` rises by `REFUSAL_POLITICIZATION_RISE` and its `oversightPressure` by `DIRECTIVE_OVERSIGHT_RISE` — politicization already drags `implementationQuality` and already opens inspector-general audits, so the consequence lands somewhere rather than on a counter of its own. The second refusal at one body (`REFUSALS_BEFORE_MATTER`) opens an `OversightMatter` with `direction: "received"` at that body's *inspected ancestor* — a bureau has no auditor of its own, so a file about the IRS is opened at Treasury — which reaches `selectOversightPressure`, and through it `congress.disposition` and the removal arc. A lawful directive is `slow-walked` rather than `carried` at a `semi-autonomous` body or one whose `implementationQuality` is below `SLOW_WALK_QUALITY`, read off the branch's own delivery figure rather than a new one.

The store is the **one optional field on `Campaign`**, and the reason is the reason `godMode` is not a version bump at all: every other store is absent only on a save from a build that predates it, so reading an absent one as empty would be the save making a claim it never made, while an administration that has never given an instruction genuinely has none. Every reader goes through `selectDirectives`, which reads absent and empty the same way. It is not filed on `institutions` although each body carries the ids of its own, because a refused instruction outlives the runtime it was pointed at — it is a fact about the presidency that gave it, not about the agency that declined — and filing it inside the agency would have made it that agency's to lose. The step drops rather than assumes — a string on one of these lists is a sentence from a build this one never shipped and there is no id it could become — and it deletes the `investigations` key rather than emptying it, because an explicit `undefined` does not survive the JSON round trip a save is.

`OfficeDef.actingAuthority` is deliberately per-office — `vacancies-act`, `statutory-succession`, `board-quorum` or `none` — because assuming one global Vacancies Act rule is wrong in both directions: some offices carry their own statutory succession, and a multi-member board gets no acting member at all, it operates on whatever quorum it still has.

**`campaign.nominations`** (`Readonly<Record<NominationId, Nomination>>`) is the confirmation pipeline, and it replaced a single instantaneous majority check. A `Nomination` is `{ id, office, institution, nominee, stage, stageEnteredDay, openedDay, committee?, controversies, history, outcome? }`, and `stage` walks the real process: `shortlist`, `vetting`, `prepared`, `nominated`, `referred`, `disclosures`, `hearing-scheduled`, `hearing`, `committee-action`, `executive-calendar`, `floor-pending`, `cloture`, `vote`, `confirmed`, then `sworn-in`, with `rejected`, `withdrawn` and `returned` as the other endings. `confirmed` is **not** terminal: an officer is sworn in some days later and the chair is empty until they are. `Nomination.transition?: true` marks a founding pick the seal sent to the calendar.

Three properties make it a process rather than a vote. Stages consume **calendar days**, not turns, and only the Senate's own stages stretch with disposition and controversy — a Senate that dislikes the president cannot make the White House's vetting take longer. **Floor time is the scarce thing**: every nomination already on the Executive Calendar slows the next, so sending up sixty names at once seats them more slowly than sequencing them. And **a projection is a projection** — `projectConfirmation` says what the Senate would do today and is shown at every stage, while what seats somebody is the count taken at the vote, against the Senate as it then is. A nomination that a committee will not report is `returned` without ever being voted down, which is how most nominations that fail actually die. Terminal nominations are kept rather than deleted, for the reason a dead bill is: the pipeline's own median time-to-seat is only readable if the failures stay in it. A v30 campaign has no institutional history because institutions did not exist, so seeding every body at its founding state invents nothing — it is the honest reading of what a v30 save actually recorded. The one thing deliberately not backfilled is who leads them: a v30 save's cabinet is the charter's seats, and inferring a Secretary of Commerce from it would seat a person the save never confirmed.

Two new effect kinds carry all of it through the one gate. **`InstitutionEffect`** `{ kind: "institution", ticks?, actingLapsed?, institution?, changes? }` and **`NominationEffect`** `{ kind: "nomination", nomination?, advance? }`, applied by `institution-effects.ts` — beside their domain for the same reason the cohort and state-geography writers are, with `apply()` still the only writer. `institutionPhase` emits the whole branch's week as one packet rather than fifty-odd ledger entries of routine administrative drift.

**`campaign.trade`** (`TradeState`) is the sectoral trade network, and it replaced a tariff that was two effects: a bilateral `tradeDependence` knock and a flat treasury credit, with no rate, no sector, no price, no volume, no incidence at home, no retaliation and no limit on repeating it.

The taxonomy is `TRADE_SECTORS` (`trade-sectors.ts`), 28 strategic sectors — not HTS codes and not NAICS. Each one **rolls up into the dataset's existing closed `SectorId`**, so this is a refinement of the twenty-three the world already models rather than a second sector system beside it: everything that reads employment or mix keeps reading `SectorId`, and only trade reads this. A `TradeSectorDef` carries `borderPassThrough`, `shelfPassThrough`, `substitutability`, `inputIntensity`, `retaliationSalience`, `instrument` and `authority`. Border and shelf pass-through are deliberately two fields because they are two different quantities that differ by a large factor, and conflating them is the most common available modeling error. `instrument` records that a tariff is not always the tool — defense articles are licensed, critical minerals and semiconductor equipment are export-controlled — so the game can refuse a rate that has nothing to attach to and name the authority that does apply.

**The edges are not stored, and that is the load-bearing decision.** A dense bilateral sector network across the roster would be about seventy thousand entries that nobody authored. What a bilateral sector edge actually is — how much of this sector the United States buys from and sells to this partner — opens at what the two countries recorded (`US_GOODS_TRADE`, below) and is simulated from there: it moves with the product of the two economies' GDP since the opening, every measure in force takes out the share of its sector's flow that its rate destroys (the same `volumeFall` a tariff's incidence is priced with; a non-rate measure at its `COERCION_AS_RATE`, a partner's answer on what the United States sells it), an embargo ends the partner's book and a war between the two leaves 5% of it. An edge's `dependence` is the partner's share of everything the country imports in that sector, and a tariff diverts only to suppliers holding at least 1% of it. A founding anywhere but the United States falls back to the old gravity shape (a few percent of the smaller economy, scaled by the relationship's `tradeDependence`, split by the partner's sector mix). `tradeEdge(campaign, partner, sector)` derives it on demand, `bilateralGoods(campaign, partner)` gives the whole book including goods no trade sector covers, so nothing is evaluated across all pairs on a turn tick, and the save carries only what a decision created:

Tariff order identifiers retain `tariff:<target>:<sector>:<turn>` and `retaliation:<target>:<turn>` for the first order on a target in that turn. Subsequent orders append a deterministic ordinal (`:2`, `:3`, …), skipping occupied identifiers, including lifted historical measures and landed replies. Each retaliation record's `answering` identifies its exact order. Rates remain additive across live orders, and each order remains separately liftable. Existing string identifiers and save shapes remain valid; there is no migration or aggregation of historical orders.

`TradeState` is `{ measures, retaliation, sectorPrice, findings? }`. A `TradeMeasure` is `{ id, kind, sector, target, rate, imposedTurn, imposedDate, authority, statute?, origin, actor?, liftedTurn? }`, where `origin` separates the administration's own measures from a partner's answer and `actor?` is the capital that imposed it, and a lifted measure is kept rather than deleted. `retaliation` holds `RetaliationRecord`s — an answer the world is expecting, with the turn it is due — and `sectorPrice` is how far each sector's US price has moved from baseline, accumulated so the record can say which decision moved it. `tariffPriceStep(partner, sector, rate)` is what a tariff adds to the consumer price level once phased in: the shelf rise on the tariffed goods × the partner's share of the sector's recorded US imports (`US_GOODS_TRADE`, not the live volumes, so the preview and the signed reading agree) × the sector's household share (`householdShare`, `trade-sectors.ts`, from the BLS CPI-U relative importance table for December 2025; 0 for sectors households do not buy directly). `projectTariffPrices` sums the steps still phasing in and turns them into an annual inflation-target shift of `step × 52 / 26` for twenty-six weeks, so the shifts add up to the step, then stop while the tariff stands; it lists each tariff's step and weeks in. Neither writes `economy.inflation`. `selectCustomsReceipts` is one week of the annual duty on tariffs still in force, and `tradePhase` retires that much debt. A lifted tariff contributes nothing.

**Every American tariff names its statute (v67).** There is no general tariff power, so `imposeTariff(campaign, statute, target, sector, rate)` (`tariff-orders.ts`) takes a `TariffStatute`: `s232` (19 U.S.C. §1862, national security, after a Commerce finding about the goods), `s301` (§2411, unfair practices, after a Trade Representative finding about the country), `s201` (§2251, an import surge, after an International Trade Commission finding about the goods; at most 50 points more, four years, and it lands on every supplier), `s122` (§2132, a balance-of-payments deficit; at most 15%, 150 days, only against a partner that sells the United States more than it buys) or `s338` (§1338, answering a partner's own tariff on American goods; at most 50%). `TARIFF_STATUTES` (`tariff-statutes.ts`) holds each one's finder, investigation length, cap, clock, reach and court standing. The measure records `statute`, and a statute with a clock sets `expiresDate`, which `expireTradeMeasures` already lifts. `TradeState.findings` is the investigations ordered, keyed `finding:<statute>:<subject>:<turn>`: a `TariffFinding` is `{ id, statute, sector?, partner?, openedTurn, openedDate, dueDate, outcome?, decidedDate?, causedBy? }`, written by the `trade` effect's `finding` field, and `tariffFindingPhase` rules on each one on its due date: §232 is affirmative for a strategic sector or one where a single supplier holds 40% of imports; §301 for a partner with its own tariff on American goods or relations at 0.45 tension or worse; §201 on a seeded 55% chance, because the Commission decides on its own record. `tariffPacket` is the pricing half, and only tests and the preview harness call it directly. Both fields are optional and absent on a save before v67. A `TariffFinding` keeps `openedDate` and `dueDate` so the player's pending reading (`selectPending`) can count it down with progress; no save field was added for this. The day `tariffFindingPhase` sets its `outcome`, `advanceUntilAttention` stops once on it (matter id = the finding's id, source `investigation`, urgency `soft`).

**A scenario reply's trade act is a typed command (2026-10-19).** The position commitment carries two trade commands, each rendered at cast and re-checked at commit by `conversation-report-actions.ts`. `tariff: { target, sector, rate, answering?, sourceSector?, sourceRate?, sourceTurn? }`: with `answering` and its source fields (a `tradeMeasure` cast) it answers that pinned retaliation and is refused once the retaliation has ended; without them it taxes the cast counterpart. Either way `fittingTariffStatute` finds the law, and `imposeTariff` lays it or the reply is refused with "No trade law covers this tariff at that rate." `tariffLift: { ours: { measure, sector, rate, imposedTurn }, theirs? }` lifts one of this administration's tariffs on the counterpart through `liftMeasure`: by default the one the family's retaliation answers (`answeredTariff`, the measure `{{ourTariffSector}}` names), or with authored `which: "response"` the one laid back on the retaliation gate's response sector (`responseTariff`). With `theirs` it also lifts the cast's pinned retaliation on the same day, with no shelf-price move because the retaliation added none. Each pin must still match the live measure or the reply is refused. In `ScenarioDef` the authored shapes are `tariff: { sector, rate }` and `tariffLift: { theirs: boolean, which?: "answered" | "response" }`, and the `tariffInForce` condition takes the same `of?: "answered" | "response"`. `auditScenarioCommands` requires a tariffable sector, a cast counterpart, and for a lift a `tradeMeasure` cast with a `tariffInForce` gate on the same tariff; a lift alone among the typed commands may carry authored `cost`, without `treasury`, since its own price shows no political cost. `auditTradeActs` (`scenario-audit-trade.ts`) holds a reply's words to its route: `unrouted-trade-act` (the words impose, lift, pause or sanction with no route), `trade-act-terms-mismatch` (a stated rate, goods or scope the route does not lay, including a rung-2 reply that claims to match their rate or tax everything), `unstated-trade-act` (a tariff the words never mention); `TRADE_ACT_DEBT` lists the one shipped id that may only leave. `previewScenario(def, boardIndex?, counterpart?)` (`scenario-reply-preview.ts`) is the developer read of all of this: every board in `reachabilityBoards` where the scenario is live (the family opened for a non-opener, as the reachability audit does; a `tariffInForce` of `response` gets a trade board with the response tariff laid), and on the chosen one each reply's `projectReply` quote and its `commitReply` packet applied to a copy, diffed as metrics, public judgments, the counterpart relationship, weekly customs, trade measures laid and lifted, flags set, the packet's effects and the beats. It never casts the founding nation.

`tariffIncidence()` returns the whole distribution rather than a number: customs revenue net of the volume the tariff destroys, the border price rise, the shelf price rise, the import fall, the diversion share, the domestic output gain, the input-cost rise, the net employment effect, and the chance and target of retaliation. Three properties of it are the point. **The country imposing it pays**: a US shelf price and US input costs both rise. **Most of the lost volume does not come home** — it goes to the next cheapest foreign supplier, which is why a country-specific tariff moves the map more than it moves the factory. And **the net employment effect can go the wrong way**, because protection adds jobs in the protected sector while the same tariff's input costs subtract more across the sectors that buy from it.

Retaliation is settled when the tariff is signed rather than rolled on read — a chance re-rolled every time a screen asked would let a player scrub for a favorable one — and it lands on a lag rather than instantly, because partners answered between two weeks and three months later in waves that tracked each escalation. The target is chosen by political salience rather than by size, which is how retaliators actually choose. Collection answers to the executive branch: `selectCustomsReceipts` reads `selectWorkDelivery(campaign, CUSTOMS_COLLECTION)`, whose owner is CBP (the catalog's holder of `customs-collection`), so an administration that has hollowed out Customs and Border Protection collects less of what it levies, and no other trade body's health moves it.

Every calibration figure in `trade-sectors.ts` traces to a row of `docs/research/TRADE-CALIBRATION.md`, which also records what could not be verified.
