The pass-through ordering — commodities absorb, differentiated manufactures do not — is specific to the United States as importer, because the US is a large enough buyer to move a world price, and is documented as not generalizing to the other forty-nine nations.

`TradeEffect` `{ kind: "trade", measure?, lift?, expect?, chance?, land?, priceMove? }` is the one writer, applied by `trade-effects.ts`. `campaign.trade` landed in the same unreleased **v31** as `institutions` and `nominations` and shares its migration.

**`campaign.security`** (`SecurityState`) is the national security half of the government: the theaters, the intelligence community's own record, the finished assessments, the covert action findings, and the reports Congress is owed. One field rather than five, because they are one subject — a president under pressure reaches for all of them in the same week, and filing them apart would have recorded five features instead of one half of a government.

`SecurityState` is `{ unifiedCommand, intelligence, assessments, covert, warPowers }`.

**`unifiedCommand`** (`UnifiedCommandState`) is `{ commands, aorOverrides, amendments }`. The static half is `COMBATANT_COMMANDS` (`command-catalog.ts`): the eleven unified combatant commands, seven geographic and four functional, each with a headquarters, a mission, standing missions, a peacetime `foundingAssignment` share of each of the six services, and an incumbent and deputy officer. The commands and the split are structure; the assignment shares and the officers are authored, and marked as such in the file — a game that named the serving four-stars would go stale the week one of them retired. A `CommandRuntime` is `{ id, commander?, vacantSinceDay?, assignment, posture, reliefs, lastReliefTurn?, missions }`, and a `CommandOfficer` is `{ name, service, permanentGrade, confirmedDay, acting? }` rather than a `PersonId`: the campaign's people are the political cast, and the star belongs to the position rather than to the officer. **Only the deviations from the region default are stored** in `aorOverrides`, for the reason trade edges are derived — a 196-row map nobody authored would be a 196-row invention, and `defaultCommandFor` reads the region plus the named exceptions that were decisions rather than geography. Every service's assignment sums to 1 across the eleven, asserted by test.

**`intelligence`** (`IntelligenceState`) is `{ taskings, products, archive?, shares?, refusals, pressure, referrals }`. The static half is `IC_ELEMENTS` (`ic-elements.ts`), the eighteen elements — two independent, sixteen components of six departments — each carrying `disciplines`, a `collection` authority (`clandestine` / `technical` / `overt-only`), a `domestic` authority, whether it may run covert action (exactly one may), an `analyticWeight`, and the `boundary` stated in plain words. Where the institution catalog already carries the body, the element points at it and reads `elementCapability` from its `implementationQuality` rather than keeping a second opinion about how well it is running. `refusals` and `pressure` are append-only and kept forever: a refusal that could be retried until it stopped being logged would be a difficulty setting wearing a statute's clothes. An `IgReferral` runs a fourteen-day credibility determination and a seven-day transmission on **days**, not turns, so it reaches the committees faster than the President's own week and there is no lever that slows it.

**`assessments`** (`readonly Assessment[]`, append-only) is the finished product. An `Assessment` is `{ id, question, target, issuedDay, issuedDate, judgment, likelihood, confidence, confidenceBasis, sources, gaps, alternative, dissents, changes }`. Likelihood and confidence are separate fields because they are separate questions — one about the world, one about the evidence — and `changes` is the permanent record that the wording was moved after it was written. `pressAssessment` writes `likelihood` and `changes` and **never** `confidence`; the handler in `security-effects.ts` does not touch it, which is what stops the mechanic from quietly rewarding the pressure.

**`covert`** (`FindingsState`) is `{ findings, unfounded }`. A `CovertFinding` is `{ id, objective, target, signedDay, signedDate, state, notification, reasonsWithheld?, notifiedDay?, activities, closedDay? }`, where `state` is `written | contemporaneous | unwritten` and `notification` is `full-committees | gang-of-eight | withheld`. `unfounded` holds both covert acts taken with no finding standing over them and objectives counsel would not paper — the attempt is the fact, so it is kept in the same list.

**`warPowers`** (`WarPowersReportState`) is `{ obligations }`. A `ReportObligation` is `{ id, target, kind, rung, openedDay, openedDate, dueDay, description, filing?, missed?, extension?, terminated? }`; `kind` is `"equipped-for-combat"` for a forward deployment (military 2) and `"hostilities"` for a blockade, strike or invasion (military 3 to 5). `dueDay` is `openedDay + 2`; `terminationClockRuns(campaign, obligation)` is whether § 1544(b) sets a window at all — a hostilities introduction, unauthorized, not yet terminated — and `terminationDaysRemaining` is the arithmetic of that window, counted from `terminationStartDay(obligation)`: the filing day or `dueDay`, whichever is earlier, as the statute reads. Unfiled, it is `dueDay`, the day the report was **owed**, which is the whole point of modeling it separately from the existing nine-turn `warPowersStanding` gate.

Five new effect kinds carry all of it through the one gate, applied by `security-effects.ts`: **`CommandEffect`** `{ kind: "command", relieve?, reassign?, amend?, posture?, confirmActing? }`, **`IntelligenceEffect`** `{ kind: "intelligence", tasking?, closeTasking?, refusal?, referral?, pressure?, advanceReferrals? }`, **`AssessmentEffect`** `{ kind: "assessment", issue?, change? }`, **`FindingEffect`** `{ kind: "finding", sign?, notify?, close?, activity?, unfounded?, refusedObjective? }` and **`WarPowersEffect`** `{ kind: "warPowers", open?, file?, certify?, miss?, terminate? }`. Three phases run beside them — `commandPhase` (posture draws on the national bench, acting commanders are confirmed once the Senate has had its time), `intelligencePhase` (referrals move on their own clock) and `warPowersReportPhase` (a missed deadline marked once and priced once, and every force still committed past the sixty days billed a compounding weekly cost for continuing). What is deliberately not reconstructed is history — a v31 campaign that fought a war gets no reporting obligations for it and no unfounded covert acts for the covert rungs it took, because manufacturing a paper trail the save never recorded would be worse than admitting there is none.

**`campaign.scenarioFamilies`** (`ScenarioFamilyState`) is the arc ledger, and it closes a defect the flag system could not see. What a family *is* is the rule in DECISIONS.md §7.8. A scenario binds its counterpart by a rule — "the tensest nation", "a country in this band" — evaluated the week it is drawn. That is right for an opener and wrong for everything after it: the ranking moves every week, so a follow-up written as a callback to last month's crisis re-ranks and can arrive about a country the president never dealt with. The flags said the arc continued; the fiction did not.

A `ScenarioFamilyRun` is `{ family, counterpart, place?, unanswered?, openedTurn, lastTurn, beats, closedTurn?, disposition?, title? }`. `place` (`ScenarioPlace = { state, city? }`) is where at home the opener happened when it happened below the capital; a later member with no authored `state` or `city`, on home ground and not at a `capital` site, resolves to it (the opener's city where it is of the member's site kind, else that state's city of the kind). It is dropped when the family closes, so it lives only as long as the arc can still deal. `unanswered: true` marks a run written when its opener was **raised** (`familyRaiseEffects`, emitted with the case raise): the counterpart and place are pinned from that week, but `familyCounterpart` and `familyPlace` hand out nothing and `selectOpenFamilies` skips the run until the answer, which clears the flag, keeps the raised place and counts the opener once in `beats`. A run saved before this has no flag and reads as answered. `disposition` (`"held" | "lost" | "lapsed"`) is present exactly when `closedTurn` is, and says how the arc ended: `held` and `lost` are authored on the close member's replies (`ScenarioReply.resolves`), `lapsed` is the engine's alone. `title` is the opener's subject as the President read it, kept so the Record can name an arc it no longer re-renders. Both are optional so a run saved before them still loads; no save bump. A `ScenarioDef` may declare `family: { id, role }` where role is `opener`, `beat` or `close`: the opener binds normally and **pins** its counterpart, and `bindCounterpart` hands that pin to every later member instead of re-ranking. A beat whose family has not opened binds to nothing and is therefore not live, which is what stops a follow-up arriving before the thing it follows; a closed family hands out no counterpart at all. Closed runs are kept rather than deleted, for the reason a settled war and a dead bill are.

Two audit rules hold the shape. `family-without-opener` refuses a beat that nothing anywhere opens — unreachable content, the same defect class as a condition nothing can satisfy — checked against `CorpusContext.familyOpeners` as well as the batch, because an arc is normally written across several batches. `family-two-openers` refuses a second door into one family: whichever fired second would be dealt as a beat about the first one's country.

**A close says how the arc ended.** `ScenarioReply.resolves?: "held" | "lost"` is required on every reply of a `close` member, at least one of each, and refused anywhere else; the parser enforces it (`checkCloseDispositions`, `scenario.ts`), because an arc with one ending, or none, is a document the Record cannot report rather than a craft call. A third audit rule, `family-without-close` (error), refuses an opener whose family has no close in the batch or in `CorpusContext.familyCloses` (the shard bodies' closes plus the bundled core pack's): an arc with no way out can only end by lapsing.

**A flag callback returns to its setter's place.** `campaign.flagPlaces?` (`Record<flag, ScenarioPlace>`, save v65) holds, per authored flag, where at home the last answer that set it happened. It is written only by the family effect's `callback: { flags, place? }`, which `rememberReply` emits for every reply with authored `sets`: with a place it records it, and without one (an answer in Washington or abroad) it removes any earlier place for those flags, because the latest deed is the one the board stands on. A home scenario gated on such a flag (`kind: "flag", is: true`), with no authored state or city and not at a capital site, resolves to that place; a family place wins over a flag place. It holds at most one entry per authored flag the corpus can set, which bounds it by the corpus rather than by the length of the term.

**An opener nobody answered is dropped.** `expiringPins` (same phase) removes an `unanswered` run once its raise is `FAMILY_LAPSE_DAYS` old, through the family effect's `expire`. It is deleted rather than closed because nothing was dealt under it, so there is no ending for the Record; this is the retention bound for pinned threads. At most one run exists per family id, so the ledger is bounded by the corpus's families either way.

**An answer moves the governor of the state it happened in.** `StateEffect.governorShift` is a signed, relative move of the seated governor's `disposition`, clamped per effect to `GOVERNOR_STANDING_STEP_CAP` (0.04, provisional) and to 0..1, and ignored for a state with no governor. A home reply datelined in a state other than DC carries one (`governorStandingEffects`, `content/src/scenario-governor.ts`), equal to `GOVERNOR_STANDING_GAIN` (0.5) times the net of the opinion deltas the reply's impact already produces; a reply that moves no opinion carries none. `governorPhase` then drifts the governor back toward its delegation at `DELEGATION_DRIFT` a week. Senators are not moved. The same step is added to `GovernorStanding.answerTilt` (optional, absent means nothing remembered), held within ±`GOVERNOR_TILT_CAP` (0.12); `governorPhase` drifts the governor toward its delegation's disposition plus that tilt and fades the tilt with a 26-week half-life (`GOVERNOR_TILT_HALF_LIFE_WEEKS`), so an answer is felt for months rather than erased by the statehouse's own drift within a fortnight. Both values are design values the balance run sizes.

**An arc nobody closed lapses.** `familyLapsePhase` (`packages/engine/src/scenario-family-lapse.ts`, last in the week) closes every open family whose last beat is `FAMILY_LAPSE_DAYS` (112, a planning value) or more calendar days behind the campaign's date, as `lapsed`, through the family effect. A close left unanswered on the desk also ends its family as `lapsed` (`inactionPacket`). `scripts/verify-family-closure.mjs` holds the one-writer rule: nothing but the family effect, the founding and the reachability boards assigns `campaign.scenarioFamilies`.

`ScenarioFamilyEffect` `{ kind: "scenarioFamily", open?: { family, counterpart, scenario, turn, title? }, beat?, close?: { family, turn, disposition } }` is the one writer, applied by `scenario-family-effects.ts`, and it is emitted from `rememberReply` — at the answer rather than the draw, because a scenario that was ranked and never answered has happened to nobody and a family that opened on it would pin a country the president was never asked about.

**`campaign.congress`** (`Congress`) is the branch above the presidency, and it is one legislature — the player's own. Every other capital is modeled by its standing program (`campaign.governments`); giving sixty countries their own chambers would be inventing a branch nobody reads. The shape is `{ chambers: { house, senate }, administrationParty, caucusAnchor, disposition, leadership, committees, authorizations, unauthorized, lobbying, appropriation }`.

`administrationParty` is `AdministrationParty` — `"democrat" | "republican" | "independent"`. It is **chosen** at founding (`FoundingChoices.leader.party`, sealed by `sealFounding`), not derived: ideology and party are independent, so a conservative Democrat and a liberal Republican are both playable, and Independent is a real third answer rather than the absence of one. `resolveAdministrationParty(ideology)` survives as a *suggestion* only — it preselects the founding control from the economic slider and still returns a `UsMajorParty` — and nothing seals a party from it.

`caucusAnchor` is `UsMajorParty`: the major caucus the administration is counted against. It equals `administrationParty` for a Democrat or a Republican; for an independent it is `sealCaucusAnchor(party, ideology)`, the caucus their economics is nearest, read off the same charter tickets. Congress is two-party whoever is president — the `administration` column of every `ChamberComposition`, every Senate seat's party word and every leadership post are counted along the anchor, because who a state sends is a fact about the state. The Speaker and Majority Leader remain whoever holds the chamber.

What being an independent changes is not the seats but the president's claim on them, and that is a **reading**, not an identity check. `party.ts` exports one:

- `administrationAlignment(lean, standing)` → −1..1, how far a Dem ← → Rep lean sits from this administration. For a major-party president it is the lean signed toward them, exactly as before. For an independent it is that lean at `INDEPENDENT_REACH` (0.75) minus `INDEPENDENT_STANDING_GAP` (0.15): nothing is fully with them, and an even state is against them rather than neutral. The electoral map reads it with the standing gap added back (`mapAlignment`), so an independent's states average to the national share.
- `administrationReach(party)` → 1, or `INDEPENDENT_REACH` — how much of a co-partisan's pull the administration commands.
- `towardAdministration(raw, standing)` → an administration-relative quantity converted to movement on the partisan axis, signed by the anchor and scaled by reach.
- `oppositionParties(party)` → **a list**: one caucus for a president with a party, *both* for an independent, who has no caucus of their own and has to argue an act to each side separately. Every "the opposition party" site takes this shape rather than `otherMajorParty`.

Governing as an independent is harder because of that arithmetic and nothing else — there is no difficulty multiplier anywhere. `openingCongress` scales the legitimacy term by reach (a president with no ticket beneath them arrives with no coattails); `countVote` scales the own-party floor by reach, so an independent needs a chamber roughly twenty points better disposed to reach the same yea count and gets there by moving the room; `figureTarget` scales the tilt toward the administration by reach but not the tilt away; `delegationTarget`, `electoralVotesWon`, `selectUsStateVoteDriver`, `selectUsCityVoteDriver`, `grievanceLean` and `routeDomestic` all read alignment or reach rather than comparing party identity.

Hill player-facing words (`Democrat` / `Republican` / `Independent`) are derived in `party.ts` (`usPartyWord`, `partyForCaucus`, `partyForShare`) from the **anchor** plus caucus or share, so a majority leader cannot invert and a caucus is never labeled with a president's independence. Cabinet and person dossiers also read `Person.party` (the named ticket stamped at generation from ideology against `Charter.parties`; the president is stamped with the ticket they *chose*, including `party-usa-independent`) via `person-record` / `hill-reading.personPartyWord`. Surfaces must not print `"Your party"`.

`chambers[chamber]` is a `ChamberComposition`: `{ seats, administration, opposition, independent }`, seats always the sum of the other three (435 and 100). `openingCongress(nation, turn, administrationParty, caucusAnchor)` derives composition from `politics.legitimacy` and `politics.factionalism` rather than rolling it, so a founding is reproducible from the nation alone, and the Senate's lean is half the House's because fifty states do not swing with a national mood the way 435 districts do. `disposition` (0..1) is a separate fact from composition and the one every vote actually turns on: composition is who is in the room, disposition is what the room currently thinks of the president. It drifts toward `dispositionTarget` (approval, less what the administration has done without asking) at `DISPOSITION_DRIFT` per turn rather than snapping, because a legislature's opinion is slow and a disposition that tracked approval exactly would be approval under another name.

`countVote(congress, chamber, appeal, required, states?, readings?, stance?, people?)` is the one arithmetic (`voteOn(campaign, question)` hands it the campaign's `states` and `people`; a caller counting a hypothetical chamber without `people` reads every seated leader at neutral rapport) and it returns a whole `ChamberVote` — `{ chamber, yeas, nays, required, passed, byCaucus? }` — rather than a boolean, because the whip count is what a president reads before deciding whether to hold the vote at all. The administration's own caucus is never an automatic yea (`OWN_PARTY_FLOOR` 0.8), and `appeal` is what the particular question is worth on top of disposition: a visible threat moves an authorization, a nominee's own competence moves a confirmation, an act the country wants moves a bill. What it counts against is `chamberDisposition(congress, chamber, states?)` rather than `disposition` directly — the room's own reading displaced by the members who can carry part of it and, when a delegation table is present, by the seat-weighted state blocs (`DELEGATION_LEVERAGE` 0.25) — so a vote is a function of people and geography rather than of two seat totals, and it stays the single vote arithmetic every other projection composes. An empty or absent delegation table contributes zero displacement, matching pre-delegation counts exactly. `byCaucus` is optional and filled only by `countVoteAgainstByCaucus` (the two removal counts); `countVote` and `countVoteAgainst` leave it absent. `countVote` itself does not decide *how many* votes are `required`; the `required` count is passed in (a simple majority, or three fifths for cloture).

`countVoteAgainst(congress, chamber, appeal, required, states?, defection?, people?)` is the same chamber counted from the opposition's end, and it returns the same `ChamberVote`. It exists because `countVote`'s yeas are members voting *with* the President, so a question whose yeas are votes against him — a veto override, an impeachment — counted through it would make the friendliest chamber the likeliest to remove him. The base is the opposition's own seats (`OPPOSITION_FLOOR` 0.8 + `OPPOSITION_RANGE` 0.2 x how badly the chamber is disposed); what carries a count past two thirds is defection from the President's own side. `defection` is the one band a caller may set, because it is the one that is genuinely question-specific, and it defaults to the veto-override band (0.35) it was measured on. `projectOverride` is its caller. A policy question lets members cross freely, so one disposition is spread across the room. A question about the President himself is counted by caucus instead (`countVoteAgainstByCaucus`, below).

**Removal is counted by caucus.** `selectRemovalVotes(campaign, charter)` returns the two `RemovalVote`s the last two beats of the arc turn on: `{ beat, step: "articles" | "conviction", question, vote: CaucusVote, caucus: CaucusWhip, caucusBreak: CaucusBreak }` (`removal-votes.ts`, re-exported by `removal.ts`). These are a House majority and a Senate two-thirds, both from `countVoteAgainstByCaucus(congress, chamber, appeal, required, states, administrationBreak)` (`congress.ts`) over the chambers as seated. The opposition votes at `OPPOSITION_FLOOR` 0.8 + `OPPOSITION_RANGE` 0.2 x (1 - `chamberDisposition` + the case appeal, `CASE_APPEAL_CEILING` 0.3 measured from the vote's own rung). Independents vote with the caucus they sit with (`independentsSitWith(caucusAnchor)`: the Democrats). The President's caucus votes yea only at `caucusBreak.breaking`, which `caucusBreakAt` sets to zero unless the charge is grave (pressure at or past `BEAT_THRESHOLDS[2]`). When it is grave, it is `caucusBreakShare(approval)` (above, "The electorate"): `CAUCUS_BREAK_CEILING` 0.65 × logistic(`CAUCUS_BREAK_SLOPE` 18.4 × (`CAUCUS_BREAK_MIDPOINT` 0.75 − approval)), where `approval` is the approval of the President's own identifiers (`ownIdentifierApproval`), not the headline. The headline alone no longer opens the break: a member reads their own voters, so at a 30 percent headline an honest failing President still holds about 78 percent of their identifiers and a quarter of the caucus is gone if a grave charge arrives, and none of it without one (DECISIONS 13.9 and 5.9 hold the rule). `CaucusBreak` is `{ grave, approval, collapsed, breaking }` with `approval` the own-identifier figure and `collapsed` true once the share exceeds `CAUCUS_BREAK_VISIBLE` (0.05). The break reads that approval, not `projectVote`: the projection charges the record, and one charge on it is the removal arc's own beats, so a break read off it let each beat lower the number that let the next one carry. `CaucusVote` is a `ChamberVote` with `byCaucus: Record<VoteCaucus, CaucusTally>` present (`VoteCaucus` = `"administration" | "opposition" | "independent"`; `CaucusTally` = `{ seats, yeas }`). `CaucusWhip` is the President's caucus on the count, `{ seats, yeas, mustBreak }`: the party's seats plus the independents who sit with it, with `mustBreak` = `required` minus the other side's yeas. The counts are **derived, not persisted**: nothing is stored, `Campaign` and `RemovalArc` are unchanged, no save version moves, and whipping the chamber moves them the same week. It returns an empty list for any `removalMechanism` other than `impeachment`, because a coup and a clerical council do not hold a floor vote and must not be gated on one they cannot hold. `removalVoteLine(vote)` is the one sentence form ("The Senate counts 50 of the 67 votes needed to convict; 17 of your own 48 would have to break."); the clause after the semicolon appears only while the count is short on the President's own side.

**Sixty votes, not fifty-one.** `floorThreshold(congress, chamber, track)` (`congress.ts`) is the single writer of the `required` count: `majorityOf(seats)` for the House and for a Senate `track` other than `"ordinary"`, `clotureOf(seats)` (three fifths, `Math.ceil(seats * 0.6)`) for the ordinary-track Senate, because a measure the minority will not stop debating never reaches a floor vote — the bar that decides whether ordinary legislation becomes law is the cloture one, not the passage one. `LegislativeTrack` (`congress.ts`) is the closed union `"ordinary" | "reconciliation" | "privileged"`; `LegislativeAct.track` (optional, defaults to `"ordinary"`) names the exception. Continuing resolutions and regular appropriations stay on `ordinary` (sixty in the Senate). `reconciliation` is revenue, tax and the debt limit, where debate is limited by statute and a majority carries. `privileged` is a resolution of disapproval, also a majority. Nominations stay at a majority through `projectConfirmation`, the Senate's own rule rather than an omission. `breakSenateTie` (`senate-tie.ts`) adds the seated `Congress.presidingOfficer` to a Senate count that is exactly 50-50 and whose `required` is a simple majority, and writes that officer onto `ChamberVote.tieBrokenBy`. A vacant chair leaves the tie failing. A count that needs sixty, and every count through `countVoteAgainst` (override, treaty consent, conviction), is left alone. Congress originates while fewer than two of its own bills are in flight, or while fewer than three are in flight once an administration bill already holds the reserved slot (`originationBlocked`). A measure that restrains the President (`disapproves`, `blocksOrders`, `barsUnilateralForce`, or `bindsTheExecutive`) is passed through `countVoteAgainst`, so a hostile chamber is the one that binds him. An act with a domestic signature is counted with one support figure per caucus (`readingsFor` in `caucus-count.ts`): the room and the appeal, plus that caucus's `partyReading` scaled by `BELIEF_WEIGHT`. The administration's seats and the opposition's seats no longer share one figure. An act with no signature still counts as one figure.

**`campaign.bills`** carries the Congressional Review Act as an ordinary catalogue act, not a second path. `act-rule-disapproval`'s two optional `LegislativeAct` fields, read only by it: `targetsOrder(campaign)` resolves the `PendingRule` Congress is moving against the week the resolution is taken up (`originate` stamps it onto `Bill.target`), and `disapproves: true` marks the act as a resolution of disapproval rather than ordinary legislation. `DISAPPROVAL_WINDOW_TURNS` (9 — sixty days of session) bounds how long after a rule goes `in-effect` the act may still `arise`. It passes on the floor, reaches the desk, is vetoable through `vetoBill` and overridable through `projectOverride` like any other bill — a joint resolution of disapproval *is* a law, so giving it a separate path would have been a second legislature.

**`leadership`** (`HillFigure[]`, `hill.ts`) is the people in the room. `HILL_POSTS` names seven — Speaker, both floor leaders in each chamber, and the Appropriations and Armed Services chairs — each with a `chamber`, a majority-or-minority `side`, an `influence` (the share of its chamber that moves with the holder: 0.14 for the Speaker down to 0.06 for a chair), and the pool `role` it is drawn from. A `HillFigure` is `{ post, person, chamber, caucus, influence, seatedTurn }`; how warm the holder is toward the administration is the holder's own `tie.rapport` (below, "The relationship ledger"), read through `figureRapport(people, figure)`, so it stays with the person when the post changes hands. The members are drawn from the same person pool the cabinet is hired from — `role-congressional-leader` and `role-committee-chair`, appended last in `ALL_ROLES` so no existing seed's cast moves — rather than from a parallel person model, and `seatLeadership` writes the seat onto the holder's `PersonRuntime.currentRole`. Who holds a majority post follows the arithmetic (`majorityCaucus`), so a midterm that flips a chamber replaces its majority leadership; `seatLeadership` returns `{ leadership, ties }` — the seated figures and a `tie` effect setting each newcomer's rapport to `figureTarget` — and keeps every incumbent whose caucus and chamber still fit **and their rapport with them**, because a president who spent a year on the Speaker has not lost that work because the Senate changed hands, and it never moves a displaced member across the aisle or across the building. `chamberDisposition(congress, chamber, states, people)` is `disposition` plus the influence-weighted displacement of each seated member's rapport from it plus the optional delegation term — so **an empty `leadership` and an absent delegation table count exactly as the room does** — which is what makes an unseated Hill a legal state rather than a broken one. The displacement is never written back into `congress.disposition`. Each member's rapport drifts weekly toward `figureTarget` (the room's reading, plus a `CAUCUS_TILT`, plus their own ambition and loyalty, plus an optional `partyReading` belief term) at `FIGURE_DRIFT`, written by `congressPhase` as the `tie` effects `leadershipDrift` returns, so every global signal reaches them through the one account of how the administration is regarded, and a bought rapport decays back toward the member's own nature.

**`committees`** (`Record<string, CommitteeSeat>`, `committees.ts`) is the Senate's panels, keyed by the institution catalog's own committee id — the thirteen `CMTE-*` ids the catalog already referred every confirmable office to, derived as `COMMITTEE_IDS` rather than listed a second time, so a committee the catalog stops referring to leaves the lattice on its own. A `CommitteeSeat` is `{ committee, chair?, ranking?, chairCaucus, balance, seatedTurn }`. `chair` and `ranking` are drawn by `seatCommittees` from the same person pool the Hill's posts are (`role-committee-chair`, US pool depth 34 — a chair and a ranking member per panel plus the two gavels `HILL_POSTS` already holds), sorted by id and taken in catalog order, skipping anyone already on `leadership`, and written onto the holder's `PersonRuntime.currentRole`. Seating is deterministic and idempotent: the same seed seats the same panels, and reseating an already-seated Congress moves nobody. `chairCaucus` is stored rather than derived because it is what a midterm changes — `seatCommittees` runs again inside `recompose`, and a Senate that has flipped hands each gavel to the member who was ranking the week before, the same two people the other way round. `balance` is the administration caucus's share of the *caucused* Senate (independents counted out of both columns, since a panel seat is assigned by a caucus). Empty is a legal state: every committee reading falls back to `congress.disposition`, which is what an unseated panel and a save written before the panels existed both get.

**A panel's own disposition is derived, never stored.** `committeePanel(campaign, id)` reads the chair and the ranking member through `caucusTarget` (`hill.ts` — the room's disposition, a `CAUCUS_TILT` scaled by administration reach, and the member's own ambition and loyalty; the same rule `figureTarget` composes a leadership figure's target from), weights them by `GAVEL_SHARE` plus the majority's own margin, and pulls the result `PANEL_GRAVITY` (0.5) of the way from the room. So there is no second disposition to drift out of step with the first, and no weekly write. `committeeDisposition(campaign, committee)` is the one entry point: `undefined` — an office or an act this build routes to no committee — returns the room's own figure unchanged.

**`lobbying`** (`LobbyRecord[]`, `lobbying.ts`) is what a president spent to move a count. A `LobbyRecord` is `{ id, post, approach, turn, landed, account }`, keyed `lobby:<post>:<turn>`. An appropriation claim accepted in one of those trades is a `Favor` the President owes, opened on the member's own tie by the same `tie` effect that moves their rapport (`draftLobbyFavor`, `lobbying-favors.ts`): `direction: "owed-by-president"`, `billId`, `actId`, `post`, `lobbyId`, `reservedTreasury`, `account?`, keyed `promise:lobby:<post>:<turn>:<billId>`, distinct from international `commitments`. `lobbyFavorAvailability` refuses a second open favor on the same bill or the same post and one whose reserve would overcommit authority, reading `openFavorsOwedByPresident`. `applyBill` settles every open favor on the bill to `repaid` on law and `defaulted` on dead (`settleFavorsOnBill`). `LOBBY_PLAYS` is the three approaches and they are deliberately not one lever at three sizes: a `call` is cheap standing and pure persuasion, a `trade` is a budget negotiation that records a political promise on live fiscal business when it lands (no treasury debit at offer or acceptance; the bill's own enactment is the charge) and needs an eligible spending, authorization, or funding measure on the floor, and `public` goes over the member's head to the country — the only one that moves the room's own `disposition` as well as the member, and the only one whose failure leaves the administration measurably worse off than it started. `projectLobbying` prices all of it before anything is spent, including what the chamber would count at afterward, and `lobbyFigure` calls it and nothing else for the odds, so the number shown is the number rolled against; the roll is seeded on `(seed, turn, post)`, so a replay lobbies identically and a failed call cannot be re-rolled by taking another action first. Repeated attention is discounted rather than refused (`recentAttention`, and `publicFatigue` over `PUBLIC_APPEAL_MEMORY_TURNS`), and one member may be worked once a week.

**Acts aimed at one member** (`lobbying-person.ts`, `credit-share.ts`). `LobbyApproach` is `"call" | "trade" | "public" | "credit" | "campaign"`, listed in `LOBBY_APPROACH_ORDER`, and every one runs through `issueLobbying` / `resolveDueLobbying` on the same seeded stream. What each costs with people other than the member is computed once on the projection and charged from it: `LobbyProjection.resentments` (`{ person, post, name, delta, cause }[]`, each charged as a `tie` effect with `rapport: { delta }` and that cause), `repeatCall?`, `grudgeIfMissed?`, `trustIfGood?`/`trustIfBad?`, `credit?: { billId, title, fraction }` and `campaign?: CampaignCharge` (`{ state, stateName, leanAfter, standingBefore, standingAfter, effects }`). A `call` that asks nothing (no live bill) inside `REPEAT_CALL_WINDOW_DAYS` (21) of an earlier call to the same post costs `REPEAT_CALL_RAPPORT` (−0.03) on the member, folded into both rapport figures and frozen on the issued act as `IssuedLobbyAct.repeatCall?`. A landed `trade` with a member of the opposition caucus costs each of the President's caucus leaders (posts with `role-congressional-leader` in the administration caucus) `ACROSS_THE_AISLE_TRADE_RESENTMENT` (−0.02). A `public` appeal is refused when the member's home state reads below `PUBLIC_APPEAL_MIN_STANDING` (0) on `administrationAlignment`, and a miss writes a `TieGrudge` of `PUBLIC_MISS_GRUDGE` (0.25) on the member with the record's account as its cause. `credit` needs a live bill whose act carries a `domestic` signature and no `sharedCredit` yet (`creditMatter`); at issuance it writes the bill with `sharedCredit` and costs each of the President's caucus leaders `SHARED_CREDIT_RESENTMENT` (−0.03). `campaign` needs the member's home state on record; at issuance it writes a `state` effect of `CAMPAIGN_LEAN` (0.01) toward the member's party there and costs `standing` 0.02, and `CAMPAIGN_RESENTMENT` (−0.02) on each caucus leader of the other side from the member: the President's own leaders when the member is across the aisle, the opposition's when the member is the President's own; the answer moves the member's trust by `CAMPAIGN_TRUST` (+0.06 landed, +0.03 missed).

**War powers.** `authorizations` is `ForceAuthorization[]` — `{ id, title, target, enactedTurn, expiresTurn, vote }`, keyed `aumf:<target>:<enactedTurn>` — and `unauthorized` is `UnauthorizedAction[]` — `{ id, target, turn, rung, description }`. `warPowersStanding(campaign, target, rung)` returns one of four readings for `MAJOR_FORCE_RUNGS` (military 3, 4 and 5; a forward deployment is posture, while a blockade, a strike and an invasion introduce forces into hostilities): `not-applicable`, `authorized`, `unilateral`, or `requires-authorization`. The President's own authority covers the first introduction of forces against a country, for `WAR_POWERS_WINDOW_TURNS` (9 — sixty days in weeks); sustaining it past that, or opening a second front while the first has never been voted on, is `requires-authorization`, and the ladder's `military-4` / `military-5` gates refuse it. Running the sixty days out does not restore the latitude. An introduction whose window expired with no vote behind it stands as an `expiredObligation`, which keeps that target at `requires-authorization` and closes a second front everywhere until Congress authorizes or `terminateForce` brings the force out. Acting unilaterally is possible and priced: `recordUnilateralForce` appends the record, charges `UNILATERAL_FORCE_COST`, and drops disposition, and every record then compounds through `removal.ts`'s new `congress` pressure term. The gate applies to the player's own nation and to nobody else's, for the reason above. Asking Congress is `issueForceAuthorization` (`force-authorization.ts`): a three-day `IssuedAuthorizationAct` freezes likelihood ≠ confidence; `resolveDueAuthorizations` re-reads both chambers on the due date; WhipCount lands only after the vote. `requestForceAuthorization` remains the issue-then-resolve-at-due helper for tests.

**Confirmation and appropriation.** `projectConfirmation` runs a nominee through the Senate at a simple majority with a large `CONFIRMATION_DEFERENCE` — real and necessary, since senators who vote against everything else a president wants still hold that an administration is entitled to its own officers, and without it no government short of a landslide could be staffed. Every Senate-confirmed seat, a cabinet chair included, is filled by `openNomination` and the nomination pipeline; there is no same-day appointment (2026-10-13). The count (`confirmationVote` in `confirmation-count.ts`) puts the deference on the room (`VoteQuestion.appeal`) and the nominee's record on each senator (`VoteQuestion.merit`, half of `nomineeAppeal`), added to every member's support after the caucus position in `member-votes.ts` and to each caucus share in the aggregate counter. Added to the room, the record was clipped at the ceiling early in a term and every capable nominee counted the same. `appropriation` is `{ enactedTurn, throughTurn, onPriorYear, priorYear }`; `requestAppropriation` holds the vote and, where it carries, composes `enactBudget`'s own packet rather than building a second one. A book pays for a fiscal year, not for a year from the week it passed: it funds through `selectFiscalYearEndTurn(campaign, selectBudgetYear(campaign))`, never `turn + 52`. The seal writes `openingAppropriation(foundingDate, turn)`, the book the administration inherits, which lapses on September 30 of the oath's fiscal year (turn 37 for the January 20, 2029 oath); `openingCongress`'s placeholder reads the same fiscal year. A refused president is left with `onPriorYear: true` — the government keeps running on last year's numbers, at a small standing stability cost per week, which is the honest outcome rather than a state that stops. `congressPhase` runs the week: disposition drifts, spent authorizations lapse, and the appropriation runs out on schedule whether or not the next one exists.

**`campaign.oversight`** (`OversightState`, `oversight.ts`) is who is looking at this government and what they have found. The shape is `{ inspectors, matters }`.

`inspectors` is `Record<InstitutionId, InspectorGeneral>` — `{ institution, seatedTurn, noticeFiledTurn?, noticeReasons, removedTurn? }` — seated at founding in every institution the catalog lists with no `parent` and a `kind` other than `presidency` / `legislature` / `judiciary` / `state-government` (`INSPECTED_INSTITUTIONS`, derived from the catalog rather than listed, so a body the catalog adds gets an auditor without this file changing). The President cannot appoint one and cannot call one off. They may empty the office, and `fileInspectorRemovalNotice(campaign, institution, reason)` is the only path: it refuses an empty office, a second notice, and a blank reason, and it does **not** remove anybody — it writes `noticeFiledTurn` and appends the reason, and `inspectorGeneralPhase` completes the removal `IG_REMOVAL_NOTICE_DAYS` (30, converted by `igRemovalNoticeTurns()`) later. Filing while an audit is open marks that matter `obstructed` and `public`, which is permanent.

`matters` is `readonly OversightMatter[]`, oldest first, closed ones kept. An `OversightMatter` is `{ id, origin, direction, subject, openedTurn, stage, public, obstructed, beats, closedTurn?, finding? }`. `origin` is `{ kind: "inspector-general", institution }`, `{ kind: "committee", committee }` or `{ kind: "special-counsel", predicate }` — the third carrying the id of the matter the counsel was appointed out of rather than an office id, because that is the whole of what distinguishes it from the department's own work. `direction` is `"directed"` (oversight the administration ordered) or `"received"` (oversight aimed at it) — only `received` matters cost anything. `stage` runs `opened → gathering → reported → closed` on clocks written in days and converted at their point of use (21 / 84 / 28), advanced by `oversightRipeningPhase` and by nothing else: the ripening rule reads no fact about the administration, so a proceeding cannot be slowed by being popular. `public` is set once and never unset — `applyOversight` enforces that against every writer, so no later week can tidy a fact the country already has. `beats` follows the `RemovalArc` beat pattern and is append-only.

**Two phases open matters, and one advances them.** `inspectorGeneralPhase` opens at most one audit a week, in the worst-run body whose auditor is seated and idle, gated on `auditWarrant(runtime) ≥ 0.45` — `oversightPressure × 0.5 + politicization × 0.3 + (1 − integrity) × 0.2`, so an audit is caused by the administration rather than drawn. An audit opens **not public**, which is the window to fix the thing rather than fight about it. `committeeInquiryPhase` opens at most one inquiry a week off three facts already in the campaign and nothing invented for it: a contested `ExecutiveOrderRecord`, a covert-act flag in `player.flags` (`destabilized:` / `surveilling:`), and an obstructed audit. Jurisdiction is the catalog's own `office.committee` routing (`committeeForInstitution`, falling back to a component's parent; `committeeForOrderDomain` maps the four `OrderDomain`s onto catalog domains), and a panel only opens a file when `committeePanel(...).disposition < 0.5` — a committee that is with the administration does not investigate it. A committee inquiry opens **public**; a chair announces it.

**A third arm opens matters, and no save version moves.** `specialCounselPhase` (`special-counsel.ts`) is the Attorney General appointing a prosecutor out of a matter the department can no longer be seen to handle itself. The bar is three facts already on the record and nothing invented for it: the matter is `received`, `public` and `obstructed`, and it has reached `reported` — a finding the country can read rather than a suspicion somebody holds. One counsel sits at a time across the whole government, oldest ripe predicate first, never twice out of the same predicate, never out of a counsel's own matter, and never at all with nobody alive in the seat `institutionDef("INST-DOJ").cabinetRole` names. The counsel's matter opens **public** and ripens on the same `oversightRipeningPhase` clock as everything else on the board. On appointment the committee with jurisdiction over Justice (`committeeForInstitution("INST-DOJ")` — `CMTE-JUDICIARY`) is notified through the existing `governance` feed kind; no new `FeedItem` kind was added.

**There is no presidential verb that touches a counsel's matter, and no AG-side removal verb either** — no day-to-day supervision is the point of the office, and a removal the engine could perform on the President's behalf would be that supervision under another name. What the President has is the chair. `counselDismissalEffects(campaign, role, name)` returns `[]` for every seat but the Attorney General's and for that seat while nothing is sitting; with a counsel sitting it composes into `dismissMinister`'s own packet — the counsel's matter marked `obstructed` (permanent, on the `applyOversight` rule), `congress.disposition` down `0.2`, and the Judiciary Committee notified of the removal. The counsel does not stop; nothing in this engine stops one. `projectCounselObstruction(campaign, role)` is the same reading before the act, for the sheet that offers it.

**This adds no stored field.** `MatterOrigin` widened by one arm inside a list `campaign.oversight.matters` already holds, `OversightState` is unchanged, `applyOversight` is unchanged, and a save written before the counsel existed loads with a board that simply has no counsel on it.

**`selectOversightPressure(campaign)`** (0..1) is what being investigated is worth. Only `received` and `public` matters count, at `STAGE_WEIGHT` (`opened` 0, `gathering` 0.12, `reported` 0.3) plus `OBSTRUCTION_WEIGHT` 0.45, the sum then scaled by `ORIGIN_WEIGHT` — 1 for an audit and for a committee, **1.4 for a special counsel**, because a prosecutor nobody in the administration supervises is not the same fact about a president as a hearing a chair had to want; a closed matter contributes nothing except its obstruction, which is remembered for 168 days. It is exported rather than inlined because two branches read it and must not grow two formulas: `dispositionTarget` (`congress.ts`) subtracts `oversight × 0.2`, so the Hill's mood carries it every week rather than being charged once, and the removal arc's `congress` pressure term is the seam it is shaped for — the same shape as the `unauthorized` count, which likewise enters both accounts.

**`campaign.bills`** (`Record<billId, Bill>`) is legislation. A `Bill` is `{ id, act, title, sponsor, introducedTurn, status, passage, stage?, setbacks?, momentum?, beats?, override?, resolvedTurn?, enactedBy?, presentedOn? }`, keyed `bill:<act>:<introducedTurn>`; `presentedOn?` (`GameDate`) is the day the enrolled bill reached the President, written when a bill leaves the floor for the desk and when a deadline measure is discharged to it. The ten-day signing window (`signing-window.ts`) runs from it, Sundays excepted, per Article I §7; a desk bill written without one reads as presented at the close of the week it was introduced, the week a discharged measure goes to the desk; `enactedBy?` (`signed` | `veto-overridden` | `unsigned`): how a law became law, written by `signBill`, the override vote and the desk's lapse; absent on a bill that is not law; `billEnactment` reads a law without one as signed; `sharedCredit?` (`SharedCredit`, `state-ties.ts`: `{ person, post, fraction, turn }`) is the credit the President handed a member while the bill was live, and `enactmentEffects` reads it on every path that makes the act law: `withSharedCredit` scales each domestic `state` lean delta that moves toward the administration and each positive-valence cohort strain by `1 − fraction` (`SHARED_CREDIT_FRACTION`, 0.3), and leaves unfavorable movement whole. `status` is `"in-progress" | "on-the-desk" | "law" | "vetoed" | "dead"` and `passage`/`override` are `ChamberVote[]`. The four staging fields are optional and every reader defaults them, because a bill that has left the chambers is not at a stage and a save written before staging carries none of them. What an act *does* lives in `LEGISLATIVE_ACTS` (`legislation.ts`), code rather than dataset for the reason the policy stances and the executive orders are: these are rules with effects, and a save carrying a copy would reload against a build that had since rebalanced them. The title is stored beside the act id anyway, because a law is remembered by name and a record that could only name it by lookup goes blank the day the catalogue is edited. A catalogue act may carry optional `domestic?: DomesticSignature`; `enactmentEffects` splices `routeDomestic` and `beliefSplitEffects` on every path that makes the act law — signed, desk-lapsed, or overridden — so the infrastructure act (`public-infrastructure`) marks industrial states and the defense authorization marks defense-weighted ones. Institutional acts (war powers, press shield) omit the field and stay unrouted. `legislationPhase` originates on a cadence, choosing among acts whose `arises` predicate the country currently satisfies, and only where `projectPassage` carries both chambers — a president does not get to veto what Congress could not pass. What it originates is a passage rather than a result: see **staged enactment** below. `signBill` and `vetoBill` (a veto message is required, not optional) are the president's half; `projectOverride` exposes the two-thirds count before the veto is taken, and the phase resolves the attempt two turns later into `law` or `dead`. A law is durable and constrains later choices through `LawConstraint`: `blocksOrders` puts named executive orders out of reach entirely (statute, not risk — `orderAvailability` refuses them), `authorizesOrders?: readonly string[]` is the opposite reach — an order named there is not merely un-blocked but affirmatively backed by the statute — and `barsUnilateralForce` withdraws the office's own war-making latitude so every major rung needs a vote. `bindsTheExecutive?: boolean` marks an act that limits what the executive's own officers may do where no order names the power it takes away (`act-press-shield`, which narrows what a federal subpoena can compel); it changes no availability and only makes the act count as a restraint on the floor. The acts that name orders today: `act-economic-stabilization` authorizes `order-price-controls` (no standing statute grants a President price controls; the 1970 act lapsed in 1974), `act-defense-authorization` authorizes `order-defense-production`, the border act authorizes `order-border-controls`, and `act-emergency-powers-limitation` blocks `order-emergency-construction`. The three constitutional grounds an order can stand on are one exported type, `ConstitutionalGround = "authorized" | "silent" | "forbidden"` (`legislative-acts.ts`), read by `constitutionalGround()`/`lawAuthorizesOrder()` from the statute book with a prohibition governing an authorization where a law does both to the same order. `strikeDownChance` and `orderAvailability` both read it — see `ARCHITECTURE.md`'s orders section.

**`voteOn(campaign, question)`** (`congress-votes.ts`) is now the one entry point every floor count goes through, and the one arithmetic behind it. A `VoteQuestion` is `{ chamber, appeal, required, stance: "administration" | "opposition" | "neutral", counted?: "with" | "against", readings?, floor?, oppositionShare?, defection?, administrationBreak? }`: the chamber and the measure's own appeal on top of disposition, the count it has to reach, whose measure it is (which decides which caucus is whipped and which has to be carried, read only in the `with` frame), which end it is counted from, the caucus-by-caucus readings of the act's own signature, a floor under the room's support (the clean stopgap's, where members answer to the government staying open), an explicit opposition share where the question decides it rather than the room, the override defection band, and — the mark of a question about the President himself rather than about a measure — the share of his own caucus that has decided to break. Every count returns a `CaucusVote` (`ChamberVote` with `byCaucus` always present), so a screen can always read how many of the President's own members would have to break. `countVote`, `countVoteAgainst`, `countVoteAgainstByCaucus` and `countFundingContinuityVote` are the same arithmetic with the question already spelled out, kept for callers holding a `Congress` and no `Campaign`. Callers import them from `congress-votes.ts` or the engine index; `congress.ts` sits in the engine's import cycle, where a re-export would load out of order.

**`LegislativeAct.plank?: PlankId`** (`legislation.ts`) is the platform plank an administration bill enacts. `administrationWouldWrite(campaign, act)` refuses transmission unless the sealed platform carries that plank and the plank's own program aligns with the party that sealed it — a measure with no plank (a debt ceiling, a stopgap) is nobody's program and is not checked against either.

**`legislative-acts-fiscal.ts#fiscalAppetite(campaign)`** adds up to `APPETITE_CEILING` (0.12) to the floor appeal of a fiscal act, off three readings nobody in the administration controls: how far the structural balance runs past `TOLERATED_DEFICIT_SHARE` (0.04) at `DEFICIT_APPETITE` (0.8), whether the rating agencies have moved to a negative outlook (`RATING_MOVE_APPETITE` 0.03), and how close the Treasury sits to the debt limit — `HEADROOM_APPETITE` (0.05) phasing in under `CRAMPED_HEADROOM_WEEKS` (13) of room. No other act in the catalogue reads it, and a hostile Congress and a friendly one both get more interested in the books as the books get worse.

**`Congress.presidingOfficer.liaison`** (`state-legislature.ts`) is a number read through `hill.ts#senateLiaison(congress)`, clamped to ±`SENATE_LIAISON_BOUND` (0.05) and zero whenever the chair is empty; `chamberDisposition` adds it to the Senate's room only, alongside leadership and the delegation blocs, so a floor count taken through it (which is every one) reads a Vice President who has worked the chamber. `presidingOfficer` is written at the seal (`foundingConfirmations`).

**`transmit` and the recurring docket.** A `Bill` may now reach the record because the President put it there. `transmit(campaign, actId)` (`legislation.ts`) writes exactly one `Bill`, `in-progress` at `referral` (the same shape `originate` writes), plus a `congress` disposition write of `−TRANSMISSION_DISPOSITION_COST` (0.03). It no longer resolves the measure on the week it is sent: the President's bill takes the staged passage below like any other, and what the transmission buys is a place on the calendar rather than a result. It adds **no field to `Bill` and no field to `Campaign`**: the id is the same `bill:<act>:<introducedTurn>` key `originate` writes, `sponsor` is the catalogue act's, and eligibility is derived (`transmittableActs` = `billsOnTheAgenda` filtered to `sponsor === "administration"`, refused while any bill is `in-progress` or `on-the-desk`, or while that act already has a bill resolved this turn). The save version is unchanged for this reason. `LEGISLATIVE_ACTS` grows from eight entries to thirteen — `act-full-year-continuing-resolution` (the existing `funds` field as `{ throughFiscalYear: true }`, funding to the end of the budget year rather than a year of turns, not a second appropriation mechanism, and `onPriorYear` is deliberately left set), `act-debt-limit-increase` (`treasuryOfGdp: +0.01` against `economy.debt: +0.01`), `act-agriculture-improvement` (`recurs` five years, `domestic` through `agriculture`/`food-processing`), `act-tax-relief-and-growth` and `act-immigration-reform` — all five using fields the type already declares.

**Staged enactment: a bill is a passage, not a roll (`bill-stages.ts`).** A measure Congress takes up enters at `stage: "referral"` with `status: "in-progress"`, and `legislationPhase` resolves one week of it at a time through `BILL_STAGES` — `referral → markup → floor`. Each week draws once from `createRngStreams(seed).stream("bill-stage", bill.id, turn)` and lands on one of four `StageOutcome`s: `success` (advance a stage; at `floor`, the desk), `progress` (`momentum += 0.04`), `controversy` (`momentum ± 0.05`, a second stream deciding the sign, and `−0.01` standing when it swings against), or `stagnation` (`momentum −= 0.03` and one setback). `BILL_SETBACK_LIMIT` is 3, and the third setback marks the bill `dead` with `resolvedTurn` set — the record is kept, as every dead bill's is. `advanceChance` is `clamp01(0.3 + STAGE_BIAS[stage] + 0.5 × (congress.disposition − 0.5) + act.appeal + momentum)`, so the room's own disposition is the largest term and markup — the stage with no bias — is the hardest of the three. Committee membership is not modeled: the markup stage reads the global `congress.disposition` exactly as a confirmation count does.

`momentum` reaches the arithmetic through `projectPassage(campaign, act, momentum)`, which adds it to the act's own appeal rather than to the count, so the one place a floor vote is counted stays `countVote`. The floor stage is therefore still decided by the cloture/track threshold: a `success` draw at `floor` only reaches the desk when `carried(projectPassage(…, momentum))` holds and the desk is free, and otherwise reads as `progress` (carries, desk occupied — the calendar being full is not the bill stalling) or `stagnation` (does not carry). A bill that reaches the desk rewrites `passage` with the count the floor actually took. `projectStage(campaign, bill)` exposes the whole reading — stage, `advances`, `stalls`, `setbacksRemaining`, the floor count and whether it carries — so a screen and the week it narrates are never two readings.

Two things are deliberately outside the machine. A `LegislativeAct` marked `urgent` (the continuing resolution) is **discharged** straight to the desk with no stage, because a stopgap that spent a month in markup could not end a shutdown before the country had lived a season of one. And the appropriation request (`requestAppropriation`, `appropriation.ts`) stays a single-shot per-account vote: it writes `congress.appropriation` rather than a `Bill`, it is the President's own book rather than a measure Congress originated, and a budget that took five weeks to resolve would put the shutdown clock inside the resolution of the thing that stops it.

Throughput is unchanged by design. The origination gap is counted from the last **introduction** rather than from the last signature, so the weeks a measure spends in committee are spent inside the eight-to-fourteen-week window Congress was going to wait anyway; a 120-week regression holds the observed spacing at or under the drawn maximum, keeps at most one bill in the chambers and one on the desk, and resolves every bill within fifteen weeks of introduction. `billsOnTheAgenda` and `originate` both read one `inFlight` list (`in-progress` or `on-the-desk`) rather than the desk alone, which is what keeps "one at a time" true a stage earlier than it used to be.

**`campaign.termClock`** (`TermClock`) is the fixed dates the presidency is arranged around: `{ term, termStartTurn, elections, outcome? }`. The dates are the federal calendar's, not counts of turns: the oath is `CAMPAIGN_OPENING_DATE` = `FIRST_INAUGURATION` (January 20, 2029), and `presidencyElections(foundingDate)` reads the schedule off `milestonesBetween`, the one source of election dates: the midterm on November 5, 2030, the presidential election on November 2, 2032, and a second term's on November 7, 2034 and November 4, 2036. `nextElection` is the schedule entry after the ones already held, with its `date` and `daysAway`. `ElectionResult.turn` is the turn the result is filed under. A win records the result and nothing more on election night; `pendingInauguration` turns the term over on January 20, 2033 (`termStartTurn` becomes that day's turn, 209). `TERM_TURNS` (208) is kept as a count derived from the calendar, the whole weeks from one inauguration to the next; `MIDTERM_TURNS`, `TERM_DAYS`, `MIDTERM_DAYS` and `CampaignPace.termTurns`/`midtermTurns` are retired. The calendar also carries the Virginia and New Jersey governors' races (`gubernatorial-election`, November of years ≡ 1 mod 4, first on November 6, 2029). A run ends on election night with no tick after it: `advanceUntilAttention` stops with `StopReason` `"ended"` on a defeat or the term limit as well as on removal. `elections` is `ElectionResult[]` — `{ id, kind, turn, voteShare, swing?, won?, summary }`, keyed `election:<kind>:<turn>` — with `swing` (`Record<Chamber, number>`, signed from the administration's side) on midterms and `won` on the presidential contest. `outcome` is `"defeated" | "term-limited"` and is set **only** when the presidency is over; winning re-election sets nothing, because a second term is a presidency with its future open again. `TERM_LIMIT` is 2: a win opens a full second four years on the same clock, and the end of that term resolves as `term-limited` with no contest held, because the President is not on the ballot — so a campaign runs at most eight years. `selectWeeksServed` is the one place `turn - termStartTurn + 1` is computed: it is the only week number a player is ever shown, and a second reader would let two surfaces print different week counts.

`projectVote(campaign)` is the count and it is pure: the bloc ballot at habitual turnout (`turnout.ts#selectBallot`: the five blocs that already are the electorate, each voting its approval plus `PARTY_LOYALTY`, weighted by share × turnout; an election that invented its own polling would be a second opinion of a country that has one; the `ELECTORATE_ANCHOR` 0.5 driver is kept only so the displayed drivers still sum to the share) plus a small `INCUMBENCY`, plus US city lean change since founding (`how the cities have moved`, leverage 0.25, omitted when zero or when the charter holds no elections), less the constitutional record — force used without a vote, orders the courts struck down, vetoes Congress overrode, removal beats opened, a government on the prior year's appropriation — each capped by `RECORD_PENALTIES` so no single kind of failure loses an election on its own. A midterm applies `seatSwing` per chamber: the national mood times `SWING_SENSITIVITY` (House 0.35, Senate 0.12, since only a third of that chamber is up and it is apportioned by state) less a structural `MIDTERM_PENALTY` (0.04, `election-seating.ts`; not yet replaced by a bloc turnout differential), so an administration on exactly even standing still comes back to a smaller caucus. Seats transfer between the two caucuses only, never out of the independents and never below empty. `electionPhase(charter)` runs it, and only under `holdsElections(charter)` — an executive that faces the country on a fixed date, which is `impeachment` and `electoral-defeat` and not a parliamentary or a junta charter.

`projectVote` carries four more drivers, each bounded where it is computed and omitted at zero: **who is going to turn out** (`turnout.ts#turnoutShift` = `selectBallot(campaign).actual − .habitual`, rounded to a thousandth: the ballot at each bloc's turnout this week less the same ballot at its habit, so the habit is charged once in the share itself and only what this administration caused is reported as turnout. A bloc's turnout is `BLOC_HABIT` (ownId 0.72, ownLean 0.58, pure 0.23, oppLean 0.58, oppId 0.72) + `blocTurnoutShift`: for each cohort with unforgiven harm (`cohortHarm` = clamp(−`centeredStrain`, 0, 1)), the President's own blocs lose `BLOC_DISILLUSION` (0.1) × π and the opposition's gain `BLOC_MOBILIZATION` (0.1) × |π| of the harm, weighted by the cohort's share × its composition in that bloc ÷ the bloc's national share, summed across the cohort axes as main effects and capped at ±`BLOC_TURNOUT_CAP` (0.04) per bloc, all causes together; the cohort-level `TURNOUT_*` coefficients and the 0.03 driver cap are deleted), **which way the country has been moving lately** (`momentum.ts#momentumShift`: the headline against a decaying memory of the closed weeks, `MOMENTUM_DECAY` 0.85, × `MOMENTUM_LEVERAGE` 0.5, capped at `MOMENTUM_CAP` 0.02), **the candidate the other party is putting up** (`opponent.ts#opponentShift` = `seededMargin × matchupKnown`, where `matchupKnown` is the square of the elapsed share of the days from the oath to the first presidential Election Day, and `seededMargin` is within `MARGIN_BOUND` 0.03), and **statehouses refusing the administration's programs** (`states-acting.ts#nonCooperationShift`: the population share of states whose governor is across the aisle with disposition below `REFUSAL_DISPOSITION` 0.3, × `NON_COOPERATION_WEIGHT` 0.03, capped at `NON_COOPERATION_CAP` 0.01). All constants are planning values. `opponent?` (the v54 field) is read by `selectOpponent`, which falls back to `drawOpponent`: the seed picks among the three of the other party's bench (governors, congressional leaders, committee chairs, opposition leaders; alive; not the VP or a cabinet chair) with the highest charisma plus ambition. A 269-269 college goes to the House (`contingentHouseVote`: one vote a state from the seated delegation's majority, DC excluded, 26 needed); a tie is never a loss by itself. When neither side reaches 26, the House is read as balloting to the inauguration without a choice (`deadlocked: true`), and `acting` names who acts as President under the Twentieth Amendment §3: the running mate the Senate chooses by a majority of the whole number (Twelfth Amendment), or the Speaker (3 U.S.C. §19) with no such majority; the President is not returned either way, and the summary says so rather than calling it a defeat.

Losing an election and being removed are **different endings and both are reachable**. `selectEnding(campaign)` returns `"in-office" | "removed" | "defeated" | "term-limited"`, reading the removal arc first because it is the only ending that arrives on a day nobody scheduled; the election path never writes `RemovalArc` and the removal path never writes the clock, so the two can never print each other's sentence.

**`campaign.press`** (`PressCorps`) is `{ standing, dispositions, lastSpokeTurn, engagements, scandalResponses? }`. `scandalResponses` is `Record<scandalId, ScandalResponse>`, keyed by the derived scandal id, `ScandalResponse = { verb: ScandalVerb, turn, acting?: PersonId }` and `ScandalVerb = "come-clean" | "fight-it" | "give-them-someone"`; absent until the first answer, and a save without it loads unchanged (no save-version bump), its answers read from the legacy flag. `dispositions` is `Record<OutletId, number>` — what each masthead thinks of this administration, 0..1, **stored per outlet**. Each drifts at `PRESS_DRIFT` toward its own `outletTarget`: `pressStandingTarget` plus that outlet's `lean` at `LEAN_WEIGHT`, clamped. `pressStandingTarget` is derived every week and never accumulated: `politics.pressFreedom`, how the country stands with the administration (the stored headline, `(approval − 0.5) × 0.4`, capped), force used without a vote, orders struck down, orders that had to be defended at all, and silence — `campaign.turn - lastSpokeTurn` past `SILENCE_TOLERANCE_TURNS` (6), because a government that stops explaining itself is covered as one.

`standing` (0..1) is **derived**: the evenly-weighted mean of `dispositions`, written only by `corpsFrom`. It is stored rather than recomputed at every read because three systems already read it and none of them should have to know the corps has parts — a public appeal in `lobbying.ts`, the capped press term in `opinion-causes.ts`, and the corps' own weekly drift in `press.ts`, which reads the headline (`(approval − 0.5) × 0.4`, capped) now that no coalition figure exists. The derivation used to run the other way — an outlet's disposition *was* `standing + lean × LEAN_WEIGHT` — which made the four outlets one number wearing four hats; each desk now holds its own reading and `standing` is the mean of them.

`PRESS_OUTLETS` is eighteen fictional desks, each a real outlet's analogue by format, lean, reach and credibility, and none named for a real one: six papers of record (daily, Capitol trade paper, wire, opinion weekly, network, business daily), twelve in mirrored left/right pairs (cable, news site, talk radio, podcast), and four regional papers by census region (`region`). Each carries `format`, `reach` (weekly share of adults, authored) and `credibility`; `FORMAT_PARTISANSHIP` (1 papers/wire/network/regional, 2 site/podcast, 2.5 cable, 3 talk radio) scales how far the desk's reading of the President carries it. `outletTarget` is the sum of `outletDrivers` around 0.5: the shared record, `lean × LEAN_WEIGHT`, the partisan term `outletReading(beliefs, placementOf(campaign)) × FORMAT_PARTISANSHIP` (party beliefs bent 0.5 toward the platform's courted cohorts, `press-placement.ts`), the act reading at `PRESS_BELIEF_WEIGHT`, and framing. Mirrored twins keep every axis and every clamp symmetric, so `standing` stays the equal-weight mean and opening at 0.5 for any party. Reach decides only filing frequency and `outletAudience` (cohorts read-only). No save version moves: the roster is catalog; `FeedItem.desk`/`filings` (v53) are now written, by `fileAtDesk(s)` (`press-filing.ts`), and `PressEngagement.outletId` is written on resolution. The flag `press-secretary-spent:<personId>` (marked) records a secretary who took a bad cycle. `outletStance` names a disposition hostile/skeptical/neutral/friendly. Each outlet carries **`beliefs: IdeologyVector`** — the same three axes as `Person.ideology`, `Party.beliefs`, the cohort centroids and `AlignmentVector` — and its `lean` (-0.8 to 0.7) is **derived from them**, not authored beside them: `lean === beliefs.authLibertarian`, assigned by the single constructor the table is built through, because a masthead's standing posture toward any president is what it believes about executive authority. `outletTarget` gains a belief term: `outletReading(beliefs, recentActPull(campaign).pull) × PRESS_BELIEF_WEIGHT` (0.4), where `recentActPull` (`press-beliefs.ts`) sums the authored `ExecutiveOrder.alignment` of every order signed inside `PRESS_ACT_MEMORY_TURNS` (16, per-axis clamped) and `outletReading` is the dot product `partyReading` and `cohortAffinity` already use — one geometry for the whole game. **No save version moves**: `beliefs` is catalog, like `lean`, `beat` and `coverage` before it, and the reactions land on `press.dispositions`, which v41 already stores. The authored vectors sum to zero on every axis, which is what keeps `standing`-as-mean faithful under the new term: the four readings cancel, so the mean of the four targets is still `pressStandingTarget` outside the 0..1 clamp. An administration that has signed nothing alignment-bearing reads exactly 0 and the surfaces say nothing rather than inventing a pull; `OutletStanding.read` carries `{ reading, act? }` for a per-desk "why" line. `PressPlay.targeting` says who a play is with: `"outlet"` for an interview and a leak, which name a desk and land there in full and at `spillover` share elsewhere, and `"corps"` for a press conference. The mastheads are the *same* names `article.ts` prints over a press dispatch — one press vocabulary, not a list in the formatter and a second list of dispositions elsewhere.

`PRESS_PLAYS` is the three levers with real prices: an interview (small, safe, slow), a press conference (the only one that moves the corps far in a week, and the only one that puts a bad record in front of people whose job is to have read it), and a leak (buys coverage without the President's name on it, until it has the President's name on it). `projectPressAction` exposes the odds — the president's own charisma and competence, the record, and a per-play exposure — and `takePressAction` rolls against exactly that number on a seed of (campaign, kind, turn). Only the two on-the-record plays reset `lastSpokeTurn`; a leak does not, which is the asymmetry that makes three plays rather than one. Press standing reaches approval **through two judgments and nowhere else**: `coverageCause` in `opinion-causes.ts` is one term, `(standing − 0.5) × PRESS_COVERAGE_WEIGHT` (0.5) capped at `PRESS_COVERAGE_CAP` (0.15), carried by the integrity and competence targets and revised at the speed a public revises an impression. A second path to the headline would be a second opinion of the country, so `player.metrics.approval` is never written from coverage. The corps' own drift reads the headline in return, which closes a loop through the judgments' slow revision and the cap, not through a direct write.

**`campaign.treaties`** (`Record<treatyId, TreatyRecord>`) is what the President concludes abroad, only some of which the Senate decides about. A record is `{ id, treaty, title, kind, counterpart, signedTurn, status, vote?, resolvedTurn? }`, keyed `treaty:<kind>:<counterpart>:<signedTurn>`; `kind` is `"alliance" | "trade" | "arms-control" | "executive-agreement"` and `status` is `"signed" | "ratified" | "in-force-by-signature" | "rejected" | "withdrawn"`. Each kind's `TreatyTerms` (`treaties.ts`) carries `bindsOn: "ratification" | "signature"` — the first three bind on ratification and go through the Senate count below; `executive-agreement` binds on signature (the Case-Zablocki Act's own distinction, 1 U.S.C. § 112b — most of what the United States actually concludes abroad is a congressional-executive or sole executive agreement, not an Article II treaty) and never reaches the Senate at all. Ratification is the one arithmetic on the board that is neither a simple majority nor an override of the President: `projectRatification` runs `countVote` against `supermajorityOf(100)` = 67, with an appeal built from the instrument's own `senateAppeal` (arms control hardest, trade easiest; absent for a signature-bound kind, which never runs this count), the counterpart's trust, whether the two governments already sit in a standing body together (`sharesCompact`, read off `blocsInPlay` rather than a second membership model), less the counterpart's military threat. `signTreaty` is deliberately **not** gated on whether the Senate would ratify — a president signing what the Hill will refuse is one of the most characteristic acts of the office — and signing a ratification-bound instrument alone writes nothing into the relationship; only ratification does, via `addTreaty` plus trust/alignment/tension movement in **both** directions. Signing a signature-bound instrument writes that movement immediately instead, at a fraction of a ratified treaty's own effect, plus a smaller standing gain and a fall in `congress.disposition` for having gone around the chamber. A rejection costs standing and stays on the record; `withdrawTreaty` — which now also accepts an `in-force-by-signature` record, unwinding what it did to the relationship at a larger standing cost than pulling back a mere signature — is the version that does not appear there as a defeat. The clock is the one part that has to be reasoned about — a v16 save records no term start — and the only honest reading is that the term began at the founding, which is where every v16 campaign's did. Press standing opens neutral and `lastSpokeTurn` is dated from the save's own turn, so a v16 campaign is not charged forty weeks of silence for a mechanism that did not exist when it was played.

**`campaign.player.metricHistory`** is `MetricSet[]`, oldest first: where the player's five metrics stood at the close of each completed turn. It is snapshotted in `advanceTurn` from the state *before* either phase runs, so an entry is the reading the player ended the turn on rather than the world's reply to it, and the live turn is deliberately absent because it is still moving. The window is bounded at `METRIC_HISTORY_WINDOW` (24 turns, `turn-loop.ts`) so a long campaign's save does not grow without limit. Two selectors read it: `selectMetricSeries(campaign, key)` appends the live reading to give the series a surface draws, and `selectMetricDelta(campaign, key)` returns the movement since the last closed turn — `undefined` only where `metricHistory` is genuinely empty, because "it has not moved" and "there is nothing to compare against" are different statements and a surface must be able to tell them apart. A built campaign's own turn 1 is not that case: `inheritedMetricHistory` seeds a predecessor's closing quarter before the president's first turn, so the delta returned there is the move against that inherited reading.

`campaign.feed` is what the player could plausibly have found out (as distinct from the ledger, which records everything). `FeedItem.desk?: { outletId, stance, byline? }` (v53) is which masthead filed the item and what that masthead thought of the administration at the moment it did — the stance copied at filing rather than read back later, because a paper that has since come round did not write a friendlier story last month. `FeedItem.filings?: readonly { outletId, stance }[]` (v53) is every other masthead that filed on the same thing; one item used to mean one story, whoever wrote it. `ArticleFacts.phase?: "onset" | "worsening" | "easing" | "over"` (v53) is where in its own arc the thing being reported stands, so a flood is written one way on the first morning and another in the week it recedes. All three are absent on every item written before v53, and absence reads as the wire carrying it without a masthead, no second filing, and a piece about something with no arc. `FeedItem.date?: GameDate` (v52) is the day the item was filed: `applyFeed` (`record-effects.ts`) stamps it from `state.calendar` on every feed write, whatever the producer wrote, so an "as of" label reads the day rather than a weekday it has to guess. An item filed before v52 has none and reads by its week. `FeedItem.kind` is `"npc-action" | "operator" | "drift" | "press" | "conflict" | "condition" | "governance" | "pursuit" | "bloc"` — `press` is the papers' reaction to a big player answer, written per scenario reply and applied through the same `FeedEffect` as every other feed write. `campaign.player.flags` is governance memory: booleans stamped by committed scenario replies (`scenario:<id>` fired markers, `staged:<class>:<turn>` and `staged-severity:<severity>:<turn>` pacing memory for the director, `flagset:<flag>:<turn>` age markers for `agedAtLeast` callbacks, `desk:closed:<turn>`, and deed flags like `cleared-the-square`) that scenario conditions read, so past answers gate future scenarios. The platform writes there too: `plank:<id>` at the seal and `plank:<id>:kept` or `plank:<id>:broken` when a pledge settles, each with its marker, so a callback can gate on a promise and age it. `press:hostile` is an engine-state flag (`press.ts`, recomputed weekly) that content may gate on; `ENGINE_STATE_FLAGS` in `scenario-deeds.ts` makes `engineOwnedFlag` treat it as bookkeeping, so `flag-condition-never-set` does not refuse a gate on it and deed metrics do not count it.
