Two derived world facts also stand flags there rather than fields of their own, on the same contract: `condition:<kind>:<nation>` while a condition runs, and `coalition:<nation>` while enough capitals are alarmed by one state to count as a coalition (`blocs.ts`). The condition flag is recomputed each turn; the coalition flag is hysteretic by design — it needs `MIN_COALITION` alarmed capitals to go up and stands until nobody is alarmed at all, so a coalition that thins is not struck off and re-announced. `coalition-since:<subject>:<turn>` (self-dated) records when a coalition against a subject formed and is cleared with the coalition. Both exist as flags so authored content can gate on them through the record it already reads.

`campaign.relationships` (the `RelationshipGraph`, keyed `"${from}:${to}"`) is not uniformly legible to the player: `selectRelationshipsFrom` returns the true directed graph, but `hasIntelOnRelationship` / `selectDiscoverableRelationshipsFrom` gate what the player's own services can currently read of a relationship between two *other* nations — the player's own relationship with any nation is always legible, an NPC-to-NPC one only once `intelCapability` outreads the pair's average `counterIntel`. See ARCHITECTURE.md §7 "World board, roster, and dossiers" for the full rule; this is a live computation over `NationStats.security`, not a stored field on `Relationship` itself, so the schema shape is unchanged.

**`FoundingConfig`** (`campaign.founding`) records the five divergence sources fixed at founding, and is the authoritative answer to "which nation is the player governing":

| Field | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `nation` | `NationId` of the nation the player governs. Every screen and rule reads the player's nation from here rather than inferring it. |
| `name` / `title` | The player character's name, and the title their charter gives them. |
| `charter` | `CharterId` — determines cabinet seats, decision constraint, and removal mechanism. |
| `cradle` | The founding placement; it overwrites the nation's `geo.region` and `geo.neighbors`. |
| `compacts` | The `OrgId`s joined at founding, after conflict and cradle-eligibility checks. |
| `cabinet` | `RoleId → PersonId` for every seat the charter defines, **including the lead chair** — that one is the player's own composed `Person`, written into the map at seal time rather than drafted from the pool. |
| `leader` | The `LeaderDraft` the player authored: name, age, background, route to office, ideology vector, party, and the optional `homeState` (a `StateId`: narrows the President's birthplace to that state and is what a running mate is balanced against), `presentation` (picks the face drawn for them), `pronouns` (`"she/her" | "he/him" | "they/them"`, only when stated; nothing infers them), `portraitRef` (the face the player built, as a `face:` reference whose presentation must agree with `presentation`; a draft saved before the face builder holds a facet reference from the old cast, whose age band is replaced by the President's own), and a device-local `portraitUri`. Retained past the seal because the composed `Person` carries the *numbers* and this carries the life that produced them. |
| `flag` | The `FlagDesign` the administration was founded under (§5f). Stored as the design rather than as an image: it draws at every size from the same four fields, it diffs like the rest of the founding, and a save carries four ids instead of an asset. |
| `platform` | The `PlankId`s the President ran on, in the order chosen. Optional on the type; `normalizeCampaign` fills `[]`. The pledges made on them are `campaign.pledges`. |
| `staff` | The `WhiteHouseStaff` seated at the oath: the Chief of Staff, the National Security Adviser and the Press Secretary, chosen or, for a post left open, the best co-partisan fit (Hill benches, the foreign, defense and intelligence benches, and journalists respectively). Records who was seated, not only what was chosen. `INST-PRESIDENCY` is led by the Chief of Staff and `INST-NSC` by the National Security Adviser. |
| `mandate` | The `FoundingMandate` (`landslide`, `comfortable`, `narrow`, `popular-vote-loss`) when the founding chose one; absent when it took the seeded default (`openingMandate`). It sets the opening Congress's coattails. |
| `leadRole` | The charter's own `cabinetRoles[0]` — the head-of-state seat, whose `Person` is the player's own authored persona rather than an appointee. `cabinet.ts`'s loyalty/ambition consequences (leaks, resignations, internal rivals) read this to exempt the player from their own mechanic. |
| `leaderTable` | The as-of date of the leader table the foreign cast was drawn against (`LEADER_TABLE_AS_OF`), stamped at the seal. Optional, and never filled in on load: a save founded before the table (or against another edition of it) seated leaders the table never shaped, so for it (player-core `world/leaders.ts`) the Leaders roster, the country file and the person file title its lead chairs by the charter, and no provenance chip claims its leaders were modeled on real governments. No save version moves. |

**The player is composed, not drawn** (`packages/engine/src/leader.ts`). Everyone else in a campaign comes out of a seeded roll (`people.ts`); the player does not. `FoundingChoices.leader` is a `LeaderDraft` — a biography — and `composeLeader(draft, nation, nationName, leadRole)` turns it into an ordinary `Person` the same zod schema validates, with **no RNG anywhere in the file**. That determinism is load-bearing: the founding screen draws these readings live while the player is still choosing, and a preview of a roll it will not reproduce is a lie. The composed person carries a real **`education` and `career`**, derived from the background's authored `formation` and the age the player chose — the school by the background's position in that nation's own catalogue, the span from the age the career begins to the age they are now. Without it the President was the one figure in the cast whose career the game could not describe, on the very screens that sold that career as the choice which shaped them. `FoundingChoices.cabinet` therefore holds only the *appointable* chairs (`appointableRoles(charter)` = `cabinetRoles.slice(1)`) while a founding is in progress; `sealFounding` appends the composed person to the pool and writes them into the lead chair.

The derivation is authored in the dataset (`packages/world/src/dataset/leader-paths.ts`), not hard-coded:

| Input | What it is worth |
|---|---|
| `LeaderBackground` | The career. Supplies the five base readings, an anchor trait, a plausible age window, the opening clause of the bio, and a **`formation`** — the same life stated in the vocabulary every other person carries (`{ archetype, field, postgraduate, startAge }`). Eight US backgrounds are authored (`path-senator`, `path-house`, `path-governor`, `path-vice-president`, `path-general`, `path-business`, `path-cabinet`, `path-mayor`), and a paired test asserts **no background dominates another on all five readings** — the moment one does, the choice has collapsed into a right answer. Each also buys exactly one opening hook through a writer that already exists (`founding-leader-hooks.ts`): a Senate or House leadership disposition, the home governor, the home city's lean, the public's prosperity judgment, or a point of opening standing, readiness or stability. |
| `LeaderRoute` | How that career reached the chair: `route-primaries`, `route-convention` or `route-insurgent`. A signed shift on the five readings, an optional second trait, the closing clause of the bio, and one hook (the own caucus's leadership for the primaries and the insurgency, in opposite directions; the room for a convention). The election-result and succession routes are retired: how decisively the election was won is the `mandate` choice. |
| Age | Seniority within the background's own window: up to `+0.08` competence and `−0.06` ambition at the top of it. Clamped into the window when the background changes, so a career switch can never strand the player outside their own biography. |
| Ideology | `ideologyConviction` is the mean absolute distance from center across the three axes. Conviction buys `+0.08` integrity and `+0.05` charisma, costs `0.04` competence, and above `0.66` adds `principled`. It is also the person's actual `IdeologyVector`, which `counsel.ts` and `dossier.ts` already read — the three sliders used to be stored as an inert `leaderTraits` triple no rule consumed. |

The composed person is eligible for the lead chair **and nothing else**, so they can never appear on a ministry's shortlist or be appointed under themselves.

Founding itself (`founding.ts`) is pure and seed-keyed: `validateFounding` returns in-fiction `FoundingRejection`s and `sealFounding` produces the `Campaign`. The same seed and choices always seal a structurally identical campaign, and `previewFounding` (player-core) validates and then seals, so the founding screen's preview equals the seal by construction. `FoundingChoices` is `{ cabinet, leader, runningMate?, platform?, firstHundredDays?, staff?, mandate? }` plus the legacy fields (`nationId`, `nationName`, `name`, `title`, `charterId`, `cradleId`, `compactIds`, `flag`), which are now optional: `resolveFoundingFields` fills any left out from the dataset (the first playable nation, the charter it names, the first cradle, no compacts, the default banner, the lead chair's own title), and a founding that omits them seals the same campaign as one that names them. Compacts, cradle and flag are no longer judged, because the United States answers each of them itself and a refusal nobody can act on is not a choice. The refusal subjects are the cabinet chairs, `leader`, `runningMate` (not among the eligible members of Congress, or not of the President's party), each White House staff role id (not on that post's bench, or across the aisle), `mandate`, and the platform's (`platform`, `first-hundred-days`, or the plank id).

**The opening position** (`opening-position.ts`) is what `sealFounding` sets `player.metrics` from. A campaign used to open on its nation's own four readings and nothing else — the same position whoever the player was and whoever they had seated, which made five screens of cabinet choice decoration. `openingPosition(nation, cabinet, leadRole)` returns `{ baseline, contributions, metrics }`: the country supplies the baseline (`reserves`, `politics.regimeStability`, `military.readiness`, `softPower.diplomaticReach`) and the people about to run it supply the difference, with the in-fiction reason for every point of it. It is pure and total, so the founding screen recomputes it live while the player is still choosing. Mandate is derived last through `recomputeMandate` and never contributed to directly.

Three rules keep it honest. A chair pushes on the metric its **portfolio** owns (treasury minister → treasury scaled by the nation's own reserves, defense → military readiness, foreign → standing, interior/intelligence → stability; any other seat a charter defines gets a smaller generic administrative push). A person's **disposition** is weighted heavier in the lead chair than in a ministry, and is confined to `PORTFOLIO_REACH` — the metrics that chair's holder actually has authority over, plus stability, which is every minister's business. Without that confinement a cautious treasury minister raised military readiness. The head of government is exempt, because they chair the room and speak for the country. And a movement below `MATERIAL_DELTA` is dropped from the list *and* the sum, so what the player is shown adds up to what is applied rather than trailing rows that render as "+0".

**Appointments** (`appointments.ts`) add one effect kind and no new stored shape. `CabinetEffect` (`{ kind: "cabinet", role, person? }`) is the only writer of `player.cabinet` outside founding; omitting `person` empties the chair, and the role's key is deleted rather than mapped to `undefined` so the saved shape matches the written one. It is deliberately separate from `PersonEffect`: the seat and the person are two facts, and `dismissMinister` and the confirmation writer (`seatOfficer`) always move both together so the cabinet map and the cast cannot disagree. Filling a chair is a nomination (see the appointment pool below); `appointMinister` and `projectAppointment` are retired. `selectCabinetSeats` reads the charter's chairs from `founding.cabinet` — live occupancy (`selectCabinet`) loses the key when a chair empties, so a surface listing "the cabinet" from it would drop a dismissed minister's ministry out of the government entirely instead of standing it vacant. `selectAppointableFor` is the shortlist: the campaign's own living, unseated, eligible nationals, best competence first, so waiting cannot conjure a better candidate than founding offered. Both acts are `actor: "player"`; dismissal costs standing scaled by the minister's charisma and turns an ambitious one (`ambition ≥ 0.65`, cabinet.ts's own challenge floor) into a marked rival faction, and appointment swings standing on the appointee's competence against the middle of the range.

**The relationship ledger** (`state-ties.ts`, `ties.ts`). `PersonRuntime.tie?: Tie` is what one person holds toward the President: directed, absent until something the President did reaches them, and read as `EMPTY_TIE` when absent. A `Tie` is `{ rapport, trust, favors, grudges }`: `rapport` is warmth on 0..1 with 0.5 neutral (`NEUTRAL_RAPPORT`; 0..1 rather than −1..1 so the Hill's earlier dispositions carried over unchanged), `trust` is whether the President's word has held, 0..1 with 0.5 neutral. A `Favor` is `{ id, direction: "owed-by-president" | "owed-to-president", what, openedTurn, dueTurn?, status: "open" | "called" | "repaid" | "defaulted", settledTurn?, billId?, actId?, post?, lobbyId?, reservedTreasury?, account? }`. A `TieGrudge` is `{ cause, weight, turn }`; a public appeal that misses writes one, and it is stored but not yet decayed. The one writer is the `tie` effect, `{ kind: "tie", person, cause, rapport?: { set } | { delta }, trust?: { delta }, openFavor?, settleFavor?: { id, status, turn }, grudge? }`, whose parts apply in that order; `apply` refuses one with an empty `cause`, an unknown person or a settlement of a favor the tie does not hold, and a delta is clamped to 0..1 and kept to three places. Founding seats the Hill through the same fold (`withTie`). Readers go through `tieOf`, `rapportOf`, `figureRapport` and `openFavorsOwedByPresident` (oldest first). Members of Congress are the first holders; nothing else writes a tie yet.

**Loyalty in play** (`loyalty.ts`) adds one runtime field and one effect field, and no new stored shape. `PersonRuntime.loyaltyAtAppointment` is the reading a chair's holder arrived with — stamped at founding and by the confirmation writer, cleared by `dismissMinister`, absent for anyone never seated — and it exists so a surface can state a direction rather than only a level. `PersonEffect.loyaltyShift` is a signed move folded into `base.loyalty` rather than stored beside it: every rule that prices loyalty reads that one field, so a shift kept alongside would make a minister read one way to the roster and another to the rule that removes them. It is clamped to 0..1 at application, stored at full precision, and rounded only where it is drawn. A save written before this carries neither key and loads as a cabinet that has not moved, which is exactly what it was. `PersonEffect.competenceShift?` is the same kind of signed move, folded into `base.competence`.

**The appropriation** (`budget.ts`) adds one stored field and one effect kind. `player.budget` is `BudgetAccount → share of GDP`, one entry per coercive instrument (`diplomatic`, `economic`, `covert`, `military` — `assistance` is funded per act rather than held as an establishment), each held in `[0, ACCOUNT_CAP]` where the cap is 4% of national income a year. Shares are stored rather than sums because that is what a budget is — a claim on national income, so a country that grows funds the same establishment more easily without anybody re-voting it. A founding opens at `sustainingBudget(runtime)`: every account at exactly what holding it costs, so an administration that has voted nothing is paying its predecessor's bills rather than dismantling the state. `BudgetEffect` (`{ kind: "budget", allocation }`) is the only writer outside founding and replaces rather than accumulates, mirroring `PolicyEffect`. What the appropriation *does* each week lands through ordinary absolute `nationStat` effects over a closed twelve-path vocabulary (`BUDGET_STAT_RANGES`) plus a `metric` effect for the net treasury movement — so the estimates are not a second mutation route, only a second author of the one that exists. `fiscalPhase` (`fiscal.ts`) is a second author of absolute `nationStat` writes on the player nation, over a different four paths — `economy.debt`, `economy.gdp`, `economy.gdpPerCapita` and `sovereignRating` — settling the week's balance into the country's own books rather than the establishment's twelve-path vocabulary above.

**Policy** (`policy.ts`) adds one stored field and one effect kind. `player.policies` is `PolicyDomain → stanceId` — one stance held in every domain, always, including the inherited one, because "continuity" is a position rather than the absence of one. Stance *ids* are stored rather than the stances themselves: the catalogue is code, and a save carrying a copy of it would reload against a build that had since rebalanced it. `PolicyEffect` (`{ kind: "policy", domain, stance }`) is the only writer outside founding and replaces rather than accumulates, mirroring `CabinetEffect`.

Six domains, four stances each, one of them inherited. Every stance carries `perTurn` movement, a one-off `enactment` price, an `alignment` pull, and `nationEffects`, signed per-turn movement on the **nation's own stats**; `treasuryOfGdp` is a fraction of the nation's own GDP rather than a flat sum, so a tax rise is worth proportionally the same to a large economy and a small one. A test asserts every non-inherited stance both returns something and costs something — the system is a set of trades, not a menu of upgrades. A program that borrows counts as charged, because the interest on what it adds comes out of the President's budget authority. `perTurn.stability` is a level rather than a rate: order settles back a tenth of the way each week (`STABILITY_REVERSION`), so a stance holds order ten times its weekly figure from its rest, and each is authored as a tenth of the level it holds. No fiscal stance carries order or standing.

Three rules make it a decision rather than a purchase. **A domain is legislable only if the charter seats the chair responsible** (`legislableDomains`) — which is the charter's own cabinet list read honestly rather than a rule written about juntas: a government with no treasury minister has nobody who could sign a budget, and so has no fiscal policy at all. **Delivery scales with the responsible minister's competence** (`executionQuality`, falling to `CARETAKER_QUALITY` on a vacant chair), applied to what a program returns and never to what it costs — scaling both would make an incompetent appointment a way of buying a cheaper policy. **Alignment is pulled once, on enactment**, not per turn, because `accumulateAlignment` models the sum of a player's decisions and a standing policy is one decision that keeps working.

**Policy teeth.** `nationEffects` is what stops a stance being decor. Without it a program moved five numbers on the player's dashboard and left the country the rest of the simulation reads — conditions, conflicts, instrument odds, NPC assessments — exactly as it found it, which made every stance in a domain the same kind of thing at a different magnitude. `PolicyStatPath` is a closed union of sixteen stat paths with the schema range each is held inside (`POLICY_STAT_RANGES`), so a stance cannot name a path that does not exist and a program running for four hundred turns cannot write a campaign the validator would reject. `policyNationEffects(runtime, program)` sums every domain's movement **per path before building any effect** — two domains can push the same stat in one week and `nationStat` writes absolutes, so one effect each would silently let the last one win — and takes the nation rather than reading the player's, because the same function is what will give an NPC's program its consequences. Competence scales what a program delivers and never what it costs, the same asymmetry `movementToDeltas` applies: a weak service watches less and censors just as much.

The contract is enforced the way the authored-field registry is, in `policy-teeth.test.ts`: every non-inherited stance must declare at least one path, at least one of its paths must be one the consumer registry marks `readBy` (a live rule, not a debt), the four stances in a domain must have genuinely different consequence shapes, and the paths that are written-but-not-yet-read — `politics.pressFreedom` and `energy.importDependence` — are **pinned exactly**, so writing a stat cannot be laundered as something reading it. That pinned pair is also the honest statement of what the information instrument still owes.

`selectPolicyOutlook` is the whole program with its per-turn worth, and `policyPhase` applies that same computation rather than a second one — so what the policy screen promises and what the turn charges cannot disagree. Policy reaches the score by the ordinary route: the per-turn movement lands on the other metrics, and they reach the headline through the public — `approval` is derived in `opinion.ts` from the six judgments, never billed directly. It reaches the player's *reputation* through alignment, which feeds `removalPressureFromAlignment` and `npcTrustAffinity`.

**`ConsequenceCard`** (`packages/engine/src/consequences.ts`) is the runtime shape of an authored aftermath, and adds no stored shape either. It is a TypeScript type in the *engine* rather than a zod schema in `packages/content`, for the same reason `ConversationScript` is: the dependency-direction lint forbids the engine importing content, so the engine owns the runtime type and authored documents compile down to it. Everything on it is JSON-safe data — no functions, unlike the engine's own internal risk definitions — so a card can arrive from a bundled pack, a remote manifest, or a save without losing anything.

`ConsequenceCard = { id, instrument, rungBands, valence, riskAffinity?, conditions?, weight, once?, beats, effects, sets?, remembered? }`. `rungBands` is `"low" | "mid" | "high"` (rungs 1–2 / 3 / 4–5) — a card is authored against a *kind* of act, not a level. `valence` is `"success" | "partial" | "failure"`. `riskAffinity` names branch ids from `RISK_IDS` and confines the card to those failures. `beats` are one to three lines substituting `{{actor}}` and `{{target}}` only, so a card cannot narrate a fact the simulation did not produce. `effects` is a **closed** recipe vocabulary — `metric` (with optional `scaleBySeverity`), `relationship` (directed deltas), `grudge`, and `feed` (headline only; the engine decides whether it reads as press or as intelligence) — deliberately excluding `nationStat`, `cabinet`, `person`, `operator` and `removal`, the effect kinds that change the shape of a campaign. There is no field for visibility anywhere on the type, so a card can never expose a covert action the odds did not price.

`once` and per-campaign memory ride the existing flag record as `consequence:<id>` (`consequenceFlag()`), alongside whatever `sets` names — so a drawn aftermath is governance memory of exactly the same kind as a committed scenario reply, gating later scenarios through the mechanism that already exists. Zero `Campaign` shape change.

**Instruments** (`instruments.ts`) add no new stored shape: `attemptInstrument` reads live `NationStats` plus geography and returns either an `EffectPacket` for `apply()` or a rejection naming the specific missing capability. Player and NPC actions travel the same ladder code. `InstrumentId` is `diplomatic | economic | covert | military | assistance` — five ladders of five rungs. `assistance` is help rather than pressure: it is the consumer of `softPower.aidReceived` (which the authored-field registry had carried as a debt owed to "aid instrument"), it writes `softPower.aidGiven` on the giver, and it is the only path by which one nation's act lowers the `severity` of another's `NationCondition`. It stands a `security-guarantee:<actor>:<target>` flag in the same governance-flag vocabulary as `embargo:` and `severed-relations:`. A relief shipment into a country living through a condition opens an eight-week `aid` commitment named against it (`addressedCondition`), so a lapse while the condition stands draws the lapse grudge; no other rung names a condition. A stabilization package writes a turn-stamped `currency-backstop:<actor>:<target>` flag, which `backstopRelief` reads. `BudgetAccount` stays the four coercive instruments (`BUDGET_ACCOUNTS ... satisfies readonly InstrumentId[]`): aid is bought per act, not held as an establishment, so `player.budget` is unchanged and no save version moves.

**`campaign.godMode`** (`GodModeState`, optional) is `{ enabled: true, enabledTurn, actions, branchPoints }`. It has no `false` state on purpose: a campaign is either untouched, in which case the field is absent, or touched, in which case the record is permanent. `apply()` enforces the permanence — `carryGodMode` re-attaches the field to the state a packet produced, so no effect and no future handler can drop it — and increments `actions` for every packet with `source: "godmode"`, including the one that enabled it. `enabledTurn` is what a retrospective needs to separate the part of the term that was played from the part that was authored; `branchPoints` records turns the timeline was rewound from (written by the `godMode` effect; the rewind itself is designed, as deterministic replay from the seed plus the decision log, and not implemented). Read it through `isGodMode` / `selectGodMode` / `isRankable` (`state.ts`) rather than reaching for the field: `isRankable` is the single rule achievements and leaderboards ask, so the exclusion is not a convention each future scoreboard has to remember.

**`godMode` is absent, not empty, on a campaign nobody has amended.** It is written only when the fiction is governed from outside itself, so its absence is a fact the campaign is entitled to state rather than a field that failed to load.

God actions add no new mutation path and no effect kinds beyond one. `packages/engine/src/god.ts` is a builder module: every function returns an ordinary `EffectPacket` with `source: "godmode"` and `actor: "developer"`, whose effects are the same `metric` / `relationship` / `nationStat` / `city` / `flag` / `cabinet` / `conflict` kinds ordinary play uses, so a god edit is clamped by the same schema ranges and lands in the ledger as an attributable `ActionRecord` — `selectPlayerLedger` stays the account of what the *administration* decided. The single new effect kind is **`GodModeEffect`** `{ kind: "godMode", enable?, branchPoint? }`, which records that god mode was entered or a timeline branched and nothing else; there is no way to express leaving. Settling a war by decree routes through `conflicts.ts`'s `settleByAgreement` rather than writing a status directly, because a conflict ended outside the terminal transition would carry a status no consequence rule can read. `editCity` is a `CityEffect` through `apply()`.

## 7c. Content documents

Scenarios are JSON documents, not code: `ScenarioDef` and `ScenarioPack` (`packages/content/src/scenario.ts`, zod) define the shape the generation pipeline emits, `studio-cli` validates, delivery serves, and the app casts against the live board. The full description lives in `ARCHITECTURE.md` §5; the shape is authoritative in the zod schema itself, which is what the publish gate parses with.

**`dilemmaShape` records what the decision *is*, underneath what the scenario is about.** Required, one of `credulity-bet` | `containment` | `retaliation` | `allocation` | `disclosure` | `loyalty-test` | `exposure` | `foot-in-the-door`. `class` says which desk the week arrives at; the shape says which argument the room is having, and the two are near-independent: a loyalty test reads the same whether the person protected is a general or a registrar.

**These eight were measured, not reasoned.** An earlier eight, inherited from the generation brief's prose doctrine, was encoded without ever being tested for reproducibility. Tested — three independent readers relabeling the same 56 shipped scenarios blind — it reached Cohen's kappa **0.48**, meaning the vocabulary did not survive contact with a second reader. Four generations later the set reaches **0.83**. It is the same size as the original and half the same members:

| | |
|---|---|
| Survived | `credulity-bet` (reproduced 6/6, 6/6, 6/6 — the only shape that never wavered), `loyalty-test`, `foot-in-the-door`, `exposure` (reworked from `legibility-bet`) |
| Cut, never replicated | `legibility-bet` (1/6, 1/6, 0/6), `sacred-trade`, `time-trade`, `mirror` |
| Cut, never once selected | `dirty-hands` — across three readers and 56 scenarios, zero uses |
| Added, were missing | `containment`, `retaliation`, `allocation`, `disclosure` |

`dirty-hands` is the sharpest finding. The corpus's voice leans on moral compromise constantly, but what the writing actually contains is the *inverse* of Walzer's structure: the harsh option pushes harm onto a population and the humane one is paid from the treasury. That is `containment`, and it had no name at all — which is why 62 scenarios had been filed under a label no independent reader would choose.

**The ordering is load-bearing.** A writer or auditor works *down* the list and takes the first shape whose test passes. That rule alone moved agreement from 0.48 to 0.83, because it decides the cases where two labels are both defensible. The tests live in the generation brief (rule 26c) and are quoted there in full.

One audit reads the field: `dilemma-shape-monoculture` (warning, when one shape holds more than half a batch of six or more). There is no missing-shape rule — the schema refuses a scenario without one.

**Corpus balance after the re-label** (247 scenarios, even share 12.5%): `containment` 22.3%, `disclosure` 17.0%, `loyalty-test` 15.8%, `allocation` 13.0%, `credulity-bet` 13.0%, `retaliation` 9.3%, `foot-in-the-door` 8.9%, `exposure` 0.8%. Flatter than the old vocabulary's 25.1% peak, but `exposure` at two scenarios is thin enough to be worth watching — it is the shape most often pre-empted by an earlier test, and the candidate to merge or retire if it stays unused.

**`motifs` records the recurring IMAGE a scenario is built from, underneath the argument `dilemmaShape` names.** Optional, 1-3 values from a closed enum: `uncorroborated-source` | `agency-non-confirmation` | `broken-chain-of-custody` | `border-crossing` | `quiet-transfer` | `divided-public-perception` | `closing-claim-of-ownership`. Two credulity bets can share nothing in prose and both still turn on a single uncorroborated informant — a resemblance invisible to `dilemmaShape` (same argument label either way) and invisible to the shingle-based `template-overlap` detector (paraphrase, not copying), which is the gap this label closes.

**Measured, then backfilled.** The seven-value vocabulary — read directly off the images a cross-batch review of `mixed-100-01` found reused near-verbatim — was checked before being trusted with a blind three-reader study over 40 scenarios sampled across every class: mean pairwise Jaccard set-agreement **0.892**. (Jaccard over label sets, not Cohen's kappa — `motifs` is a multi-label 0-3 tag, not `dilemmaShape`'s single closed choice, so the two numbers measure the same thing, reliability, on different math and are not directly comparable.) All seven entries held: six drew at least one real hit in the sample and five reached 3-of-3 reader unanimity on at least one scenario; the seventh, `broken-chain-of-custody`, drew zero hits in the 40-scenario sample but is not thin by the same logic `exposure` is above — it went on to label 6 scenarios once the full 275 were read. No entries were cut.

The full 275-scenario shipped corpus is backfilled: **46 scenarios (16.7%) carry at least one motif**, the rest correctly carry none — most scenarios have no recurring image, which is the design, not a gap. Counts: `uncorroborated-source` 15, `border-crossing` 14, `agency-non-confirmation` 8, `broken-chain-of-custody` 6, `quiet-transfer` 3, `closing-claim-of-ownership` 3, `divided-public-perception` 2.

One audit reads the field: `motif-monoculture` (warning, when one motif holds more than 40% of the scenarios in a batch that *carry* `motifs`, once 8 or more do — the base is however many scenarios chose to tag one, not the batch size, since the field is optional). The ceiling and floor are provisional in the same way the other pool-scale rules are: untuned against a real pool. It is not decorative: run over the real shipped `migration` shard, 5 of that shard's 6 labeled scenarios share `border-crossing` — the exact reproduction, as a countable fact rather than a remembered manual finding, of the "the crossing" duplication first found by reading batches side by side. Run over a realistic 119-scenario cross-class slice of the real corpus (`migration`, `foreign-crisis`, `hostage`, `war`, `justice`, `unrest`), the rule fires for real: 13 of 23 labeled scenarios in that slice share `border-crossing`.

**The cast is how a scenario finds its counterpart, and two picks read the wars rather than the relationships.** `ScenarioCast` is a discriminated union on `pick`: `tensest` (the player's most charged edge this turn), `closest` (the firmest friend), `band` (any nation matching an archetype / power tier / military tier / stability band), `nation` (a declared set-piece id), `warCounterpart`, `belligerent` (with an optional `side` of `aggressor` / `defender`), `none` (trouble at home — the counterpart slot points back at the player's own nation), and `stricken` (the counterpart is at home: whoever the board says is bearing the condition the scenario is about). `stricken` binds nothing yet — the stricken place is a state rather than a country, and until that resolution exists the honest answer is that nothing is bound, which leaves the scenario unreachable rather than cast onto the wrong place. It exists because trouble at home had exactly one cast, `none`, so a week about a disaster could name no governor, no mayor and no stricken state. `warCounterpart` binds the principal opposite the player in their hottest active conflict; `belligerent` binds a principal of the hottest active conflict the player is *not* in, `side` naming which chair and, without it, the player's own tension deciding. Both sort by intensity and break ties on the conflict id, then on nation id, so a save binds identically everywhere. **A war scenario must cast `warCounterpart`, not `tensest` beside a `warState` gate**: tension is re-ranked from live state every draw and belligerence is not, so the pair can gate open on one war and bind the counterpart of another — the front week put in front of a country the government is at peace with. Both war picks bind nothing when no such conflict exists, which is an unreachable cast exactly like a `band` with no match, so `auditReachability` catches content using them without the matching `warState` condition. `previewBoards` (`content/src/preview-board.ts`) carries a board for each: `preview-6` opens a war the player is in, `preview-7` a war between two other nations.

**The outlet cast.** `ScenarioCast` gains `{ pick: "outlet", outlet?: <PRESS_OUTLETS id> }`: the counterpart is one of the game's fictional mastheads, named or (unnamed) the national desk coldest to the administration this week, whatever its format. The country slot binds home, as for `none`, so no reply reaches a foreign government. `{{outlet}}` renders the masthead's name (a board token beside `{{state}}` and `{{sinceDeed}}`), and the parser refuses the token on any other cast. A transmission on an outlet week is credited to the masthead.

**The pulse and commodity gates.** Six condition kinds read the player's own country in bands, never figures (`scenario-pulse.ts`): `inflationBand` (`below-target` | `near-target` | `elevated` | `high`, against the Fed's 2 percent), `unemploymentBand` (`tight` | `normal` | `slack` | `severe`), `costOfLivingBand` (since the oath: `easing` | `steady` | `rising` | `straining`), `crimeTrend` (since the oath: `falling` | `steady` | `rising`), `cyclePhase` (the engine's four phases) and `commodityBand` (`{ commodity, in: [slumped | normal | elevated | spiking] }` over the engine's own commodity ids — `COMMODITY_IDS` in `packages/engine/src/commodities.ts`: crude, natural-gas, lng, wheat, corn, soybeans, potash, nitrogen, copper, lithium, rare-earths). Each is `{ kind, in: [<band>, ...] }`. Thresholds are planning values except the 2 percent target. The commodity index is read through the engine's own `selectCommodities(campaign)`, so the gate reads the market model the moment it stores one; a commodity absent from that record reads its reference level (`referenceIndex`, `commodities.ts`). The reachability gate builds one board per band a batch asks for (`preview-board-pulse.ts`). These kinds, and the outlet cast, owe `CONTENT_SCHEMA_VERSION` 3 at the first publish that carries one.

**`conditionActive` means running.** A `conditionActive` gate is met only while a matching condition is active; bounds then read its severity. (Before this, an unbounded gate read the severity of nothing, zero, and held on every board.)

**Conditions are what makes a scenario a decision the board earned.** `ScenarioCondition` is a discriminated union on `kind`; `ScenarioDef.conditions` is an array of them and every one must hold for a scenario to be live. Bounds are `atLeast` / `atMost`, both optional, both inclusive.

| `kind` | Fields | Reads |
|---|---|---|
| `metric` | `metric` (`approval` / `treasury` / `stability` / `militaryReadiness` / `standing`), bounds | The player's own five readings. `treasury` is budget authority in USD millions — absolute, floored at zero, capped at the carry share of the discretionary appropriation, and never nought-to-one |
| `alignment` | `axis` (`economicLeftRight` / `authLibertarian` / `nationalistGlobalist`), bounds | What the player has said in conversations |
| `relationship` | `field` (`trust` / `alignment` / `tension` / `tradeDependence` / `militaryThreat`), bounds | The edge with the **cast counterpart** |
| `counterpartTrait` | `trait` (the eight `NationTraits` keys), bounds | The counterpart's personality |
| `cabinetAttribute` | `roleHint`, `attribute`, bounds | Whoever actually holds that chair — a vacant or dead one satisfies nothing |
| `flag` | `flag`, `is`, optional `agedAtLeast` | Governance memory, and how cold the deed is |
| `turn` | bounds | Pacing |
| `removalPressure` | bounds | `selectRemoval(campaign).pressure` |
| `charterInstitution` | `institution`, `present` | Whether the founding charter seats that body at all |
| `warState` | `role` (`any` / `aggressor` / `defender` / `none`) | The player's own part in the live conflicts; `none` means a war they are staying *out* of |
| `conditionActive` | optional `condition`, bounds | The severity of the worst matching `NationCondition` at home |
| `coalition` | `against` (`player` / `counterpart`), `present` | The engine's coalition flag (`coalition:<nationId>`, `blocs.ts`) resolved against the player's own nation or the bound counterpart |
| `sharedMembership` | `present` | Whether the player and the counterpart hold any `Nation.memberships` org in common |
| `policyStance` | `domain` (`ScenarioPolicyDomain`, mirrored from the engine's `POLICY_DOMAINS`), `stance` (a program id), `active` | `selectPolicyStance(campaign, domain)` — what this administration legislated, as against what it said |
| `operatorPressure` | `field` (`insurgency` / `protest`), bounds | The player nation's `security.insurgencyPressure` / `politics.protestPressure` |
| `press` | `measure` (`standing` / `silenceWeeks` / `hostileShare` / `outlet`), optional `outlet`, bounds | What the press is doing: `measure` is `standing` (0..1), `silenceWeeks` (weeks since the administration last answered a question), `hostileShare` (share of desks turned hostile, 0..1) or `outlet` (where one named desk stands, 0..1; `outlet` names the desk and an id not on the roster never satisfies), bounded by `atLeast`/`atMost`; read through the engine's `pressReading` |
| `geography` | `field` (`coastal` / `landlocked`), `is` | The player nation's `geo.coastline` |
| `revealedPreference` | `trait` (`concedesUnderPressure` / `escalatesWhenTested` / `honorsFriends`), bounds | The engine's `revealedPreference(campaign)` profile — what the world has read off the player's own record. Floored on evidence, not on the scalar: see below |
| `stateGrievance` | optional `signature`, `scope` (`locus` / `any`), bounds | Signed net mark weight via `grievanceReading` (−1..1). Omitted signature = any mark; a slug filters. `scope: "locus"` reads `locus.state` (audit error `ungrounded-state-grievance` if missing); `scope: "any"` takes the most extreme \|reading\| across states. The eighteenth condition kind. |
| `cohortStrain` | `cohort` (`CohortId`), optional `cause`, bounds | Signed net strain weight (−1..1) on `campaign.cohorts[cohort]`. Omitted cause = any strain; a slug filters (including `omitted:` neglect). The nineteenth condition kind. |
| `institutionState` | `institution` (an `INSTITUTION_CATALOG` id, refused at parse time if it is not), `field` (`delivery` / `politicization` / `backlog` / `vacantDays` / `openMatters`), bounds | The live state of one body of the executive branch, off `campaign.institutions[institution]`: `delivery` is the derived `implementationQuality` and `politicization` is 0..1; `backlog`, `vacantDays` and `openMatters` are counts (`openMatters` via `selectMattersFor` × `isMatterOpen`). Unmet on a board carrying no runtime for that body. The twentieth condition kind, in `scenario-institutions.ts`. |
| `oversightMatter` | optional `origin` (`inspector-general` / `committee` / `special-counsel`), optional `stage` (`opened` / `gathering` / `reported`), optional `public`, optional `obstructed`, optional `atLeast` (default 1) | How many **open** oversight matters against this administration match every stated facet, counted government-wide off `selectOpenMatters`. `closed` is not a stage the schema carries — the census counts open matters, and content about a shut file gates on the flag its arc stamps. `direction` is likewise absent: `MatterDirection` has a `directed` value nothing in the engine ever opens. The twenty-first condition kind, in `scenario-oversight.ts`. |
| `shutdown` | optional `is` (default `true`), optional `weeksAtLeast` | Whether the government is closed, via `inShutdown` / `selectShutdown`. `weeksAtLeast` reads `ShutdownReading.weeks` and implies `is: true`; `is: false` is the gate for a week that would read absurdly during a lapse. |
| `appropriation` | optional `onPriorYear`, optional `weeksLeftAtMost` — at least one required at parse time | `campaign.congress.appropriation`. `onPriorYear` is the president who got no budget at all; `weeksLeftAtMost` is `throughTurn − turn`, the run-up to the deadline, which goes negative once it has passed. `Appropriation.priorYear` (the lines a floor struck out) is deliberately not exposed: no board a first-year campaign reaches produces a partly-carried book. |
| `pendingRule` | optional `status` (`pending` / `in-effect` / `canceled` / `disapproved`), optional `atLeast` (default 1) | How many of `campaign.pendingRules` are in the named status. `disapproved` parses but no preview board reaches it (it needs a resolution of disapproval carried through both chambers against a veto), so content gating on it is refused by `auditReachability` rather than shipping dead. |
| `calendar` | optional `months` (one-based), optional `season` (`winter` / `spring` / `summer` / `fall`) | When in the year it is. Content could gate on the turn and nothing else, so a scenario about a heating crisis or a hurricane season wrote the month into its prose, which reads wrong the first time the gate fires in July. Answers `false` until the resolver reads the campaign date against it; no shipped scenario uses it |
| `ticket` | optional `party`, `background`, `route`, `plank` | What the administration ran on, as against what it has done. The platform is on the board and no condition read it, so a scenario could not ask for the President who campaigned on a plank and is now being asked to break it. Answers `false` until the resolver reads the platform; no shipped scenario uses it |

Audit companions: `unrouted-domestic-act` (**error**) refuses a `cast: none` scenario with `locus.state` whose replies declare neither `gazetteer` nor `domestic`; `dead-grievance-gate` (**warning**) fires when a `stateGrievance` names a signature slug no reply in the batch writes. `unplaced-domestic-signature` (**warning**) fires when a home scenario bound to a state or city has a position reply that routes a national `domestic` signature without `gazetteer.stateLean` (a capital locus is exempt): the event happened in one place and the national route alone moves that place no more than any other.

Audit companions: `unnamed-group-toll` (**error**) requires `human.cohorts` whenever `borneBy` is `one-group`; `cohort-toll-implausible` (**error**) refuses a `thousands`/`mass` toll whose named cohorts' combined national share (sum within axis, product across axes) is under 0.05; `dead-cohort-gate` (**warning**) fires when a `cohortStrain` names a cause slug no reply writes (class or `domestic.signature`).

Audit companions to `institutionState` (all in `scenario-institutions.ts`): `institution-bounds-scale` (**error**) refuses a bound written on the wrong scale for its field — a 0..1 field asked for more than 1, or a count field asked for a fraction of one unit; `unknown-institution` (**error**) is the second line of defense for content built outside the type; `institution-vacancy-gate-unfounded` (**warning**) fires on a `vacantDays` gate with `atLeast > 0` at a body whose chair the founding fills (one with a `cabinetRole`) when the scenario waits on no `is: true` flag, because nothing empties such a chair but a removal; `institution-politicization-unreachable` (**warning**) fires on `politicization` `atLeast` above `INSULATED_POLITICIZATION_CEILING` (0.4) at a `semi-autonomous` or `independent` body; `institution-open-matters-unreachable` (**error**) refuses an `openMatters` `atLeast` above 1, because an inspector general opens an audit only at a body with none running, so the count is never two. A `flagAge`-style `inert-boost` **error** covers an `institutionStrain` boost with no `institutionState` condition beside it.

Audit companions to the four oversight kinds (all in `scenario-oversight.ts`, dispatched with the institution rules through `auditExecutiveStateGates`): `oversight-facets-contradict` (**error**) refuses `stage: "reported"` with `public: false` (reporting is the transition that sets the public flag) and `obstructed: true` on a `committee` or `special-counsel` origin (obstruction is what a removal notice does to the inspector general conducting an audit); `oversight-count-unreachable` (**error**) refuses a non-whole or sub-one `atLeast`, and more than one open `special-counsel` (the engine appoints no second while the first is open); `shutdown-gate-contradicts` (**error**) refuses `is: false` beside `weeksAtLeast`, and (**warning**) a `weeksAtLeast` at or above `IMPLAUSIBLE_LAPSE_WEEKS` — `ceil(1 / LAPSE_STABILITY_COST)`, derived from the engine constant, past which the lapse has cost more stability than a government holds; `pending-rule-count-unreachable` (**error**) is the count rule for `pendingRule`. The whole vocabulary is named once as `ExecutiveFindingKind`, which is the single member `scenario-audit.ts`'s `ScenarioFindingKind` union carries for the subsystem.

**`institutionStrain` `WeightBoost` source.** `max(1 − implementationQuality, politicization)` of the body the scenario's first `institutionState` condition names, 0 when it names none, so content about a hollowing agency gets likelier as it hollows. `underStrain` in `preview-board.ts` now hollows the branch (staffing, morale and budget down, politicization and backlog up, `vacantDays` set to the weeks the campaign has actually run) so `institutionState` content is reachable on the standard board spread; `openMatters` is not seeded there and never will be — an inquiry poked into one arbitrary agency is a board that pretends. It is reachable instead on the **supplement boards** in `preview-board-oversight.ts`, which `auditReachability` now adds automatically for any batch carrying an `oversightMatter`, `shutdown`, `appropriation`, `pendingRule` or `openMatters` gate (`supplementBoards`). Three boards, each evolved by running the engine's own phases rather than by writing state onto a campaign: **the obstructed-audit week** (departmental readings pressed to where `auditWarrant` clears its bar, then twenty weeks of `inspectorGeneralPhase`, `committeeInquiryPhase`, `oversightRipeningPhase` and `specialCounselPhase`, with `fileInspectorRemovalNotice` answering each audit — which yields every open stage, all three origins, and both sides of `public` and `obstructed`); **the week the money stopped** (the turn set past `appropriation.throughTurn`, then `congressPhase` and `shutdownPhase`); and **the rules in their window** (three orders signed through `signOrder`, run past `judicialReviewPhase` and `rulemakingPhase`, yielding `pending`, `in-effect` and `canceled`). Boards are built once and cached.

**`human.cohorts` on `ScenarioImpact`** (optional): `z.array(CohortId).min(1).max(3).optional()` names who a toll or act fell on. `cohortEffects` in `impact-model.ts` compiles one `CohortEffect` per named cohort (valence −1, weight from toll×agency×`COHORT_TOLL_SCALE` or `COHORT_NAMED_STRAIN` when there is no death toll); cause is `domestic.signature` when present, else the scenario class. Opinion routing via `BORNE_BY_ROUTING` is unchanged.

The last six read board state no metric stands in for, and each carries a preview board on which it is true — a condition kind with nowhere to hold ships dead content, which is the lesson `preview-6` was added for. `preview-5` (under strain) now also carries real insurgency and protest pressure, `preview-6` carries a coalition flag against both belligerents of the war it opens, `preview-8` is a government that has enacted `defense-rearmament` through the engine's own `enactPolicy`, and `preview-9` is founded on a landlocked compact member whose firmest friendship is a fellow signatory — the two facts (`landlocked`, `sharedMembership`) no patch to the default founding can produce, which is why `sealedCampaign` takes an optional nation to found on. `preview-10` and `preview-11` are the two poles of a record long enough for the world to have read it — the only boards on which a `revealedPreference` condition can be true at all. On each, a rival opens a pursuit against the player and the player answers it three times, and the player turns an instrument on a state it is close to three times; `preview-10` answers softly and helps its friend (concedes high, escalates low, honors friends high) and `preview-11` reaches for the heaviest rung on both (the exact inverse). Both poles exist because every scalar has two bounds: with only the soft board, a scenario asking for a government that climbs would be refused as unreachable. `packages/content/src/packs/pilot.ts` carries one live scenario per kind, held to the whole audit and to reachability. Two of the six are refused on a `cast.pick: "none"` scenario by the audit's `domestic-incoherence` rule for the same reason `relationship` is: `sharedMembership` and `coalition` with `against: "counterpart"` ask about a counterpart a domestic scenario does not have.

**`revealedPreference` is floored on evidence, not on the scalar.** The profile (`packages/engine/src/revealed-preference.ts`) reports the midpoint for a trait the record has not tested, because "they do both about equally" and "the record does not say" are the same number — so a bare bound would be arithmetically true of a campaign on turn one, and content asserting the player always folds would open before they had folded once. The evaluator checks `profile.evidence` first and refuses below `REVEALED_PREFERENCE_EVIDENCE_FLOOR` (3), reading `evidence.friendshipTests` for `honorsFriends` and `evidence.pressedCommitments` for the other two, so the traits never borrow each other's evidence. It is the one condition kind that gates on the player rather than on the board.

**Severity is authored, and the schema requires it.** `ScenarioDef.severity` is `"routine" | "serious" | "critical"` (`ScenarioSeverity`) — what the author claims the week is. `routine` is the connective tissue of governing (a budget squabble, an appointment, a provincial complaint), `serious` a real crisis with room to think, `critical` the rare week the building shakes. The pacing director reads it to space criticals out (see `ARCHITECTURE.md` §5) and the audit reads it for register: the `manufactured-clock` rule refuses a routine scenario whose opening or replies carry countdown phrasing (`COUNTDOWN_PHRASES`, `audit-lexicon.ts`) and warns on a serious one, and the batch-level `severity-monotone` warning fires on a batch of four or more that is over half critical or contains no routine week. `ScenarioSelector` carries `severity` too, so the director paces over the cheap half of the pool.

**A briefing may be wrong, and the marker that says so is authored.** `ScenarioDef.intelConfidence` is optional and one of `"single-sourced" | "disputed" | "confirmed"` (`ScenarioIntelConfidence`) — how well sourced the falsifiable claim the week rests on actually is. It is scenario-level rather than per-beat, because the aftermath layer's handle is the act the player took rather than the sentence they believed: a per-beat marker would need a per-beat reveal the consequence pool has no way to select. Only the two low values can ever resolve false; `confirmed` is a promise the board never overturns.

The verdict is **derived, never stored**. `intelProvedFalse(campaign, def)` (`packages/content/src/scenario-intel.ts`) is a pure function of the campaign seed and the scenario id — stable across turns and reloads, different per campaign, and costing no save bytes. It draws at `FALSE_INTEL_RATE` (1/6, fixed by doctrine; no per-scenario dial exists) and returns `false` outright for `confirmed` or an absent marker. `intelContext()` hands the result to the aftermath layer as `ConsequenceContext.intelFalse`, and returns `{}` for an unmarked scenario — absence is not a claim that the briefing was sound, so a card asking either way is refused.

Two audit rules make the fairness doctrine mechanical rather than advisory (both `error`): `intel-tell-missing` refuses a marked scenario whose `subject`, `opening` or `transmission` carries no sourcing hedge, and `intel-doubt-unanswerable` refuses a marked scenario with no instrument reply, because the aftermath pool is only consulted for an act and a marker no act can answer is dead content. The remaining rules live on the aftermath side (below). See `ARCHITECTURE.md` §5b.

**A marked briefing requires a counterpart, and the schema now says so.** `ScenarioDef`'s `superRefine` refuses `intelConfidence` of `single-sourced` or `disputed` together with `cast.pick: "none"`. The reveal is drawn only for a reply that commits an instrument, and `domestic-incoherence` forbids an instrument reply where there is no counterpart to reach for — so the combination is not a scenario a writer can fix, it is one the design cannot currently deliver. Left to the audit it produced a pair of findings that contradicted each other (`intel-doubt-unanswerable` for having no instrument reply, `domestic-incoherence` for adding one) and a writer could bounce between them indefinitely. This is containment, not the fix: letting the domestic half of the corpus carry a doubtful briefing means letting a reveal be drawn off a `position` commitment, which widens the engine's consequence contract (`ConsequenceDef.instrument` and `rungBands` are both required today).

**Option confidence and briefing confidence are the same axis — evidence — never the odds.** An option's confidence reads the scenario's `intelConfidence`, whether the instrument's projection is deterministic or rolled, and the seated chair's competence in the domain; it is provably independent of the sign of `successChance`, so a well-sourced briefing and a favorable roll never get folded into one reading.

A hedge is `hedgeIn(text)` (`audit-lexicon.ts`): the literal list `HEDGE_MARKERS` ("a single source", "uncorroborated", "not independently confirmed"), plus `HEDGE_PATTERNS`, which match the *relation* — something whose job is to know is the thing in doubt ("the second service disputes it", "the two reports do not agree"), or the thing in doubt is a piece of reporting ("disputed by the second service", "disputes the estimate"). The bare adjectives and verbs are deliberately **not** in the literal list: "disputed" and "disagree" are ordinary vocabulary here (the disputed side of a border, chiefs who disagree about what to do), and accepting them let a marked briefing carrying no tell at all pass the gate, which is a lie shipping unmarked. The relation forms cover contracted negations ("won't sign", "don't agree") as well as expanded ones, and count a lab or an examiner as a body whose job is to know: the cabinet is told elsewhere to speak in contractions and plain words, and a matcher that only knew the formal register rejected well-written tells and pushed writers into stiffer prose.

**Pool-scale diversity rules are warnings, and every threshold is provisional.** `auditScenarios` also runs four batch-shape checks over the array it is given, all `severity: "warning"` and all floored so a small batch stays quiet: `tone-tuple-monoculture` (over 40% of a batch of 8+ sharing one ordered reply-tone tuple), `opening-framing-monoculture` (over 60% of a batch of 8+ opening with the same speaker-*kind* sequence — `roleHint` is not part of the sequence), `condition-coverage-thin` (fewer than half of a batch of 8+ carrying any authored condition at all), and `flag-economy-imbalance` (a batch writing 10+ distinct `sets` flags while reading none of them, in the batch or in the packs already installed — the ratio counterpart to `island-pack`, which only fires on scenario count rather than flag volume). None of the four ceilings were fitted against a real pool: the bundled core pack is 13 scenarios and the live corpus was 53 at time of writing, both too small for a threshold to measure anything but the sample. Retune once the 2,000-scenario corpus target (`CONTENT_PLAN.md`) is reached.

**Flag conditions can wait.** The `{ kind: "flag", flag, is }` condition takes an optional `agedAtLeast: N` (integer ≥ 1): the flag must have been set at least N turns before the scenario goes live, so a consequence can come back cold as a callback rather than landing as next week's script. It works off a turn-stamped marker (`flagset:<flag>:<turn>`, `flagSetFlag`) that `rememberReply` stamps beside every authored `sets` flag — additive in the existing `campaign.player.flags` record, no save-shape change. Evaluation scans back only N−1 turns; a flag set before markers existed is old enough by construction. `ScenarioDef` refuses `agedAtLeast` on a condition with `is: false` at parse (issue path `conditions.N.agedAtLeast`), because an unset flag has no marker to age. To wait N weeks for something that did not happen, age the opener's own `scenario:<id>` fired marker, which `rememberReply` stamps with `flagset:` like any authored flag, and put the negated flag beside it.

**Draw weight is dynamic, and one source reads the player's file rather than the world.** `WeightBoost` is `{ source, factor }` and `ScenarioDef.boosts` is an optional array of them; `effectiveWeight` multiplies the authored `weight` by `1 + factor × value` per boost, so a boost makes a live scenario likelier rather than making a dead one possible. `source` is a closed set: `tension` and `distrust` read the edge with the cast counterpart (the audit's `inert-boost` error refuses either on a `cast.pick: "none"` scenario, which has no such edge), `instability` and `removalPressure` read the player's own standing, and `conditionSeverity` and `warIntensity` read the board's multi-turn state — the worst condition at home, and the hottest war the player is actually in.

`flagAge` is the seventh, and the only one that reads governance memory: its value climbs from 0 the turn the scenario's gating flag was stamped to 1 once the flag has stood `FLAG_AGE_SATURATION` (12) turns, and stops there so an ancient deed cannot own the pool. It reads the same `flagset:<flag>:<turn>` markers `agedAtLeast` does, scanning only the saturation window, and takes its gate from the scenario's first `{ kind: "flag", is: true }` condition — a flag that is unset contributes nothing (the scenario is dark anyway) and one stamped before markers existed is fully ripe by construction. It exists because a flag-gated follow-up otherwise competes at flat weight against the whole live pool forever, which at corpus scale is a callback the player may never be dealt. Two audit rules pair with it: `callback-never-ripens` (warning) on a flag-gated scenario carrying no `flagAge` boost, and `inert-boost` (error) on a `flagAge` boost with no flag to age. Gates on engine-stamped flags (`scenario:<id>`, `desk:closed:<turn>`, and the engine-state `press:hostile`) are exempt from the warning — nothing stamps an age marker for them, so the boost would be a constant.

**How much of what content remembers is ever read back is a reported statistic, not a rule.** `deedsRemembered` (`packages/content/src/scenario-deeds.ts`) counts, over a set of documents, the distinct authored flags their replies `set` against the distinct authored flags their conditions read, plus `answered` — the reads no scenario in the same document stamps. Engine-owned flags are excluded from both halves. `mergeDeeds` unions the per-document results so a validate run reports the pool rather than each file, and `studio scenarios validate` prints one `formatDeeds` line per run. Deliberately no threshold: at the time of writing the corpus reads 12 distinct flags against 301 set, and a ratio fitted against a pool that young would measure the sample. `flag-economy-imbalance` and `island-pack` remain the enforcing rules.

**Two channels, and the schema enforces the difference.** A scenario's `opening` is the player's own room: `ScenarioSpeaker` is `cabinet` (with a `roleHint`) / `player` / `narrator`, and there is no counterpart kind. A foreign government's words are `ScenarioDef.transmission` — an optional `ScenarioTransmission` of `{ relay, attribution, text }`, where `relay` is one of `cable` / `readout` / `broadcast` / `intercept` / `communique`, `attribution` names an office (4–48 chars) and never a person, and `text` runs 60–320 characters. The speaker vocabulary is built from `presentation.ts`'s `SCENARIO_SPEAKER_KINDS` and held to it by a compile-time assertion, so the schema and the presentation contract cannot drift apart. `ScenarioDef.superRefine` refuses a transmission on a `cast.pick: "none"` scenario: there is nobody to have sent it. `castBound` resolves it to a `ResolvedTransmission` carrying the bound nation and its name, deliberately outside the engine's `ConversationScript` — the script is the thread, and the point of a transmission is that it is not in the thread.

**A position reply may reach the world it is about, within bounds.** `ScenarioReply.commitment` of kind `position` carries `cost`, an optional alignment `pull` and a `remembered` line, and now two optional fields that let authored words touch the board instead of only the player's own metrics. `relationship` is signed movement on the edge with the cast counterpart — `trust`, `tension`, `militaryThreat` only, each `.min(-POSITION_RELATIONSHIP_LIMIT).max(POSITION_RELATIONSHIP_LIMIT)` (±0.15) and the shape `.strict()`, so a magnitude past the bound is unrepresentable rather than merely refused by the audit. `conflictMove` is `"offerTerms" | "acceptTerms" | "deescalate"`, a zod mirror of the engine's `CONFLICT_MOVES` the way `ScenarioInstrument` mirrors `InstrumentId`. Both the bound and the enum are **imported from `@admin/engine` rather than restated** (and re-exported from content so the generation brief, which does not depend on the engine, quotes the number the parser enforces). No stored shape changes: `conversations.ts` translates the fields into the existing `relationship`, `flag` and `conflict` effect kinds, so `apply()` remains the only writer and a `Conflict` settled at a table is byte-identical in shape to one fought to a close. The audit's `ungrounded-conflict-move` (error) refuses a conflict move on a scenario not gated on the player being at war; see `ARCHITECTURE.md` §5.

**A position reply also answers the player back.** The same commitment carries a `rejoinder`: `{ speaker, text }`, one beat spoken after the answer and before the week closes. `speaker` is `ScenarioRejoinderSpeaker` — `cabinet` (with a `roleHint`) or `narrator`, and deliberately not `player`, held to a subset of `ScenarioSpeaker` by a compile-time assertion the same way the speaker vocabulary is held to the presentation contract. `text` takes the `beat` prose floor, and the field is folded into the audit's `passages()` so it clears every language, neutrality and length rule an opening beat does. It carries no effects: what the answer costs is priced on the commitment, and a rejoinder that also moved the board would be a second, unaudited price on every position. `renderReply` binds it during casting — role hint resolved against the seated cabinet, placeholders rendered — so the engine receives a `ScriptedBeat` rather than an authored shape, and `commitReply` speaks it as the third beat of the thread. **Required**, on the same path `dilemmaShape` took: the field arrived optional because no corpus carried it, all 749 shipped positions were then backfilled, and it was made required the moment that was true. A position reply without one does not parse, so the flat week is now a document the validator refuses rather than a warning somebody reads. One rule still watches its quality — `rejoinder-restates`, per scenario. The coverage warning that drove the backfill (`rejoinder-coverage-thin`) was removed with the same change: once the parser demands the field, coverage is always total and the rule could never fire. See `ARCHITECTURE.md` §5 for why the aftermath had to be authored rather than drawn from the consequence pool.

**Instrument and join commitments carry the same `rejoinder` shape, optional while the corpus backfills.** The beat is the room at the moment the order is given, and it is written blind to the resolver's roll: it must be true whichever branch resolves, so it carries acknowledgment, first friction or dissent — never a result. `commitReply` speaks an instrument's rejoinder between the player's line and the resolver's aftermath, and a join's after the narrator states the entry. Same speaker union, same prose floor, same no-effects rule, same fold into `passages()`; `rejoinder-restates` compares it against the reply text only (there is no `remembered` line on these kinds), and `rejoinder-presumes-outcome` (warning) flags a commit-time line that claims to know how the attempt went. It follows the position field's path: required once the 78 shipped instrument/join replies are backfilled.

**A reply may also enter somebody else's war.** `ScenarioReply.commitment` has a third kind beside `position` and `instrument`: `{ kind: "join", side: "beside" | "against" }`. It carries no `cost` — entering is priced by the engine (`joinCost(campaign, conflict, side)`, derived from the same constants `joinConflict` spends plus the unilateral-force price where no authorization covers the war), and the side of the real conflict is resolved from the board at commit time rather than taken from the document, so a scenario cannot enlist the player onto a side its own cast is not on. `conversations.ts` returns `joinConflict`'s own packet, so a war entered from a conversation stores identically to one entered from Ops and passes the same War Powers check. No stored shape changes. The audit's `ungrounded-join` (error) requires the `belligerent` cast, the only pick that guarantees the counterpart is at war; see `ARCHITECTURE.md` §5.

**Consequences** are the second content document type (`packages/content/src/consequence.ts`): `ConsequenceDef` and `ConsequencePack`, the authored aftermath a drawn outcome is dressed with. A def carries `id`, `instrument`, `rungBands` (`low`/`mid`/`high`), `valence` (`success`/`partial`/`failure`), optional `riskAffinity` and `conditions`, `weight`, optional `once`, one to three `beats`, a closed-vocabulary `effects` list (`metric` / `relationship` / `grudge` / `feed`), optional `sets`, and an optional `remembered` line. `compileConsequences()` (`consequence-compile.ts`) maps a def to the engine's `ConsequenceCard` one field at a time; the mapping is total because the document schema was written against the card.

`ConsequenceConditions` gained `intelFalse?: boolean` on both sides of the compile (content zod and the engine's `ConsequenceConditions`), matched against `ConsequenceContext.intelFalse` in `matchesConditions`. `true` makes a card a **reveal** — it may only be told in a campaign where the marked claim really was false; `false` is the counterweight, told when thin sourcing held. Absent means the card does not care, which is every card that is not about the briefing. Because the match is against the *context* rather than a campaign flag, a reveal lands in the same beat as the act it exonerates, and it can never fire in a later, unrelated week. `BRIEFING_CONSEQUENCES` (`packages/content/src/packs/consequences/briefing.ts`) is the bundled pool of them; they sit outside the five instrument lists because they are conditioned and therefore drawn on top of the 45-cell floor rather than as part of it.

The audit's `reveal-without-the-check` (error) enforces the third fairness rule: a card with `conditions.intelFalse === true` whose beats never name the check that would have caught it (`MISSED_CHECK_MARKERS`) is refused. A reveal that only announces the player was fooled teaches nothing, and doubt that teaches nothing is noise.

`reveal-cell-uncovered` (error) is the fourth: every cell of the instrument × band × valence matrix must hold at least one reveal, because a reveal is drawn from a cell like any other card and a cell without one is an act whose false briefing can never be told. It covers the success cells too — the resolver does not know the premise was hollow and will rule the act a success on its own terms. `BRIEFING_CONSEQUENCES` meets the floor with fifteen cards spanning all three bands (one per instrument per valence) plus band-specific tellings, all carrying `BRIEFING_WEIGHT` so a card that is only eligible in a rare week is not then outvoted inside it. The `coverage-cell-thin` depth warning counts only cards with no `intelFalse` condition: a card told in a rare week does nothing about the repetition that target measures.

The two vocabularies now agree on the metric enum: both name `approval`, `treasury`, `stability`, `militaryReadiness` and `standing` (`packages/content/src/scenario.ts`). The old asymmetry — the engine accepting a `mandate` the document schema refused — went when `mandate` did. Both metric and relationship delta shapes are `.strict()`, and every enumerated field is a literal union, so a generated value outside the vocabulary fails to parse rather than compiling into a card nothing will ever draw.

**Resolution step sequences** are the third content document type (`packages/content/src/resolution-beat.ts`): `ResolutionBeat`, `ResolutionSequenceDef` and `ResolutionSequencePack`, the staged act a decision plays out through between the order and the packet. They are distinct from `ConsequenceDef.beats`, which stays exactly as it is: a consequence card is the one-to-three-line aftermath told once the outcome is known, and a sequence is the ordered two-to-five steps played while it lands. **No stored shape changes and no effect payload exists anywhere on the type** — the outcome is computed before the first step plays and lands through the `EffectPacket` the resolver already built, so a sequence is presentation data and nothing else.

A def carries `id`, `instrument`, `rungBands`, `valence` and optional `riskAffinity` — the same four-part key a consequence card is drawn on, reusing the same zod enums rather than restating them — plus `weight` and `steps`. A `ResolutionBeat` step is `{ kind, line, outcome, particulars, unitAffinity? }`. `kind` is one of `order-given`, `movement`, `contact`, `named-unit-attack`, `loss`, `rescue`, `hold`, `stand-down`, `report-back`. `outcome` is the step's own settled turn: `held` (nothing moved either way), `gain`, `cost` or `mixed`. `particulars` are the slots the line uses, from a closed list of four — `actor`, `target`, `unit`, `person` — each of which has a known filler at selection time (the two nations from the resolution request, a generated unit designation, a name-bank person). A slot with no filler behind it would be a line that can never be completed, so the list grows only when a generator ships with it. `unitAffinity` is the optional `{ branches?, echelon? }` a step declares when its verbs cannot survive an unconstrained `{{unit}}` draw — `{ branches: ["air"] }` on a line that says "is wheels up", `{ branches: ["army", "marines"], echelon: "formation" }` on a line that puts two battalions across. It is refused on a step that never names `{{unit}}`, refused when it constrains nothing, and refused when it asks for a scale none of its branches has. The draw is one per sequence, not one per step, so the step affinities are merged by `unitConstraintOf()` (branches intersect, `formation` wins the scale) and a sequence whose steps ask for two incompatible formations does not parse.

**Outcome honesty is structural, not editorial.** There is no `pending` or `unknown` step outcome to author, so a step cannot claim the result is still open when the packet has already settled it. `TERMINAL_OUTCOME` fixes the last step's outcome per valence — `success` ends on `gain`, `partial` on `mixed`, `failure` on `cost` — and a document that ends anywhere else does not parse. `KIND_OUTCOMES` holds each kind to the outcomes it can honestly have (a `loss` is always a `cost`, an `order-given` is always `held`), `REQUIRED_PARTICULARS` makes a `named-unit-attack` name both `{{unit}}` and `{{target}}` and a `loss` name `{{unit}}`, a `rescue` is refused unless an earlier step lost something **and names something that loss named** (shared `{{person}}` slot, or a shared content word — kind ordering alone let a sequence lose a vehicle and a radio and then recover a person), and a `stand-down` must be the last step. Declared slots and the tokens in the line must agree exactly in both directions, so neither an undeclared token nor an orphaned slot survives a rename. A line matching a real formation, ship prefix or named special-operations unit is refused outright: a designation arrives through the generated `{{unit}}` slot or it does not arrive. `riskAffinity` is refused on any valence but `failure`, exactly as on a consequence card. Every object in the family is `.strict()`, so an added or renamed field fails to parse rather than being silently dropped — the failure mode that shipped 157 unreadable scenarios once already.

**Filling a sequence is `renderResolutionSequence()`** (`packages/content/src/resolution-render.ts`), which takes a def, a `ResolutionCast` of `{ actor, target, person }` display names and the usual seed parts, draws one `UnitDesignation` for the whole sequence under `unitConstraintOf()`, and returns every line with its slots filled. It owns the two things prose cannot: a slot in the possessive renders American style (`the United States' own airwaves`, never `the United States's`), and a line opening on a slot is capitalized after the fill, because the roster hands over `the United States` with its article. `resolution-render.test.ts` renders all forty-five shipped and fixture sequences across two hundred and forty fills apiece and checks the sentences that come out — the read that no gate performed while the bank shipped with aviation verbs drawing an undersea squadron.

**The key space is enumerable, which is how reachability is checked.** `resolution-beat-reachability.ts` builds `SITUATION_KEYS` from the engine's own `INSTRUMENT_IDS`, its `rungBand()` mapping over rungs 1–5, and `RISK_IDS` per instrument: every success and partial carries no branch, and every failure carries one of that instrument's named branches, because the resolver partitions the whole failure mass across them. `coversKey()` answers whether one document answers one situation (a failure def with no `riskAffinity` is the floor for its cell and fits any branch of its valence). `unreachableSequences()` returns any document no instrument × rung × branch combination can ever produce — a covert branch named on a diplomatic sequence, for instance — and `uncoveredSituationKeys()` reads the same space the other way so an unfilled cell is visible. Selection is by `resolutionSequenceKey()`, the canonical `instrument:band:valence:branch` string (`any` where there is no branch), which is also the deterministic seed the engine's selection hashes.

The scenario and consequence document types clear the same audit family (the step-sequence family carries its rules in its own schema for now, and joins the audit family with its authored bank).

**Commit-time copy stays inside the week.** Two audit kinds read only the fields a player sees the moment they commit: `commit-time-flash-forward` (**error**) for a time anchored more than a week past the decision ("eleven weeks later", "the following year", "by spring", "weeks passed", "by Christmas", and an interval told as a report: a past verb in the same clause as "after" or "in" and a stated count, "the vote carried after five weeks") and `commit-time-elapsed` (**warning**) for a short anchored interval or a soft duration ("two days later", "took eleven weeks"). On a scenario they read `rejoinder`, `remembered`, `press` and `context.inaction`; on a consequence card, every beat, feed headline and remembered line. A modal or conditional sentence is a forecast and passes; a sentence naming an earlier case is a precedent and passes. The rules are in `scenario-audit-time.ts` and the lexicon in `audit-lexicon-time.ts`.
